## WHAT THIS EDITION IS, AND WHAT IT REFUSES TO DO

This is a Mains-focused edition, not a second comprehensive package. Topic 13 is the only topic in
this lane whose syllabus footprint is **a medium and not a place**. It owns the atmosphere as a
system, which is why its size cannot be derived from a regional inventory and why its lesson count
is derived from obligations instead.

Five things this edition does that a conventional treatment of "weather" does not.

1. **It adjudicates its sources rather than assembling them.** The evidence base for this topic is
   the richest in the lane — Majid Husain's Chapter Three runs to fifty-four printed folios, the
   largest single book span any topic here has owned — and it is also the most error-laden. Six
   mechanism errors, five obsolete values, fourteen definitional conflicts and eight unit or
   apparatus defects sit inside it. **Forty corrections are applied in this edition, each one in the
   lesson where the material is taught, each quoting the source's own words and naming its page.**
   The value of this edition is not that it has gathered the material; it is that it has judged it.
2. **It corrects its own owners, in both directions, and says which way it is going.** Where the
   owner contradicts a named book and the **book** is right, the edition corrects the owner — the
   Foehn mechanism at N-15 and the geostrophic/Rossby conflation at N-01. Where the owner
   contradicts a named book and the **owner** is right, the edition says so and names the page —
   the tornado's classification at N-06. Silence in either direction would leave a student ambushed
   the first time they open the book.
3. **It rebuilds the India half from scratch.** C2 is **1,563 words with no numbers**. Everything
   quantified about India's jets, winter season, isotherms, western disturbances, temperature
   records, heat and cold criteria and forecasting institutions comes from Khullar, from Majid's
   *Geography of India*, or from a dated first-party official document. **C2 is cited for no figure
   anywhere in this file.** It supplies the frame; it never supplies a quantity.
4. **It refuses every trend.** Weather is the most volatile subject in the syllabus and the most
   tempting to over-claim. This edition may use a dated bulletin to show **what a system is** and
   **how the state measures it**. It may never use a bulletin, a season or a sequence of events to
   show that something is **becoming more frequent or more intense**. No first-party source stating
   such a trend was retrieved (U-02), and therefore **no such sentence appears in this file**.
5. **It never answers an objective question.** Fourteen of the twenty-one owned demands are Prelims
   demands. They appear here as a **demand statement and a capability statement**. There is no
   option letter, no key and no elimination chain anywhere in this file, including for the nine
   demands whose official keys are recorded as locally available. **Key availability is not key
   disclosure.** The seven owned Mains demands are subjective and therefore do receive full models.

**The structural fact that shapes everything below.** The map-location bank gives this topic almost
nothing, and what it gives is containers. Of **169** weather assets this topic needs, **148 are
absent**, 21 match lexically and five of those 21 are false positives — `ridge` inside "Adam's
Bridge", `loo` inside "Floods", `bora` inside "Tambora", `norther` inside "northern", `okta` inside
"Loktak". **Sixteen genuine rows survive and every one of them is a container, a range or a sea.**
Not one weather process, system, index, instrument, isoline, institution, local wind or
severe-weather term is in the bank at all. This is the weakest inheritance any topic in this lane
has received. **The bank is not owned by this lane and has not been edited.** The gap is closed
internally through the seven map assets M1 to M7.

## READING CONVENTIONS — FROZEN

| Mark | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | Taken from a named local book, with its printed folio or scan page |
| 📄 | Taken from a named official document, with its document date |
| ⚠️ | Standard physical geography carried by the owner's own ⚠️ tier; no book or live citation exists |
| ✗ | A source defect, quoted so that it can be recognised, never asserted |
| ⇄ | A live conflict between two sources, both of which are shown |
| ↪ | A route-out to the owning file, by exact path |
| 🇮🇳 | The lesson's mandatory India hook |

**Citation conventions, frozen.**

- **Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*** — the only one of the four books with a usable text
  layer. Cited `Majid IWG printed 3.24 (scan PDF p.97)`. Pagination is **chapter-relative**: "3.35"
  is a chapter-three folio, not page 335. **Sixteen pages in the Chapter Three span carry no folio in
  the text layer** — PDF 83, 86, 87, 88, 90, 92, 96, 104, 107, 108, 109, 111, 119, 124, 125, 126 —
  and are cited by **PDF page only** (U-04). **PDF 85 extracts its folio as "8.12"**, an OCR
  corruption of 3.12; it is cited as *folio corrupted in extraction* (U-05).
- **G.C. Leong, *Certificate Physical and Human Geography*** — image-only; read by page render.
  **Only seven folios were read directly** at the page foot: PDF 100 → 95, 110 → 103, 112 → 105,
  114 → 107, 120 → 113, 122 → 115, 124 → 117. **Every other Leong page in this topic is cited by PDF
  page alone, and no Leong folio is computed anywhere** (U-03). PDF 119's folio is physically clipped
  at the left margin and reads only "12"; it is **not** asserted as 112.
- **K. Khullar, *India: A Comprehensive Geography*** — image-only, two rotated printed folios per
  scan page. **Four folios read directly: PDF 79 → printed 124 and 125; PDF 83 → printed 132 and
  133.** PDF 80 and PDF 84 were read for content but their folio corners were not independently
  captured; they are cited as `printed folio 134 (folio not read; scan PDF p.84)` (U-06). **No
  Khullar folio is computed anywhere.**
- **Majid Husain, *Geography of India*** — image-only and an **abridged extract**. Ten scan pages
  stand for forty-six printed folios and **the mapping is non-linear** (U-07). Folios read at the
  page corner: PDF 68 → 4.3, 69 → 4.8, 71 → 4.18, 74 → 4.33, 77 → 4.46. **No folio in this book is
  computed.** Its entire contribution to Topic 13 is **one page, printed folio 4.8**.
- **Units are SI-first with the source unit in parentheses** (N-36). Leong's two chapters are wholly
  imperial — degrees Fahrenheit, miles per hour, feet, inches; Majid and Khullar are metric. The IMD
  criteria sheets **lose their degree signs in text extraction**, rendering "10 °C" as "100C"; every
  threshold in this file was re-read from the retrieved PDF and not from any transcription.

**A note on the Leong scan, which matters for every Leong citation in this file.** The scan
**duplicates a two-page spread inside the very chapter this topic owns**. **PDF 107 is a second scan
pass of PDF 105** — both carry the complete Beaufort Wind Scale, forces 0 to 12, and both open
"Winds … The wind is measured by an anemometer" — and **PDF 108 duplicates PDF 106**. Those two extra
pages are why the printed-to-PDF offset **drifts from 5 at the chapter opening to 7 from PDF 110
onward**. The duplication is stated as a **read finding from page renders, not as a hash finding**:
the two page images hash differently because they are separate scan passes (U-12). It was **not**
proved that the scan omits any page, only that it duplicates two (U-13).

**What the movement changes, and what it does not.** It changes **nothing** in the frozen
architecture, because **every file that architecture was derived from re-hashed unchanged at both
readings** — the rules, both owners, the validator, the map bank and the coverage map. It changes the

range is now **12-20 lessons across twelve topics**, and the maximum of 20 — previously held alone by
Topic 05 — is now a **tie**. **This does not reopen Topic 13's count.** The audit derived 20 from

not reopen Topic 13's count", and froze it independently. It also **clears the ordering constraint**:
the audit required that Topic 13's generator either wait for Topic 12's index row or append rather
than rewrite. **Topic 12's row now exists, written by its own agent.**

**Ordering constraint, restated.** This edition is **generated, validated and left unreleased**. **No
index row has been added, nothing has been staged, committed or pushed**, by instruction. No owner,
ledger, map bank, rule file or tool has been touched, and the single working-tree change attributable
to this run is the creation of its own edition folder.

## THE TWENTY LESSONS — FROZEN SEQUENCE AND MANDATORY INDIA HOOK

There is **one** sequence. The world half is not a block that ends before India begins. India enters
as the worked case of each process from Lesson 1 onward, and **every lesson without exception
carries an India hook**.

| # | Lesson | Stage | Core question | India hook |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What weather is, and how it is measured | Foundation | Why does a definition of climate need a number of years in it? | The Indian observatory: the Stevenson screen, the gauge and the instruments actually read |
| 2 | The energy that drives everything | Foundation | If the Sun heats the Earth, why is the air heated from **below**? | Why the Indo-Gangetic winter aerosol load is a radiation question before it is a pollution question |
| 3 | Where the heat goes: isotherms and the land-sea contrast | Foundation | Why does a line of equal temperature **bend** when it crosses a coast? | Khullar's January isotherm map, Dras at −45 °C, and the month-by-month march of India's heat maximum |
| 4 | The vertical atmosphere | Foundation | Why does all weather happen in the bottom one per cent of the atmosphere? | Why the tropopause over India stands higher than over Europe, and what that does to Indian convection |
| 5 | When the atmosphere turns upside down: inversion | Foundation | Why does the coldest air sit at the **bottom** of a Himalayan valley? | The Indo-Gangetic winter inversion, Delhi's fog, and sugarcane red-rot in western Uttar Pradesh |
| 6 | Pressure: the force nobody feels | Foundation | Why does a potato take longer to boil in Leh than in Chennai? | Majid's Leh and Kargil case, worked downward from 1013.25 mb |
| 7 | The four forces that move air | Core | Why does wind blow **along** the isobars instead of across them? | Why the Coriolis force is weakest where India's cyclones are born |
| 8 | The belts: why the planet's winds are where they are | Core | Why is the Thar not the same kind of desert as the Sahara? | Why India sits under a belt that is supposed to make a desert |
| 9 | Winds that live for a day | Core | Why is a "valley wind" the wind that blows **up** the slope? | The Loo, and why one sentence in one book is all the grounding it has |
| 10 | Winds that come down a mountain: the Foehn family | Core | Why is descending air warm when rising air is cold? | Why India has no true Chinook belt, and what the Himalayan rain shadow does instead |
| 11 | Moisture: humidity, dew point and saturation | Core | Why does dew **not** form on a cloudy night? | Why Indian relative humidity peaks at dawn and collapses by afternoon in the same 24 hours |
| 12 | Clouds: ten genera and two radiative effects | Core | Why does a high cloud warm the Earth and a low cloud cool it? | Why the pre-monsoon cumulonimbus over the eastern plains is India's most dangerous cloud |
| 13 | Rain: three mechanisms and a mountain | Core | Why does one side of a range get ten times the rain of the other? | The Western Ghats, Mawsynram and the Shillong plateau against the Deccan rain shadow |
| 14 | Air masses and fronts: the temperate cyclone machine | Core | Why does a front produce rain when nothing is rising on its own? | Why India's winter rain comes from a **frontal** system and its summer rain does not |
| 15 | Tropical cyclones: the ocean heat engine | Advanced | Why does a storm fed by water die over land in a day? | The Bay of Bengal's lethality as a product of exposure, not of frequency |
| 16 | When two storms meet: the Fujiwhara effect | Advanced | Why do two cyclones not simply collide? | Why the north Indian Ocean's narrow basin makes binary interaction rare but severe |
| 17 | Convection at its most violent | Advanced | Why does a tornado belt exist in America and not in India? | Himalayan cloudbursts, the Kalbaisakhi, and India's ingredients assembling for hours not months |
| 18 | The jets: the rivers of the upper air | Advanced | Why does a textbook definition of a jet stream exclude one of India's two jets? | Why India is the only major country whose weather is governed by **two** jets in opposite directions |
| 19 | India's upper air: the Tibetan barrier, two branches, two jets | Advanced | Why does a plateau 5 km high decide when India's rain starts? | The whole lesson |
| 20 | Western disturbances, the winter calendar and the measuring state | Advanced | Why is a western disturbance a **trough**, not a low? | The whole lesson |

**Stage allocation.** Lessons 1-6 Foundation · Lessons 7-14 Core · Lessons 15-20 Advanced.

**Twenty lessons, twenty distinct primary visual grammars.** Of the twenty primaries, **two** are
matrices or tables; the remaining **eighteen** are maps, sections, curves, profiles, ladders,
wheels, trees, blocks, rails, cutaways, anatomies, rosettes, clocks, staircases, towers, triptychs,
panels, orbits, ribbons and spines. A grammar may recur as a *secondary* visual and never as a
second primary.

**Twenty rubrics, pairwise distinct on the ordered tuple.** Five lessons carry a 10-mark original
question (1, 6, 9, 12, 16), eight carry 15 marks (2, 4, 8, 10, 11, 14, 18, 20) and seven carry 20
marks (3, 5, 7, 13, 15, 17, 19). Every split sums to its stated mark and **no ordered tuple recurs**.

**Why twenty and not seventeen, which is the lane median.** Five independent counts. **(a)**
Twenty-one owned demands, seven of them Mains at three mark weights with **five structurally
different directives** — *briefly mention and explain*, *how*, *explain* three times over three
different kinds of object, *what is … explain its impact*, and *compare*. No single answer spine
serves them. **(b)** Five distinct physical systems, each with its own mechanism, vocabulary and
visual grammar: radiation-temperature, pressure-wind, moisture-cloud-precipitation, the
severe-weather family, and the upper-air circulation. **(c)** A measurement-and-criteria register
with no analogue in the lane — the Beaufort scale in two nomenclatures, the IMD heat, cold and
warm-night criteria with their declaration rule, the cloudburst criterion, the averaging period,
fifteen isolines and twenty-four instruments. **(d)** Forty corrections, six of them mechanism or
physical-sign errors, each needing room to be stated, sourced and refuted rather than asserted away.
**(e)** The weakest map-bank inheritance yet measured, so every map must be built inside the file.

**Why twenty and not twenty-eight.** The ceiling is held down by **thirty-eight route-outs across
twelve owner areas**: the entire monsoon mechanism to Topic 16, climate classification to Topic 14,
ENSO and the IOD to Topic 12, cyclone preparedness and colour-coded warnings to Disaster Management,
cloudburst *response* to Disaster Management, wet-bulb health thresholds to Disaster Management, the
cryosphere to Topics 06 and 25, air pollution and climate-change science to Environment, crop-damage
economics to Agriculture and Economy, aviation meteorology to Transport, and tsunami to Topic 03.

```text
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 1 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **observation-station cutaway with a twin-nomenclature strip**

### The intuition, before any term

Everyone can tell you the difference between weather and climate. Weather is today; climate is
generally. That answer is worth nothing in an examination, and the reason is a number.

Climate is not "generally". Climate is **the statistics of weather over a stated period**, and the
period is a **decision taken by an institution**, not a fact about the sky. Change the period and
you change the climate of a place without a single molecule of air having moved. That is why the two
books this topic is built on do not agree about what a climate is: **Leong says thirty-five years
and Majid says thirty**, and C1, which names both of them as its sources, gives no number at all.

Start here and a second habit follows immediately. Every quantity in the next nineteen lessons —
1013.25 millibars, 6.5 degrees per kilometre, 40 degrees for a heat wave, 240 kilometres per hour in
the southern jet branch — came out of an **instrument**, under a **convention**, at a **date**. The
instrument is where the number is manufactured. A candidate who has met the Stevenson screen before
meeting the lapse rate will never again write a temperature as though it fell out of the air.

**Caption.** Nine instruments, one site. The screen and the gauge carry the two siting rules that
make a reading comparable with another reading a thousand kilometres away — and comparability, not
accuracy, is what a climate statistic needs.

### The twin-nomenclature strip — the Beaufort scale, printed twice

This is the part of the visual that does the examination work. ✅ **Leong prints the complete
Beaufort Wind Scale, forces 0 to 12, entirely in miles per hour**, at scan PDF p.105 — and again at
**PDF p.107, which is the duplicate scan pass of the same page** (see the reading conventions). 📄
**NOAA/NWS gives the same force numbers over the same speed bands with different names.** The force
numbers did not move. **The labels moved.** That is N-08, and it is exactly the kind of displacement
a statement question is built on.

```text

   Lower line 📄 NOAA/NWS weather.gov/mfl/beaufort, retrieved at generation time
   SI conversion supplied by this edition (N-36)
```

**✗ N-08, applied.** Leong's names are the **superseded nomenclature**. The force numbers and the
speed bands are identical; only the labels moved, and they moved by one position from force 7
upward. **Teach the modern names; show Leong's as the book's.**

**✗ N-09, applied.** ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.105 and again at p.119, gives the hurricane threshold as
"**more than 75 m.p.h.**". 📄 The modern threshold is **64 knots**, which the NWS table renders as
**72-83 mph at force 12** (≈ 119 km/h). State Leong's figure as the **book's** and the knot
threshold as **the standard**. The gap is small in magnitude and large in examination terms, because
one is a definition and the other is a rounding of it. This correction is used again in Lesson 15,
where the threshold is the thing that makes a storm a cyclone.

### ⇄ Conflict 1 — how many years are in a climate?

This is C1's opening claim, and its two named books disagree about it.

| Source | Position | Exact location |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Leong | "**For climatic averages, a minimum period of 35 years is desirable.**" | scan PDF p.98 (folio cropped) |
| ✅ Majid IWG | Climate "is a summary of mean weather conditions over a time period **usually based on 30 years of record**" | printed 3.51 (scan PDF p.124) |
| ⚠️ WMO standard | The standard climatological **normal** is a **30-year** period | standard practice; no first-party WMO page retrieved this pass (U-08) |
| ✗ C1 | Gives the weather/climate distinction with **no period at all** | C1 line 12 |

**✗ N-29, applied. Teach 30; show 35 as Leong's.** And say *why* the number exists at all: a mean
over a short run is dominated by the particular years in it. Thirty years is long enough that a run
of unusual years is diluted and short enough that the statistic still describes a climate a living
person has experienced. Neither book argues the point; both simply assert a number, and the
assertion is the finding.

⚠️ **What follows for every later lesson.** When Lesson 20 reports that an IMD heat wave is declared
on a **departure from normal of 4.5 °C**, the word *normal* is this convention doing work. A
departure is measured from a **base period**. Change the base and the departure changes without the
weather changing. **No departure figure anywhere in this file appears without its criterion.**

### The elements of weather — C1's chain, kept, with the instrument attached

C1 §1 gives the element set and the causation chain, and the chain is sound. What this edition adds
is the **third column**: nothing in the first two columns is knowable without the third.

| Element | What it is | How it is read |
|---|---|---|
| Insolation | Incoming solar radiation at a surface | Sunshine recorder for **duration** (✅ Leong PDF 105); solarimeter / pyranometer for **flux** (✅ Majid Table 3.3) |
| Temperature | The thermal state of the air at screen height | Dry-bulb, maximum and minimum thermometers in the **Stevenson screen** (✅ Leong PDF 102-103) |
| Pressure | The weight of the air column above a point | **Mercury** barometer, **aneroid**, **barograph** (✅ Leong PDF 101; Majid Table 3.3) |
| Wind | Horizontal air motion: speed **and** direction | **Anemometer** cups for speed, **wind vane** for direction (✅ Leong PDF 104) |
| Humidity | Water vapour content, absolute or relative | **Wet-and-dry-bulb thermometer**, **hygrometer**, **psychrometer** (✅ Majid Table 3.3) |
| Cloud | Fraction of sky covered, and genus | Estimated by eye in **oktas**, eighths (✅ Leong PDF 105); genus by the ten-genus key (Lesson 12) |
| Precipitation | Depth of water delivered to a level surface | **Rain gauge**, with the siting rule (✅ Leong PDF 100, printed 95) |
| Visibility | Greatest distance at which a marker is seen | Observer against known landmarks (✅ Leong PDF 110-111, printed 103) |

**The causal order inside the set is the spine of this entire edition.** Insolation is unequal →
temperature is unequal → **pressure** is unequal → air moves, and moving air is **wind** → wind
carries **moisture** → moisture lifted and cooled condenses to **cloud** → cloud yields
**precipitation**. Lessons 2 to 13 walk that chain one link at a time, in that order, and nothing in
it is reversible. The chain is the reason Lesson 6 cannot come before Lesson 3.

### Majid's two glossaries — the apparatus neither owner carries

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.51-3.53 (scan PDF p.124-126)** carries two tables that are, between them,
the most compact examinable apparatus in the whole evidence base. C1 reproduces **none** of it.

**Table 3.2 — fifteen isolines.** An isoline joins points of equal value; the prefix *iso-* plus the
quantity is the whole grammar, and the examiner tests the unusual ones.

| Isoline | Joins points of equal | Isoline | Joins points of equal |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Isobar** | barometric pressure | **Isohyet** | rainfall |
| **Isotherm** | temperature | **Isohel** | sunshine |
| **Isoneph** | cloudiness | **Isocryme** | winter temperature |
| **Isomer** | mean monthly rainfall | **Isonif** | snowfall |
| **Isoryme** | frost | **Isophene** | biological seasonal events |
| **Isallobar** | pressure **change** over time | **Isokeraun** | thunderstorm frequency |
| **Isamplitude** | temperature **range** | **Isoterp** | perceived comfort |
| **Isanomaly** | **departure from the mean** — see N-03 | | |

**✗ N-03, applied.** ✅ Majid's own Table 3.2 defines "**Isanomaly**" as "**Equal barometric
pressure**" — which is the definition of an **isobar**, given correctly in the very next row of the
same table. An isanomaly joins points of equal **departure from the mean**, normally of temperature.
The defect is shown here, named by its page, and **not reproduced**. It matters more than a typo:
the *anomaly* concept is the one the whole heat-wave and cold-wave criterion apparatus in Lesson 20
is built on.

**Table 3.3 — twenty-four weather instruments.** Altimeter · anemometer · atmometer · barograph ·
barometer · cathetometer · cryometer · evaporimeter · fathometer · hydrometer · hygrometer ·
hypsometer · lysimeter · manometer · nephoscope · photometer · psychrometer · pyrometer ·
radio micrometer · raingauge · salinometer · solarimeter · thermometer · thermostat ·
wet-and-dry-bulb thermometer · wind-vane.

**✗ N-35, applied, and it is a reading instruction rather than a content correction.** ✅ Majid's
Table 3.3 prints "**Antinometer**" where the instrument is the **actinometer**; and the **text layer
shifts the last three instrument rows**, so that "Radio micrometer / Raingauge / Salinometer" face
"Heat radiation / Precipitation / Salinity" **out of order**. The table must be read **from a page
render and never from the extracted text**. This edition read it from a render. The warning is
printed here because a student extracting the same PDF will hit the same shift.

⚠️ **The one instrument that is not in either book and must be named anyway.** A **Doppler Weather
Radar** measures the **radial velocity** of precipitation particles and therefore detects rotation
inside a storm — which is the only reason the mesocyclone of Lesson 17 is an operational object
rather than a theoretical one. It is named here as a capability. **U-10: no first-party count of
radars commissioned under Mission Mausam was retrieved, and no count is stated anywhere in this
file.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook — what an Indian observatory actually reads, and why the screen is the hook

The Stevenson screen is not a museum piece in India; it is the object that makes the Lesson 20
criteria enforceable. 📄 The IMD heat-wave criterion declares a heat wave when the **maximum
temperature** of a station reaches at least **40 °C in the plains** or **30 °C in the hills**, and
when "above criteria met **at least in 2 stations in a Meteorological sub-division for at least two
consecutive days**", declared on the **second day** (IMD, "Q. What is heat wave?", retrieved and
re-confirmed at generation time).

Read that criterion against the visual above and three things follow that a candidate almost never
says.

1. **"Maximum temperature" means the screen maximum, not the hottest reading available.** A
   thermometer on a Delhi roof in May will read far above 40 °C. It is not a station maximum,
   because it is not in a screen, at screen height, over grass, with a double roof and an air space.
   The criterion is a statement about a **standard exposure** as much as about the air.
2. **The two-station rule is a statement about network density.** A sub-division must *have* two
   stations for the criterion to be satisfiable. The criterion therefore embeds the observing
   network inside the definition of the hazard.
3. **The two-day rule converts an event into a persistence.** A single hot afternoon is not a heat
   wave by IMD's own criterion. This is the first appearance of the idea that Lesson 18 and Lesson
   19 will generalise: **the damaging property of a weather extreme is usually its duration, and
   duration is a property of the circulation, not of the thermometer.**

⚠️ **U-08, flagged and not resolved.** Whether the retrieved IMD criteria sheets are the current
**operative version** was not confirmed against a dated circular. Every criterion in this file
carries its retrieval and is described as the criterion **as retrieved**, not as the criterion as
amended to date.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "This is a lesson about boxes and thermometers. The examiner sets questions on the
monsoon and on cyclones, not on the Stevenson screen."

**Reply.** The examiner sets questions on **criteria** constantly, and a criterion is an instrument
statement in disguise. The owned 2026 Prelims demand in Lesson 20 asks for a **resolution objective**
— 12 km to 6 km — which is a measurement property. The heat-wave and cold-wave criteria are
threshold statements about screen readings. The Beaufort scale is a question about where a number
came from before anemometers were cheap: Beaufort's scale is **observational**, defined by what the
sea and the trees do, which is precisely why it survived into an instrumented age. The objection
mistakes the subject for its vocabulary.

**Unresolved residual.** Two, and both are named. **U-01**: the first-party IMD glossary page
returned **HTTP 404** at audit and again at generation, so the **cloudburst criterion has no
first-party anchor in this edition** and is never tagged ✅. **U-08**: the criteria sheets' version
currency is unconfirmed. Neither gap is filled by substitution.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing "climate is the average of weather" with no period.** That is C1's own gap. A climate
  statement without a base period is not a climate statement.
- ❌ **Quoting Leong's Beaufort names as current.** Five of them moved. "Storm" is force 11 in Leong
  and force 10 in the NWS (N-08).
- ❌ **Writing the hurricane threshold as 75 mph.** That is Leong's book figure; the standard is
  **64 knots**, rendered by the NWS as 72-83 mph at force 12 (N-09).
- ❌ **Defining an isanomaly as equal pressure.** That is Majid's Table 3.2 defect; an isanomaly is a
  line of equal **departure from the mean** (N-03).
- ❌ **Treating a rooftop or courtyard reading as a station reading.** The siting rules are part of
  the measurement.
- ❌ **Reciting instrument lists from the extracted text of Majid's Table 3.3.** The last three rows
  are shifted and "actinometer" is misprinted (N-35).
- ❌ **Saying an okta is a cloud type.** It is a **unit of cloud amount** — one eighth of the sky.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson serves the **salient features of world's physical geography** clause by
establishing the atmospheric element set, and it is the precondition for the
**geophysical-phenomena** clause's severe-weather limb, because every severe-weather criterion in
Lessons 15 to 20 is a threshold on a measured element.

**Owned demands served.** None directly — this lesson owns no demand of its own. It is the
**enabling lesson** for the measurement content inside **P14 (2026 Prelims Q84)**, which asks for the
Bharat Forecast System's **resolution objective**, and for every criterion used in Lesson 20. **No
key for P14 is disclosed, and the key held locally for 2026 is in any case recorded as provisional.**

**Answer use.** In any answer that uses a threshold, name the **agency** and the **criterion** before
the number. One sentence does it: *"IMD declares a heat wave when the station maximum reaches 40 °C
in the plains or 30 °C in the hills, at two stations in a sub-division on two consecutive days,
declared on the second."* That sentence is worth more than a paragraph of description because it
shows the examiner that the candidate knows a threshold is an institutional act.

↪ **Route-out.** **Climate classification, and the use of normals as an input to a climate class**,
belong to `Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen`. This lesson owns the **normal**; Topic
14 owns the **class built from it**. Köppen, Thornthwaite, Trewartha and Stamp are not taught
anywhere in this file.

### Revision points

1. **Climate is the statistics of weather over a stated period; the period is an institutional
   decision.** Without a period there is no climate statement.
2. ⇄ **Leong 35 years (scan PDF p.98) against Majid 30 years (printed 3.51); the WMO normal is 30;
   C1 gives none.** Teach 30, show 35 (N-29).
3. **Stevenson screen:** louvred, white, ~1.25 m above grass, **double roof with an air space**
   (✅ Leong PDF 102). Without the air space you measure the box.
4. **Rain-gauge siting:** clear of obstacles by **at least twice their height**; the siting rule is
   the examinable part, not the funnel (✅ Leong PDF 100, printed 95).
5. **Wind:** anemometer **cups** give speed, **vane** gives direction; winds are **named for the
   direction they come from** (✅ Majid printed 3.6).
6. **Beaufort:** forces **0-12**, devised by **Admiral Sir Francis Beaufort (1774-1857) in 1805**
   (📄 NWS). Leong prints the full scale in m.p.h. at PDF 105 and again at the duplicate PDF 107.
7. ✗ **N-08: five Beaufort names moved from force 7 up.** 7 Moderate Gale → **Near Gale**; 8 Fresh
   Gale → **Gale**; 9 Strong Gale → **Severe Gale**; 10 Whole Gale → **Storm**; 11 Storm → **Violent
   Storm**; and 2 Slight Breeze → **Light Breeze**. Force numbers and speeds unchanged.
8. ✗ **N-09: Leong's hurricane threshold "more than 75 m.p.h." against the standard 64 knots**
   (NWS force 12 at 72-83 mph ≈ 116-134 km/h).
9. **Isolines to know cold:** isobar (pressure) · isotherm (temperature) · isohyet (rainfall) ·
   **isohel** (sunshine) · **isoneph** (cloudiness) · **isallobar** (pressure *change*) ·
   **isanomaly** (departure from the mean).
10. ✗ **N-03: Majid's Table 3.2 defines isanomaly as "equal barometric pressure"** — the isobar's
    definition, correctly given in the next row. Not reproduced.
11. ✗ **N-35: Majid's Table 3.3 prints "Antinometer" for actinometer and the extraction shifts its
    last three rows.** Read that table from a page render only.
12. **Cloud amount is recorded in oktas — eighths of sky** (✅ Leong PDF 105). An okta is a unit, not
    a cloud type.
13. **The element chain, in order:** insolation → temperature → pressure → wind → moisture → cloud →
    precipitation. Lessons 2-13 follow it exactly and it is not reversible.
14. 📄 **IMD's heat-wave declaration is a measurement statement**: screen maximum, **2 stations**,
    **2 consecutive days**, declared on the **second day**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A statement reads: "The climate of a station is its average weather, and its maximum
recorded temperature last summer was 46 °C." Identify every measurement convention that the sentence
is relying on without declaring, and state what a Mains-standard sentence would add.

**Model answer:** The sentence relies on four undeclared conventions. **First, an averaging period.**
Climate is a statistic over a stated run of years — ✅ Leong requires "a minimum period of **35
years**" (scan PDF p.98) while ✅ Majid uses "**30 years** of record" (printed 3.51), and the WMO
normal is 30. Without the period the word *average* is empty, and no *departure from normal* can be
computed later. **Second, an exposure standard.** A station maximum is a **Stevenson screen**
reading — louvred, white, about 1.25 m over grass, with a **double roof and an air space** (✅ Leong
PDF 102) — and not the highest thermometer reading obtainable at the site. **Third, a siting
standard**, which is why the rain gauge beside it must stand clear of obstacles by twice their
height. **Fourth, an institutional threshold** if the figure is to be called extreme: IMD's heat-wave
criterion is 40 °C in the plains or 30 °C in the hills, satisfied at **two stations** for **two
consecutive days** and declared on the second. A Mains sentence therefore names the period, the
exposure and the agency before the number, and treats 46 °C as a reading produced by a standard
rather than as a fact about the air.

**Misconception to avoid:** That instruments are a preliminary to the subject rather than part of
it. Every threshold in Lessons 15 to 20 — heat wave, cold wave, warm night, cloudburst, hurricane
force — is a statement about a standard exposure and a stated interval. A candidate who treats
measurement as background will quote thresholds without the conditions that make them true, which is
precisely the discrimination a statement question is built to find.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 1

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "Every climatic statement is an institutional statement
before it is a physical one." Examine, with reference to the measurement of weather elements.

**Model:** A climatic statement reports statistics, and statistics need a declared interval. The
interval is institutional, and institutions disagree: Leong requires "a minimum period of **35
years**", Majid "**30 years** of record", the WMO normal **30** — so one station has more than one
climate. The readings are institutional too. A temperature is a **Stevenson screen** value, in a
louvred white box with a **double roof and an air space**; a rainfall is a gauge value sited clear of
obstacles by twice their height; a wind force is a **Beaufort** number whose names were revised
though its speeds were not, so "Storm" is force 11 in Leong and 10 in the NWS. Thresholds complete
it: IMD declares a heat wave on a screen maximum of 40 °C in the plains, at two stations, on two
consecutive days. The physics is measured; the measurement is governed. *(146 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Element set named and distinguished from climate **3** · averaging-period
conflict handled with both sources **3** · instrument and isoline apparatus used **2** · Beaufort
nomenclature discipline **2**

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Climate classification; normals as an input to a climate class; Köppen, Thornthwaite, Trewartha, Stamp | `Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen` |

## Lesson 2 — The energy that drives everything: insolation, albedo and the heat budget

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 2 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 §2 (lines 22-35) opens the heat budget and C1's ➕ albedo block
                   (lines 204-221) carries NO NUMBERS OF ANY KIND — not one albedo
                   value, not one flux, not one percentage. Structural gap 2 of 4.
                   This lesson carries FOUR owned Prelims demands, more than any
                   other lesson in the edition.
📕 Book evidence : Majid IWG printed 3.4-3.7 (scan PDF p.77-80) — insolation, albedo,
                   the six controls of temperature distribution · Leong scan PDF
                   p.112 (printed 105) "The Atmosphere" and "Insolation"; p.113 —
                   why temperatures fall poleward
```

Progress: 2 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **shortwave-to-longwave energy cascade ribbon**

### The intuition, before any term

Hold your hand out on a clear day. The Sun warms it. Now ask the question that this lesson exists to
answer: **if the Sun warms things, why does air get colder as you climb towards it?**

A mountaineer moving *towards* the Sun gets *colder*. An aircraft at 10 kilometres sits in air near
−50 °C with the Sun blazing on the fuselage. The only explanation that survives is that **the air is
not heated by the Sun directly in any meaningful amount. It is heated from below, by the ground,
after the ground has been heated by the Sun.** The atmosphere is largely transparent to what comes
in and largely opaque to what goes out, and that single asymmetry is the engine of every process in
the remaining eighteen lessons.

That asymmetry has a shape: **short wavelengths in, long wavelengths out**. Say it once and the rest
of this lesson is bookkeeping.

### The primary visual — one continuous cascade from beam to space

```text
   THE SHORTWAVE-TO-LONGWAVE CASCADE
   One continuous ribbon. Follow it left to right; nothing re-enters upstream.
   Proportions are SCHEMATIC. ⚠️ No numerical flux share is asserted anywhere in
   this edition: C1's albedo block carries no number and no local book or retrieved
   official source supplies a budget table. The ORDERING and the SIGN are the content.

   ☀ SOLAR BEAM ── SHORTWAVE ── peak in the visible, because the Sun is hot
   ║
   ╠══► [1] REFLECTED AT THE TOP, BY CLOUD ───────────────────────► BACK TO SPACE
   ║        Cloud is the single largest reflector. Lesson 12 returns
   ║        to this: a LOW thick cloud reflects and COOLS.
   ║
   ╠══► [2] SCATTERED BY AIR MOLECULES AND DUST ──┐
   ║        Short wavelengths scatter most ────────┤──► part reaches the ground
   ║        → the sky is blue; the low Sun is red  │    as DIFFUSE radiation
   ║        → this is the AEROSOL WEDGE, below ────┘──► part goes BACK TO SPACE
   ║
   ╠══► [3] ABSORBED IN THE ATMOSPHERE ── a MINORITY of the beam.
   ║        Ozone takes ultraviolet high up; water vapour takes some
   ║        near-infrared low down. THIS IS NOT WHAT HEATS THE AIR.
   ║
   ╚══► [4] ABSORBED AT THE SURFACE ── the MAJORITY. The ground, the
            sea, the canopy. THE SURFACE IS THE HEATER.
            │
            │   ◄── ALBEDO BRANCH: the mirrored arm ──►
            │   Of what ARRIVES at a surface, a fraction is reflected
            │   straight back without heating anything. That fraction
            │   is the ALBEDO. It is a PROPERTY OF THE SURFACE.
            │        FRESH SNOW ████████████████░░  highest
            │        DESERT SAND ████████░░░░░░░░
            │        DRY SOIL    █████░░░░░░░░░░░
            │        GRASS/CROP  ███░░░░░░░░░░░░░
            │        FOREST      ██░░░░░░░░░░░░░░
            │        WATER, Sun HIGH  █░░░░░░░░░░  lowest
            │        WATER, Sun LOW   ██████████░  ← and this inverts!
            │   ⚠️ Ordering only. No value is asserted (see the box below).
            ▼
         SURFACE WARMS, and a warm surface must radiate. It is COOL
         compared with the Sun, so by the physics of thermal emission
         it radiates at LONG wavelengths — the thermal infrared.
            │
            ▼
   ╔════════ TERRESTRIAL LONGWAVE EMISSION ════════════════════════════════╗
   ║   [5] ABSORBED by water vapour, carbon dioxide and other             ║
   ║       infrared-active gases, and by CLOUD. The atmosphere is         ║
   ║       OPAQUE here where it was TRANSPARENT above.                    ║
   ║            │                                                         ║
   ║            ├──► [6] RE-RADIATED DOWNWARD → warms the surface again   ║
   ║            │        and the lower air. THIS IS WHAT HEATS THE AIR.   ║
   ║            └──► [7] RE-RADIATED UPWARD → eventually escapes to space ║
   ║   [8] ATMOSPHERIC WINDOW: a band where longwave escapes DIRECTLY     ║
   ╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
            │
            ├──► [9] CONDUCTION into the lowest few millimetres of air
            ├──► [10] CONVECTION carries it upward — THE WEATHER MACHINE
            └──► [11] LATENT HEAT: evaporation takes heat from the surface
                      and releases it ALOFT where the vapour condenses.
                      Lesson 15's cyclone is this term, industrialised.

   THE ONE-LINE RESULT, AND THE REASON THIS LESSON IS SECOND:
   SHORT IN, LONG OUT; TRANSPARENT IN, OPAQUE OUT; HEATED FROM BELOW.
   Therefore temperature FALLS with height in the troposphere (Lesson 4),
   therefore pressure falls with height (Lesson 6), therefore air moves
   (Lesson 7). Every later lesson is downstream of this box.
```

**Caption.** One ribbon, eleven stages, and a single asymmetry at its centre. The atmosphere lets
shortwave in and holds longwave back; the ground is the heater; the air is warmed from beneath.

### Why the air is heated from below — the mechanism stated carefully

The claim needs three separate facts, and a candidate who supplies only one of them has not made the
argument.

1. **The emitting temperature fixes the wavelength.** A very hot body radiates mostly at short
   wavelengths; a cool body radiates mostly at long ones. The Sun is hot, so its beam is shortwave.
   The Earth's surface is cool, so its emission is **thermal infrared** — a different part of the
   spectrum entirely. This is why the exchange is **not symmetric**, and it is the step candidates
   usually omit.
2. **The atmosphere's opacity is wavelength-dependent.** Air is largely transparent to the incoming
   shortwave and substantially opaque to the outgoing longwave, because the gases that matter —
   water vapour first and most, then carbon dioxide and the rest — absorb strongly in the infrared
   and weakly in the visible.
3. **Therefore the absorbing layer is at the bottom.** The surface absorbs the beam; the surface
   emits infrared; the lower atmosphere absorbs that infrared and re-radiates part of it downward.
   The **air nearest the ground is in the thickest part of the infrared traffic**, and it is the
   warmest. Lift away from the ground and both the conduction supply and the infrared traffic thin
   out. **Hence the lapse rate of Lesson 4 — it is a consequence of this lesson and not an
   independent fact.**

⚠️ The argument above is **standard physical geography**, carried under C1's own ⚠️ tier. ✅ Majid
supplies its surroundings at printed 3.4-3.7 — the insolation section, the albedo concept and the
six controls — but **no local book in this evidence base states a numerical energy budget**, and
none is invented here.

### Albedo — a property of the surface, and the one that inverts

**Albedo is the fraction of incident shortwave radiation that a surface reflects.** It is not a
property of the Sun, not a property of the atmosphere, and not a property of the place. It is a
property of **what the beam lands on**, which is why land-use change is a radiation question.

The ordering in the visual is the examinable content. Three features of it do the work.

| Feature | Why it is tested |
|---|---|
| **Snow is the extreme** | A snow-covered surface reflects most of what reaches it and therefore barely heats. This is why Lesson 5's **snow-covered surface** is the fifth condition for a ground inversion, and why a Himalayan valley under fresh snow goes on cooling through the day |
| **Water is the other extreme — conditionally** | With the Sun high, water is among the **lowest**-albedo surfaces: the beam penetrates and is absorbed through a depth. With the Sun **low**, the same water becomes a **mirror**. The albedo of water is a function of **solar elevation**, which no other common surface shares |
| **The inversion at low Sun is the trap** | "Water has low albedo" is true at noon in the tropics and false at dawn at high latitude. A statement question that fixes the surface and varies the Sun angle is testing exactly this |

⚠️ **No albedo value is stated anywhere in this file.** C1's ➕ albedo block carries none, Majid's
printed 3.5 discusses albedo without a table, and no official source supplying Indian surface
albedos was retrieved. **The ordering is taught; the numbers are not invented.** A candidate who
writes "fresh snow reflects about 80 to 90 per cent" is quoting something, and this edition will not
be the thing they are quoting without a source.

### The aerosol and dust term — a radiation term before it is a pollution term

This is the second of the owned-demand clusters in this lesson, and the boundary runs straight
through it.

✅ Majid IWG, printed 3.4-3.5, treats dust as a **scattering agent** in the beam: particulate matter
in the atmosphere intercepts shortwave radiation, sends part of it back to space and part of it
forward to the ground as **diffuse** radiation. That is the mechanism. Three geographical
consequences follow and all three are examinable.

1. **Dust loading is not uniform across the planet.** It is highest where there are **dry surfaces
   with loose material and wind to lift it** — the arid and semi-arid subtropics — and lowest over
   the **open ocean far from land** and over **ice-covered high latitudes**, which have neither the
   loose material nor the lifting surface. The **equatorial humid tropics** sit in between and lower
   than the arid belt, because rainfall washes the air out continuously and the surface is
   vegetated.
2. **A dust-loaded atmosphere reduces the direct beam and raises the diffuse fraction.** The total
   reaching the ground falls; the share arriving from all over the sky rather than from the solar
   disc rises. Shadows soften; the Sun can be looked at.
3. **The near-surface consequence is a suppressed maximum and a modified minimum**, which is why
   Lesson 5's inversion and Lesson 11's fog interact with this term rather than merely coexisting
   with it.

↪ **Route-out, stated here because this is exactly where the file would leak.** ⚠️ Aerosols and
particulates **as an air-quality problem** — PM2.5, source apportionment, stubble burning, the
Graded Response Action Plan, winter smog policy — belong to the
`Environment-and-Ecology` air-pollution owner. **Topic 13 owns the scattering mechanism and the
inversion that traps the particles (Lesson 5); it does not own the particles.**

↪ **Second route-out, the biggest boundary in the file.** The **greenhouse-gas inventory, radiative
forcing, climate-change attribution, IPCC assessment content and any trend** belong to
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/17_Climate-Change-Science-Greenhouse-Effect` and
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18_IPCC-Assessment-Reports`. **This lesson teaches the radiation
mechanism — that infrared-active gases absorb terrestrial longwave and re-radiate part of it
downward. It does not teach the change.** The distinction is exact and C1 enforces it at lines
342-346 and 517-518; the enforcement is adopted here verbatim. A Geography answer that starts
costing carbon has left its own paper.

### The latitudinal energy geography — surplus, deficit and the transport that follows

The Earth is a sphere lit from one side, so insolation per unit area falls from the equator to the
poles for two compounding reasons, both of which must be stated.

```text
   WHY A HIGH-LATITUDE SURFACE RECEIVES LESS, FOR TWO REASONS AT ONCE

   EQUATOR  ──────►│████│  beam strikes near-normal
                   └────┘  SAME energy, SMALL footprint → high flux per m²
                           SHORT path through the atmosphere → little depletion

   MID-LAT    ────►│██████████│  beam strikes obliquely
                   └──────────┘  SAME energy, LARGER footprint → lower flux per m²
                                 LONGER slant path → more scattering and absorption

   POLE   ───────►│████████████████████│  beam strikes at a very low angle
                  └────────────────────┘  SAME energy spread very wide → low flux
                                          LONGEST path → greatest depletion
                                          AND a high-albedo (ice/snow) surface
                                          reflecting much of what survives.

   RESULT, THE CONSEQUENCE THAT THE WHOLE EDITION USES:
      LOW LATITUDES  ── more energy absorbed than emitted ── ENERGY SURPLUS
      HIGH LATITUDES ── more energy emitted than absorbed ── ENERGY DEFICIT
      The planet is not getting hotter at the equator and colder at the poles
      without limit. Therefore ENERGY MUST BE TRANSPORTED POLEWARD.
      THAT TRANSPORT IS THE ATMOSPHERE'S CIRCULATION AND THE OCEAN'S CURRENTS.
      → Lesson 8's three-cell arrangement exists to carry out this instruction.
      → Lesson 14's temperate cyclone is a transport device, not an accident.
      → Lesson 18's jets sit exactly where the gradient is steepest.
```

✅ Leong, scan PDF p.113, gives the poleward decline and its reasoning directly, inside the chapter
that opens "The Atmosphere" and "Insolation" at printed 105 (scan PDF p.112). ✅ Majid, printed
3.5-3.6, gives the **six controls of temperature distribution** that modify the pure latitudinal
picture: latitude, altitude, distance from the sea, prevailing winds and air masses, ocean currents,
and the nature of the surface including its vegetation and slope aspect. Lesson 3 takes that list
and draws it as a field.

⚠️ **The transport instruction is the single most useful sentence in this lesson for Mains.** It
converts a descriptive fact — it is hotter at the equator — into an **obligation** that the rest of
the atmosphere must discharge. Every circulation taught after this point exists to discharge it.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the Indo-Gangetic winter as a radiation problem

In December and January the Indo-Gangetic plain carries a heavy load of suspended particulates and a
persistent shallow fog. The usual account is a pollution account. The **radiation account comes
first**, and it is Geography's.

```text
   THE WINTER LOOP OVER THE INDO-GANGETIC PLAIN — RADIATION FIRST
   Each arrow is a Lesson 2 term. The air-quality consequence is ROUTED, not taught.

   [a] LOW WINTER SUN ANGLE over 25-30°N
        → the beam arrives obliquely and takes a LONG SLANT PATH
        → less energy per square metre AND more depletion on the way in
                 ↓
   [b] HEAVY AEROSOL LOADING in that long path
        → SCATTERING ([2] on the cascade ribbon) removes more of the
          direct beam and converts part of it to diffuse radiation
                 ↓
   [c] REDUCED SURFACE HEATING BY DAY
        → the surface warms less → the DAYTIME MAXIMUM is suppressed
        → "cold day" conditions, which are a MAXIMUM-temperature idea,
          quite distinct from the cold wave's MINIMUM-temperature idea
                 ↓
   [d] LONG CLEAR WINTER NIGHT → strong longwave loss from the surface
        → a GROUND INVERSION forms (Lesson 5, Majid printed 3.9)
                 ↓
   [e] THE INVERSION IS A LID: it stops vertical exchange, so
        [b]'s aerosol and [d]'s moisture are both CONFINED to a
        shallow layer → FOG, and lower visibility
                 ↓
   [f] FOG RAISES ALBEDO AND BLOCKS THE BEAM → back to [c].
        THE LOOP CLOSES AND REINFORCES ITSELF.

   ↪ WHERE GEOGRAPHY STOPS: at [e]. The composition of what is trapped,
     its health effect, and the policy response are the Environment
     air-pollution owner's. The LID is ours; the LOAD is not.
```

⚠️ **This is a mechanism, not a trend.** Nothing here states that Indian winter fog, aerosol load or
cold days are increasing or decreasing. **No first-party source for any such trend was retrieved
(U-02), and no trend is asserted anywhere in this file.**

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "The greenhouse mechanism is taught here but climate change is routed out. That is an
artificial line — they are the same physics."

**Reply.** They are the same physics and different questions, and the examiner's own routing proves
it. The **mechanism** — that infrared-active gases absorb terrestrial longwave — is a *salient
feature of the atmosphere* and belongs to the clause this topic owns. The **change** — forcing,
attribution, trend, inventory, mitigation — is a different syllabus body with its own owner file, and
the ledgers route it there. A Geography answer that explains the mechanism in two sentences and then
analyses the geography of surplus and deficit has used the physics correctly. One that recites
emission shares has answered an Environment question in a Geography paper and will be marked as
having done so.

**Unresolved residual.** **No numerical energy budget exists in this evidence base.** C1's albedo
block has no numbers, Majid's insolation pages are qualitative, and no official budget table was
retrieved. The honest position is **ordering and sign without magnitudes** — which is, in fairness,
what the owned demands here actually test.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **"The Sun heats the atmosphere directly."** A minority of the beam is absorbed aloft; the
  heating of the air is overwhelmingly **from below**, by terrestrial longwave, conduction,
  convection and latent heat release.
- ❌ **Treating albedo as a property of a place.** It is a property of the **surface**, and for water
  it varies with **solar elevation** — low at high Sun, high at low Sun.
- ❌ **Saying dust is highest over the humid tropics.** The loading maximum is over **arid and
  semi-arid** surfaces; rainfall scavenges the humid tropics continuously.
- ❌ **Writing that scattering only sends radiation back to space.** It also delivers the **diffuse**
  component to the ground. Both halves are needed.
- ❌ **Using the greenhouse mechanism to make a climate-change claim.** Mechanism is Geography's;
  change is Environment's, and no trend is stated in this file (U-02).
- ❌ **Saying a surplus at low latitudes makes the equator hotter year on year.** It drives
  **poleward transport**. The surplus is discharged, not accumulated.
- ❌ **Confusing a "cold day" with a "cold wave".** A cold day is a suppressed **maximum**; a cold
  wave is a threshold on the **minimum** (Lesson 20).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson sits squarely inside **salient features of world's physical geography** —
the radiation balance is the atmosphere's most salient feature — and supplies the energy argument
that the geophysical-phenomena clause's severe weather later consumes.

**Owned demands served — four, the densest cluster in the file.**

| Demand | What it tests | What this lesson supplies |
|---|---|---|
| **P4 — 2023 Prelims Q62** · insolation distribution in the atmosphere; infrared rays | Where the incoming beam is intercepted, and which part of the spectrum is which | Stages [1]-[4] of the cascade and the short-in/long-out asymmetry |
| **P6 — 2024 Prelims Q1** · atmosphere heated more by terrestrial than by solar radiation; greenhouse gases | The *heated from below* proposition and the role of infrared-active gases | The three-step mechanism argument and stages [5]-[7] |
| **P11 — 2025 Prelims Q26** · atmospheric dust distribution across climatic zones | Where dust loading is high and low, and why | The aerosol wedge and the arid-maximum / humid-and-polar-minimum geography |
| **P13 — 2025 Prelims Q29** · atmosphere maintaining Earth's temperature; carbon dioxide absorbing radiation | Which radiation CO₂ absorbs, and the maintenance idea | Stage [5], stated as **longwave absorption**, with the change routed out |

**Answer neutrality.** All four are **objective** demands. **P4's key is not available locally and
none is inferred. P6, P11 and P13 are recorded as having official Set-A keys held locally and those
keys are not disclosed here, because key availability is not key disclosure.** No option letter
appears and no elimination chain is written for any of them.

**Answer use.** Open any radiation answer with the asymmetry in one sentence — *short wavelengths in,
long wavelengths out; the atmosphere is largely transparent to the first and substantially opaque to
the second; the air is therefore heated from below* — and then spend the marks on **geography**:
surplus, deficit, and the transport obligation. The physics is the licence; the geography is the
answer.

### Revision points

1. **Short in, long out.** The Sun is hot and radiates shortwave; the Earth's surface is cool and
   radiates **thermal infrared**. The asymmetry is the engine.
2. **The atmosphere is largely transparent to shortwave and substantially opaque to longwave.**
   Hence the air is **heated from below**.
3. **Three steps make the argument:** emitting temperature fixes wavelength → opacity is
   wavelength-dependent → the absorbing layer is therefore at the bottom.
4. **Albedo is the reflected fraction and a property of the SURFACE.** Ordering: fresh snow highest,
   then desert sand, dry soil, grass, forest, water lowest — **but water's albedo rises sharply at
   low solar elevation**. That inversion is the trap.
5. **Scattering does two things**: returns part of the beam to space **and** delivers the **diffuse**
   component to the ground.
6. **Dust loading is highest over arid and semi-arid surfaces**, lowest over open ocean far from
   land and over ice-covered high latitudes, intermediate and low over the washed humid tropics.
7. **Latitude reduces insolation twice over:** a larger footprint for the same energy, **and** a
   longer slant path through the atmosphere. At high latitude a high-albedo surface adds a third.
8. ✅ **Majid's six controls of temperature distribution** (printed 3.5-3.6): latitude · altitude ·
   distance from the sea · prevailing winds and air masses · ocean currents · nature of the surface.
9. **Low latitudes run an energy surplus, high latitudes a deficit**, and the imbalance is an
   **instruction to transport energy poleward** — discharged by the circulation of Lesson 8, the
   cyclones of Lesson 14 and the jets of Lesson 18.
10. **Latent heat is a transport term**: evaporation removes heat from the surface and releases it
    **aloft** at condensation. Lesson 15's cyclone is this term at industrial scale.
11. **The atmospheric window** is the longwave band that escapes to space directly.
12. ⚠️ **No numerical budget or albedo value is asserted anywhere in this file.** C1's albedo block
    carries none and no source in the evidence base supplies one.
13. ↪ **Boundary:** the **mechanism** is Geography's; the **greenhouse-gas inventory, radiative
    forcing, attribution and any trend** are Environment's.
14. 🇮🇳 **The Indo-Gangetic winter loop:** low Sun angle → long slant path → aerosol scattering →
    suppressed daytime maximum → long-night longwave loss → ground inversion → confinement → fog →
    higher albedo → back to the start. **A mechanism, never a trend.**

### Concept check

**Question:** "Carbon dioxide and water vapour keep the Earth warm by trapping the Sun's rays."
Identify every error in that sentence and restate the mechanism as a Mains answer would.

**Model answer:** Three errors. **First, the wavelength is wrong.** The Sun's rays are **shortwave**
and the infrared-active gases absorb weakly there; what they absorb strongly is the **longwave
thermal infrared emitted by the Earth's own surface**. The sentence has the spectrum backwards.
**Second, "trapping" is the wrong verb.** Nothing is held; the gases **absorb and re-radiate**, and
the part re-radiated downward returns energy to the surface and the lower air while the part
re-radiated upward continues towards space, with an **atmospheric window** band escaping directly.
**Third, the heating path is omitted.** The surface absorbs the beam, the surface emits infrared, and
the air is warmed **from below** — by that infrared, by conduction into the lowest layer, by
convection and by latent heat released at condensation aloft. Restated: the atmosphere is largely
transparent to incoming shortwave and substantially opaque to outgoing longwave, so the ground is
the heater and the air is warmed beneath rather than above. That asymmetry is also why temperature
falls with height through the troposphere, which the next two lessons develop. **The mechanism is
Geography's; any claim about its change is routed to the Environment owner.**

**Misconception to avoid:** That describing the greenhouse mechanism licenses a climate-change
statement in a Geography answer. The mechanism is a salient feature of the atmosphere and belongs
here; forcing, attribution, inventory and trend belong to
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/17_Climate-Change-Science-Greenhouse-Effect`, and no trend of any kind
is asserted anywhere in this edition.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 2

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine how the wavelength asymmetry of incoming and
outgoing radiation governs both the vertical heating of the atmosphere and the latitudinal
obligation to transport energy poleward.

**Model:** Radiation enters and leaves the Earth system at different wavelengths because the two
emitters are at very different temperatures. The Sun is hot, so its beam is **shortwave**; the
Earth's surface is cool, so its emission is **longwave thermal infrared**. The atmosphere's opacity
is wavelength-dependent — largely transparent to the first, substantially opaque to the second — so
the majority of the beam is absorbed **at the surface** while the outgoing infrared is absorbed
within the air by water vapour, carbon dioxide and cloud and partly re-radiated downward. **The
atmosphere is therefore heated from below**, which is why temperature falls with height through the
troposphere and why conduction, convection and latent-heat release all run upward from the surface.

Vertically, the asymmetry also makes albedo decisive: of what arrives, the reflected fraction never
heats anything, and because water's albedo rises steeply at low solar elevation, the same ocean is a
sink at noon and a mirror at dawn.

Latitudinally, insolation falls poleward twice over — the same energy is spread over a larger
footprint, and it travels a longer slant path — with high-latitude snow and ice reflecting much of
what survives. ✅ Majid's six controls (printed 3.5-3.6) modify but do not reverse this. Low
latitudes therefore run a **surplus** and high latitudes a **deficit**, and since neither diverges
without limit, the imbalance is an **obligation to move energy poleward**. The three-cell
circulation, the temperate cyclone and the jet streams exist to discharge it. *(243 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Shortwave-longwave asymmetry derived **6** · albedo and the aerosol term **4**
· surplus-deficit geography **3** · attribution of every figure **2**

### Route-outs required of this lesson

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Greenhouse-gas inventory, radiative forcing, climate-change attribution and trends, IPCC assessment content | `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/17_Climate-Change-Science-Greenhouse-Effect` and `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18_IPCC-Assessment-Reports` |
| Aerosols and particulates as an air-quality problem; PM2.5, smog policy, GRAP, stubble burning | `Environment-and-Ecology` air-pollution owner |

## Lesson 3 — Where the heat goes: isotherms, the land-sea contrast and India's heat march

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 3 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : NEITHER OWNER CARRIES AN ISOTHERM MAP OR ANY JANUARY ISOTHERM
                   DISCUSSION. C1 §2 tabulates belts; C2 §1 lists controls of Indian
                   weather qualitatively with no latitude, no altitude, no figure.
                   Structural gap 3 of 4, and it sits under THREE owned Prelims
                   demands. The whole lesson is rebuilt from Khullar and Majid.
```

Progress: 3 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **paired isotherm plan-map with a bending-index margin**

### The intuition, before any term

Draw a line through every place that is at 20 °C in January. It will not be a straight line, and it
will not follow a parallel of latitude. It will **wander**, and — this is the whole lesson — it will
**bend sharply the moment it crosses a coast**.

Why would a line of equal temperature care about a coastline? Because land and water take and give
up heat in utterly different ways, and an isotherm is a line that has to reconcile the two. Where it
leaves land for sea in winter, it has to move to a **different latitude** to stay at the same
temperature. **The bend is the land-sea contrast made visible**, and the direction of the bend
reverses between January and July.

That is why an examiner can set an entire question on a January isotherm map. The map is not a
picture of temperature. It is a **record of the thermal behaviour of two different materials**,
drawn in a single stroke.

### The primary visual — the January field, with its bending margin

```text

      (scan PDF p.83, folio read directly)│  and read in BOTH seasons
```
