## Lesson 7 — The four forces that move air, and the correction at the centre of this topic

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 7 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : This lesson carries the TOPIC'S SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT CONCEPTUAL
                   CORRECTION. ✗ Majid IWG heads a section "Geostrophic Winds (Rossby
                   Waves)" and defines ONLY geostrophic balance beneath it. ✗ C2 line
                   104 REPEATS THE EQUATION BY CITATION. ✓ C2 line 106 then gives the
                   CORRECT Rossby definition two lines later, so the owner carries
                   both the error and its own correction without noticing (N-01).
                   The fix is placed HERE, at the forces, and not at Lesson 18, because
                   it is a FORCE-BALANCE error and not an upper-air error.
📕 Book evidence : Majid IWG printed 3.12 (scan PDF p.85; FOLIO CORRUPTED IN
                   EXTRACTION as "8.12", U-05) — the four forces, the friction depth,
                   the printed spelling "Cariolis", and the conflated heading ·
                   Leong scan PDF p.116 — FERREL'S LAW; p.117 — the LAW OF DEFLECTION
🔍 Live check    : none required. Force balance is standard dynamics; the correction
                   is made against the books themselves.
📰 CA anchor     : none.
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity (Coriolis
                   deflection of OCEAN currents and the Ekman spiral)
⚠️ Live gaps     : U-05 — the folio of the page carrying the four forces extracts as
                   "8.12", an OCR corruption of 3.12. The folio is NOT asserted; the
                   page is cited with the corruption disclosed.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 7 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **four-force vector rosette beside a balance-versus-wave fork**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 6 ended with a pressure difference and called it a force. If that were the whole story, wind
would blow straight from high pressure to low pressure, perpendicular to the isobars, and every
weather map would be boring.

Look at a real chart and the wind does nothing of the sort. **It blows almost along the isobars**,
with the low pressure on its left in the Northern Hemisphere — nearly at right angles to the force
that started it. Something is bending it, continuously, by almost ninety degrees.

That something is the rotation of the Earth, and the way to feel it is to stop thinking about the
air and think about the **ground underneath it**. Fire a projectile due north from Chennai. While it
flies, the ground beneath it is rotating eastward — and at a higher latitude the ground is moving
east **more slowly**, because it is nearer the axis. The projectile keeps its original, faster
eastward motion and therefore arrives **east of** its target. From a fixed point in space, nothing
pushed it. From the rotating Earth, it **curved to the right**.

**That apparent deflection is the Coriolis force, and it is why air travels along isobars rather
than across them.** Everything else in this lesson is bookkeeping around that one idea — except the
correction, which is the most valuable thing in the topic.

### The primary visual — the rosette and the fork

```text
   PART A — THE FOUR-FORCE ROSETTE
```

**Caption.** Four forces at two heights, and then the fork. The fork is the correction: the two
objects on it share a heading in a named book and share nothing else.

### ✅ The four forces, as Majid states them — the owned 2024 Prelims anchor

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.12 (scan PDF p.85; folio corrupted in extraction as "8.12", U-05):**

1. ✅ "**Gravitational Force of the Earth** … practically uniform" over the atmosphere's depth. It
   acts **vertically**, and is balanced by the **vertical** pressure gradient. It therefore does not
   appear in the horizontal wind problem, and candidates who list it as a horizontal force have
   misread the page.
2. ✅ "**The Pressure Gradient Force**: This force drives air from areas of higher pressure to areas
   of lower pressure, causing wind." **It is the only force that initiates motion.** The other three
   act on air that is already moving, or balance something else.
3. ✅ "**The Coriolis Force**: It is a **deflective force** … to the **right in the Northern
   Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern Hemisphere**." ⚠️ Note the printed spelling on this
   page is "**Cariolis**"; the defect is recorded and the standard spelling is used.
4. ✅ "**The Friction Force**: … **The effect of surface friction extends to a height of about 500
   m** and varies with surface texture, wind speed, time of day and year, and atmospheric
   conditions."

**The complete owned answer needs Leong's two laws alongside Majid's four forces**, and this is the
combination C1 never makes.

| Law | ✅ As stated | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Ferrel's Law** | The generalisation that moving bodies on a rotating Earth are deflected — **right in the Northern Hemisphere, left in the Southern** | Leong, scan PDF p.116 |
| **The Law of Deflection** | "**The Coriolis Force is absent along the equator but increases progressively towards the poles**" | Leong, scan PDF p.117 |

### The properties of the Coriolis force — the examinable list

```text
   THE SIX PROPERTIES OF THE CORIOLIS FORCE
   Everything a statement question can test lives in this box.

   1. IT IS APPARENT, NOT REAL. It exists because the frame of reference
      rotates. Viewed from outside the Earth there is no force at all —
      the air goes straight and the GROUND TURNS UNDER IT.

   2. IT ACTS AT RIGHT ANGLES TO THE MOTION. ✅ Majid: "a DEFLECTIVE force".
      Therefore it changes DIRECTION ONLY. It can never speed air up or
      slow it down. IT DOES NO WORK.

   3. ITS SENSE DEPENDS ON HEMISPHERE. ✅ Majid: "to the RIGHT in the
      Northern Hemisphere and to the LEFT in the Southern."

   4. ITS MAGNITUDE DEPENDS ON LATITUDE. ✅ Leong's LAW OF DEFLECTION
      (scan PDF p.117): "The Coriolis Force is ABSENT ALONG THE EQUATOR
      but INCREASES PROGRESSIVELY TOWARDS THE POLES."
                  EQUATOR  ──────────────────────────►  POLE
                  ZERO     ░░░▒▒▒▓▓▓███████████████     MAXIMUM

   5. ITS MAGNITUDE DEPENDS ON SPEED. Faster air is deflected more
      strongly. THIS IS WHY FRICTION MATTERS: friction slows the wind,
      a slower wind feels less Coriolis, so the balance breaks and the
      wind turns toward the low.

   6. IT ACTS ON ANY MOVING MASS, not only on air — which is why the
      ocean currents of Topic 12 obey the same rule. ↪ ROUTED BELOW.

   THE ONE-LINE SUMMARY FOR AN ANSWER:
   "A deflective, apparent force, acting at right angles to the motion,
    rightward in the north and leftward in the south, zero at the equator
    and increasing poleward, proportional to speed, doing no work."
```

### ✗ N-01 — the correction, stated in the owner's own voice

This is the topic's central conceptual correction and the handling matters as much as the content.

**What the book does.** ✅ Majid IWG, printed 3.12 (scan PDF p.85), heads a section "**Geostrophic
Winds (Rossby Waves)**" — the parenthesis presenting the second term as a synonym for the first —
and then defines **only geostrophic balance** beneath it: ✅ "Geostrophic winds are the winds moving
between pressure areas along the paths that are **parallel to the isobars**. In the upper troposphere
the **pressure gradient force equals the Coriolis force**."

**What the owner does with it.** ✗ C2, line 104: "✅ Majid Husain links Rossby-wave/geostrophic
upper-air flow with winds moving parallel to isobars where pressure-gradient force balances Coriolis
force in the upper troposphere." The conflation is **propagated by citation** — C2 has inherited the
heading's implication and tagged it ✅, as though a book had stated it.

**What the owner also does, two lines later.** ✓ C2, line 106, gives the **correct** definition of a
Rossby wave as a planetary wave. **The file therefore carries both the error and its own correction
and does not notice.**

**Why they are different objects, stated so that it cannot be forgotten.**

| | Geostrophic wind | Rossby wave |
|---|---|---|
| **Category** | A **balance** between two forces | A **wave** in a flow |
| **Where it lives** | At a point, or over a small region | Across thousands of kilometres; only a few crests fit around a hemisphere |
| **Existence condition** | PGF exactly equals Coriolis | The Coriolis parameter **changes with latitude** |
| **Treatment of latitude** | Coriolis is evaluated at **one** value and treated as constant | The **variation** of Coriolis with latitude is the restoring mechanism — the beta effect |
| **Time behaviour** | Instantaneous; it either holds or it does not | Evolves over days; may progress eastward, become stationary, or retrogress westward |
| **Relation between them** | — | Rossby-wave flow **is approximately geostrophic at every point along it** — which is exactly why the two get confused, and exactly why they are not the same thing |

⚠️ **The last row is the sophisticated half and it is worth the marks.** The confusion is not
arbitrary. The flow *in* a Rossby wave is, at each point, close to geostrophic balance. But being
geostrophic at every point does not make a flow a wave, any more than a river being locally downhill
at every point makes it a meander. **The wave is a property of the whole pattern; the balance is a
property of each point in it.** A candidate who can say that has done something no textbook in this
evidence base manages.

**The correction is not an attack on the owner.** It is **C2's own line 106 promoted over its line
104**, and it is Majid's own body text — which defines geostrophic balance perfectly well — promoted
over his own heading. Both owners contain the right answer; neither has noticed that it also
contains the wrong one.

⚠️ **Why this correction is placed at Lesson 7 and not Lesson 18.** It is a **force-balance** error,
not an upper-air error. Fix it where the forces are taught and the student arrives at Lesson 18's
jet and Lesson 19's wave already knowing that a balance condition and a planetary wave are different
categories of object. Defer it and the student carries the conflation through eleven lessons.
Lessons 18 and 19 therefore **use** this fork; they do not re-argue it.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the Coriolis force at the latitude where India's cyclones are born

✅ Leong's Law of Deflection says Coriolis is **zero at the equator and increases poleward**. That
single sentence has a sharp Indian consequence.

```text
   THE CORIOLIS EXCLUSION BAND AND INDIA'S CYCLONE BASINS

      LATITUDE          CORIOLIS           CONSEQUENCE FOR A TROPICAL VORTEX
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   0° EQUATOR          ZERO ░              Converging air CANNOT be organised
                                           into a rotating vortex — nothing
   up to about 5°      very weak ░▒        bends the inflow into a spin.
                                           NO TROPICAL CYCLONE FORMS HERE,
                                           even with abundant warm water.
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ~5° to ~20°         sufficient ▒▓       ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.119: typhoons
                                           occur "mainly in regions BETWEEN
                                           6° AND 20° NORTH AND SOUTH of the
                                           equator". THE LOWER BOUND IS THIS
                                           LESSON'S VARIABLE. Lesson 15 uses it.
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   beyond ~30°         strong ▓█           Coriolis is ample, but the SEA is
                                           too cool and the SHEAR too strong.
                                           A different limit binds.
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

   🇮🇳 WHY THIS IS AN INDIA POINT AND NOT A GENERIC ONE:
   The North Indian Ocean basin — the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea —
   lies almost entirely BETWEEN ABOUT 5°N AND 25°N. It is one of the few
   major cyclone basins whose equatorward edge is PRESSED AGAINST THE
   CORIOLIS EXCLUSION BAND. Systems forming in its southern part are forming
   close to the limit at which rotation can be organised at all.
   ⇒ Lesson 15 develops the genesis conditions.
   ⇒ Lesson 16 shows why a NARROW basin makes binary interaction rare.
   ⚠️ NO FREQUENCY FIGURE AND NO TREND IS STATED HERE OR ANYWHERE (U-02).
```

↪ **Route-out.** ⚠️ **The Coriolis deflection of ocean currents, the Ekman spiral and
wind-driven gyre circulation** belong to `Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity`.
**This lesson owns the force; Topic 12 owns what it does to water.**

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "Correcting a published textbook in a study edition is presumptuous. The student
should learn what the book says."

**Reply.** The student will meet the book. ✅ Majid's heading "Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)" is
printed, and a student who has been taught by this edition and then reads that heading must know
**why** it is a conflation, or they will assume the edition was careless. Three features of the
handling make it defensible rather than presumptuous. **The book's own words are quoted**, not
paraphrased. **The page is named** — printed 3.12, scan PDF p.85, with the folio corruption
disclosed. **The owner's own second statement is used as the corrective**, so the correction is
internal, not imported. The alternative — silence — would leave the student holding a false
synonymy that governs Lessons 18 and 19.

**Comparison worth carrying — the three balances a candidate should be able to name.**

| Balance | Forces involved | Where it describes the flow |
|---|---|---|
| **Geostrophic** | PGF = Coriolis | Straight flow in the free atmosphere, away from the equator |
| **Gradient** | PGF, Coriolis **and** the centripetal requirement of curvature | Curved flow around a low or a high — the real case near a cyclone |
| **Cyclostrophic** | PGF = the centripetal requirement; **Coriolis negligible** | Very small, very fast vortices — the **tornado** of Lesson 17, where the curvature is so tight that rotation dominates and the planet's spin barely matters |

⚠️ **The third row is the one that pays.** It is the reason N-04's correction in Lesson 17 works: a
tornado's pressure deficit is a **consequence of its rotation** under cyclostrophic balance, not its
cause — and a tornado can rotate either way precisely because Coriolis is not what is setting it.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-05**: the page carrying the four forces extracts its folio as "**8.12**",
an OCR corruption of 3.12. The folio is **not asserted**; the page is cited as "printed folio 3.12
*(folio corrupted in extraction; scan PDF p.85)*" or by PDF page alone. The content was read and is
not in doubt; the **page identity** is disclosed as imperfect.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Listing gravity among the horizontal forces.** ✅ Majid gives it first, but it acts
  **vertically** and is balanced by the vertical pressure gradient.
- ❌ **Saying the Coriolis force causes wind.** It **deflects** wind. Only the **pressure-gradient
  force** initiates motion.
- ❌ **Saying Coriolis changes wind speed.** It acts at right angles to the motion and **does no
  work**. Direction only.
- ❌ **Treating Coriolis as constant.** ✅ Leong's Law of Deflection: **zero at the equator,
  increasing poleward**. Its **variation** is what makes a Rossby wave possible.
- ❌ **Equating the geostrophic wind with the Rossby wave.** ✗ The conflation is printed as a heading
  in ✅ Majid printed 3.12 and propagated at ✗ C2 line 104; ✓ C2 line 106 contradicts it (N-01).
- ❌ **Forgetting friction's depth.** ✅ "about **500 m**", varying with surface texture, wind speed,
  time of day and year.
- ❌ **Explaining surface convergence into a low without friction.** Frictionless air would circle a
  low forever and never fill it. **Convergence is a friction effect.**
- ❌ **Applying Coriolis to a tornado.** At that scale the balance is **cyclostrophic** and Coriolis
  is negligible — which is why ✅ Majid can record counter-clockwise rotation "**except in rare
  instance**" (Lesson 17).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The forces acting on moving air are the mechanism underneath every **salient feature**
of atmospheric circulation, and the Coriolis latitude dependence is a structural precondition for
the **geophysical-phenomena** clause's cyclones.

**Owned demand served — one, and it is this lesson's anchor.**

**P10 — 2024 Prelims Q14, Coriolis force characteristics.** The capability tested is the **property
list**: what kind of force it is, what it acts on, which way, how it varies and what it cannot do.
The six-property box above is the complete coverage. ⚠️ **The official Set-A key is recorded as held
locally and is not disclosed here. No option letter and no elimination chain appears.**

**Answer use.** Any answer involving wind direction should carry one compact force sentence:
*"Air is set in motion by the pressure-gradient force alone; the Coriolis force then deflects it —
rightward in the Northern Hemisphere, zero at the equator and increasing poleward — until, above the
roughly 500 m friction layer, the two balance and the wind runs parallel to the isobars."* That
sentence is reusable in Lessons 8, 14, 15, 18, 19 and 20, and it converts description into dynamics
in twelve seconds of writing.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Four forces (Majid printed 3.12):** **gravitational** (vertical, balanced by the vertical
   pressure gradient) · **pressure-gradient** (the only initiator) · **Coriolis** (deflective) ·
   **friction** (opposes motion).
2. ✅ **Friction extends to about 500 m**, varying with "surface texture, wind speed, time of day and
   year, and atmospheric conditions".
3. ✅ **Coriolis deflects right in the Northern Hemisphere and left in the Southern** (Majid printed
   3.12; **✗ printed there as "Cariolis"**).
4. ✅ **Ferrel's Law** (Leong scan PDF p.116) generalises that deflection; ✅ **the Law of Deflection**
   (Leong scan PDF p.117) states that Coriolis "**is absent along the equator but increases
   progressively towards the poles**".
5. **Six Coriolis properties:** apparent · right-angled to motion · hemisphere-dependent ·
   latitude-dependent · speed-dependent · acts on any moving mass. **It does no work.**
6. **Geostrophic balance:** PGF = Coriolis; the wind runs **parallel to the isobars**, low on the
   left in the Northern Hemisphere; valid in the **free atmosphere** away from the equator.
7. **Within the friction layer the balance breaks**: friction slows the wind, weaker Coriolis cannot
   match the PGF, and the wind **crosses the isobars into the low**. **Surface convergence is a
   friction effect.**
8. ✗ **N-01, the topic's central correction:** ✅ Majid printed 3.12 heads a section "**Geostrophic
   Winds (Rossby Waves)**" and defines only geostrophic balance; ✗ **C2 line 104 repeats the
   equation by citation**; ✓ **C2 line 106 then gives the correct Rossby definition**.
9. **A geostrophic wind is a balance condition at a point. A Rossby wave is a planetary wave that
   exists because the Coriolis parameter VARIES with latitude — the beta effect.** One assumes away
   the term the other is made of.
10. **Rossby-wave flow is approximately geostrophic at every point along it** — which is why they are
    confused, and why they are still different objects. The wave is a property of the pattern.
11. **Three balances to name:** **geostrophic** (PGF = Coriolis) · **gradient** (adds curvature) ·
    **cyclostrophic** (PGF = curvature, Coriolis negligible — the **tornado**).
12. 🇮🇳 **The Coriolis exclusion band:** no tropical cyclone forms within roughly 5° of the equator,
    and ✅ Leong puts typhoon genesis "between **6° and 20°** north and south". The **North Indian
    Ocean basin sits pressed against that lower limit**.
13. ↪ **Coriolis on ocean currents and the Ekman spiral are Topic 12's.** The force is ours; the
    water is not.
14. ⚠️ **U-05:** the folio of this evidence page extracts as "8.12", an OCR corruption of 3.12. The
    folio is disclosed as corrupted and **not asserted**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Rossby waves are geostrophic winds in the upper troposphere, where
the pressure-gradient force balances the Coriolis force." Identify the error, name where it comes
from, and give the correct distinction.

**Model answer:** The sentence **equates two different categories of object**, and the equation is
traceable. ✅ Majid, printed 3.12 (scan PDF p.85, folio corrupted in extraction), heads a section
"**Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)**" and then defines only geostrophic balance beneath it:
"geostrophic winds are the winds moving between pressure areas along the paths that are **parallel to
the isobars** … the **pressure gradient force equals the Coriolis force**". ✗ The advanced owner
repeats the equation by citation at its line 104, and then ✓ gives the correct Rossby definition at
line 106, so the file carries both the error and its own corrective. **The distinction is this.** A
**geostrophic wind** is a **balance condition evaluated at a point**: two forces are equal and
opposite, the Coriolis parameter is treated as a constant, and the flow runs along the isobars. A
**Rossby wave** is a **planetary-scale wave** that exists *because* the Coriolis parameter **varies
with latitude** — the beta effect — which is precisely the term the geostrophic approximation
assumes away. The wave spans thousands of kilometres, evolves over days, and may progress eastward,
stand still or retrogress. The subtlety that makes the error understandable is that **the flow in a
Rossby wave is approximately geostrophic at every point along it** — but being geostrophic at each
point does not make a pattern a wave, any more than a river being locally downhill makes it a
meander.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the Coriolis force can be treated as a fixed quantity. Its
**latitudinal variation** is not a refinement — it is the entire reason planetary waves exist, the
reason ✅ Leong's Law of Deflection matters, and the reason no tropical cyclone can organise within
about five degrees of the equator.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 7

**Original question (20 marks, 250 words).** Analyse the forces acting on moving air, and show how
the latitudinal behaviour of the Coriolis force governs both the direction of surface wind and the
existence of planetary waves.

**Model:** ✅ Majid, printed 3.12, enumerates four forces: **gravity**, which acts vertically and is
balanced by the vertical pressure gradient; the **pressure-gradient force**, which alone initiates
motion by driving air from high to low pressure; the **Coriolis force**, "a **deflective** force …
to the **right in the Northern Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern**"; and **friction**, whose
effect "extends to a height of about **500 m**".

Above that layer the first two balance: PGF equals Coriolis and the wind runs **parallel to the
isobars** — geostrophic flow. Within it, friction slows the air; a slower wind feels weaker
Coriolis; the balance breaks and the wind **crosses the isobars into the low**. **Surface convergence
into a cyclone is therefore a friction effect**, without which a low could never fill.

Latitude governs the rest. ✅ Leong's **Law of Deflection** (scan PDF p.117): Coriolis "is **absent
along the equator but increases progressively towards the poles**". Hence no tropical cyclone
organises within about five degrees of the equator, and ✅ Leong places typhoon genesis between **6°
and 20°**.

Crucially, the **variation** itself has a consequence. A **Rossby wave** exists because the Coriolis
parameter changes with latitude — the beta effect — which the geostrophic approximation assumes away.
✗ Majid's heading "Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)" conflates a point balance with a planetary wave;
they are different objects. *(226 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Four forces enumerated with reasons **6** · Coriolis sign and latitude
behaviour **5** · geostrophic balance derived **4** · Rossby wave separated from it **4** · friction
depth stated **1**

| Coriolis deflection of ocean currents, the Ekman spiral, wind-driven gyre circulation | `Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity` |

```text

                   3.13 states flatly: "In all, there are SEVEN pressure belts on the
                   globe." Four is a list of TYPES; seven is the COUNT, and the count
                   is the Prelims-testable form (N-31). ✗ C1 line 225 also gives the
                   trade-wind deserts as "Sahara, THAR, Kalahari" where ✅ Leong gives
                   "Sahara, Kalahari, ATACAMA and the GREAT AUSTRALIAN Deserts"
                   (N-16) — an unsourced substitution that is also physically weaker.
📕 Book evidence : Leong scan PDF p.117 — FIG. 115, the complete pressure-belt and
                   planetary-wind diagram; the Law of Deflection; the trade-wind
                   desert list; South-Westerlies and North-Westerlies; the Roaring
                   Forties / Furious Fifties / Shrieking Sixties; the 30°-40°
                   shifting band · Leong scan PDF p.116 — trades within 5 degrees,
                   Ferrel's Law · Majid IWG printed 3.13-3.20 (scan PDF p.86-93) —
```

Progress: 8 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **pole-to-pole pressure-and-wind wheel**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 2 ended with an instruction: **the low latitudes have more energy than they can keep and the
high latitudes have less than they need, so energy must be moved poleward.** Lesson 7 supplied the
rules under which air can move. This lesson is what the planet does with those two facts.

If the Earth did not rotate, the answer would be a single enormous loop in each hemisphere: air
rising at the equator, flowing poleward aloft, sinking at the pole, returning along the surface. One
cell, one job done.

The Earth does rotate, and Lesson 7's deflection breaks that single loop into **three**. Poleward-
moving air aloft is turned so far to the right that by about 30° it is flowing nearly west-to-east
and has stopped making poleward progress at all. It piles up and **sinks**. That sinking is the
subtropical high; the sinking creates the deserts; and the air returning equatorward from it is the
trade wind. **The whole belt structure is Lesson 2's instruction being carried out under Lesson 7's
constraint.**

| Subtropical | Sub-Tropical High (**Horse Latitudes**) at **30°** | **25-35°**, ✅ "**dynamically induced**", with convergence aloft causing ✅ "the **subsidence of air**" and "**contraction of air volume, increase density**" | Follows Majid's latitudes with Leong-style names |
| Sub-polar | "**Temperate Low**" at **60°** | "**Sub-Polar Low**", **60-65°**, ✅ "**dynamically induced**" | — |

**✗ N-31, applied.** **Seven is the count; four is a list of types.** A candidate asked how many
pressure belts there are has a count question, and the count is seven.

**✗ N-32, applied.** The three sources give **three namings and three latitude bands for the same
physical belts**. The edition shows them as **alternative conventions for one structure** rather than
adopting one and suppressing the others, because this is exactly the sort of divergence a statement
question is built from. **Neither book is declared wrong.**

### The planetary winds, named correctly for both hemispheres

✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.116-117**, supplies the detail that makes the wind belts answerable rather
than merely listable.

| Wind | ✅ What Leong says |
|---|---|
| **Trade winds** | "the **most regular** of all the planetary winds"; the belt's core lies within about **5 degrees** of the pattern Leong describes at scan PDF p.116 |
| **Westerlies, Northern Hemisphere** | Named the **South-Westerlies** — because they blow *from* the south-west |
| **Westerlies, Southern Hemisphere** | Named the **North-Westerlies** — ✗ **C1 names the westerlies "SW" for the Northern Hemisphere only and gives the Southern Hemisphere no name of its own** |
| **The Southern Ocean belt** | From **40°S to 60°S**: the **Roaring Forties**, **Furious Fifties** and **Shrieking or Stormy Sixties** — unobstructed by land, therefore the strongest sustained surface westerlies on the planet |
| **Polar easterlies** | Cold, dense outflow from the polar high |

⚠️ **The naming rule, which disposes of a very common error.** ✅ Majid, printed 3.6: "**Winds are
named for the direction from which they come. A wind blowing from east to west is an east wind.**"
Therefore the **South-Westerlies blow towards the north-east**, and the Southern Hemisphere's
**North-Westerlies blow towards the south-east**. A candidate who reads "South-Westerlies" as "winds
blowing to the south-west" has inverted every westerly on the planet.

⚠️ **Why the Southern Hemisphere's westerlies are so much stronger.** There is almost no land between
40°S and 60°S. Friction (Lesson 7) is a surface-roughness term, and an ocean is smooth. With the
friction term minimised, the wind sits closer to its geostrophic value and sustains speeds that no
land-interrupted belt can.

This is the lesson's assigned India hook and the answer is partly a **refusal**.

```text
   over it, subsiding, drying, and making a desert of the whole subcontinent.

   WHAT THIS LESSON CAN SAY:

      north and south with the change in seasons."
   ✅ Leong scan PDF p.117: the belts migrate, and the band 30°-40° is
      alternately under two different regimes.
   ⇒ THE BELTS ARE NOT FIXED. A latitude's regime is a SEASONAL AVERAGE of
     whichever belt is over it at the time, and in South Asia the seasonal
     displacement is unusually large — large enough that the subtropical
     subsidence does not hold year-round over most of the subcontinent.
   ⇒ WHERE THE DISPLACEMENT IS LEAST EFFECTIVE — the far north-west, behind
     the Aravalli alignment and at the margin of the seasonal reach — AN ARID
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — thermal against dynamic, which is the examinable classification.**

| | Thermally induced | Dynamically induced |
|---|---|---|
| **Belts** | Equatorial Low; the two Polar Highs | The two Subtropical Highs; the two Sub-Polar Lows |
| **Cause** | Surface temperature — hot air rises, cold air sinks | **Rotation**: convergence or divergence of air **aloft**, forcing subsidence or ascent beneath |
| **Test** | Is the surface unusually hot or cold? | Is the surface temperature ordinary while air is piling up or draining away above it? |
| **Trap** | — | **A subtropical high is not a cold place.** It is a place where air is sinking. Candidates reverse this constantly |

**Objection.** "The three-cell model is a nineteenth-century simplification. Teaching it is teaching
something known to be incomplete."

**Reply.** ✅ Majid credits **Hadley, 1735** for the original model and presents the three-cell
arrangement as a development of it, and the arrangement does what a model should: it predicts the
**observed** alternation of wet and dry belts, the **observed** direction of the trades and
westerlies, the **observed** location of the world's hot deserts and the **observed** position of the
polar front, all from two inputs — unequal heating and rotation. Its known limitation is that the
middle cell is not a simple thermally direct loop. That limitation is itself the gateway to Lessons
18 and 19, where the mid-latitude transport turns out to be carried by **waves and eddies** rather
than by a smooth cell. **Teach the model, state its limit, and the limit becomes the next lesson.**

**Unresolved residual.** **N-32 is not resolved.** Leong puts the sub-polar belt at **60°** and calls
it a Temperate Low; Majid puts it at **60-65°** and calls it a Sub-Polar Low. No official source
settles a textbook naming convention, and none was sought, because the divergence is not an error —
it is two conventions. Both are shown.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Saying there are four pressure belts.** ✅ Majid: "**In all, there are seven pressure belts on
  the globe.**" ✗ C1 tabulates four types (N-31).
- ❌ **Calling the subtropical high a cold high.** It is **dynamically induced** — air piling up aloft
  and sinking. The surface beneath it is often among the hottest on Earth.
- ❌ **Listing the Thar as a trade-wind desert.** ✅ Leong's four are **Sahara, Kalahari, Atacama and
  the Great Australian Deserts**; ✗ C1 substitutes the Thar (N-16).
- ❌ **Reading "South-Westerlies" as winds blowing to the south-west.** ✅ Majid printed 3.6: winds
  are named for the direction they come **from**.
- ❌ **Giving the Southern Hemisphere's westerlies no name.** ✅ Leong calls them the
  **North-Westerlies**.
- ❌ **Treating the belts as fixed.** ✅ Majid: the Equatorial Low is "**not stationary**"; ✅ Leong's
  **30°-40°** band alternates between two regimes.
- ❌ **Explaining the monsoon here.** The belts migrate; **that** is this lesson's claim. The monsoon
  is `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism`'s and is not taught in this file.
- ❌ **Forgetting why the Southern Ocean westerlies are strongest.** Almost no land, therefore minimal
  friction (Lesson 7), therefore speeds no land-interrupted belt can sustain.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The planetary pressure and wind belts are the **largest salient feature of world's
physical geography** in the atmospheric half of that clause, and they supply the background state
against which every disturbance in Lessons 14 to 20 is a departure.

**Owned demands served.** None anchored here. This lesson is **structurally load-bearing** for
several: the subsidence that makes a subtropical high is the same compression that makes Lesson 10's
Foehn and the same mechanism as Lesson 5's upper-air inversion; the polar front located here is where
Lesson 14's cyclones form and Lesson 18's jet runs; and the belt migration is the background against
which Lesson 20's western disturbance travels. **No key is disclosed anywhere.**

**Answer use.** In any question about a wind, a desert, a rainfall regime or a storm track, **locate
it on the wheel first**. One sentence — *"the station lies under the subtropical high, which is
dynamically induced by convergence aloft and therefore subsides, drying the column"* — establishes
the mechanism before any regional detail. The wheel is the cheapest structural opening available in
this topic.

### Revision points

1. **The belt structure is Lesson 2's poleward-transport instruction executed under Lesson 7's
   deflection.** Without rotation there would be one cell per hemisphere.
2. ✅ **"In all, there are SEVEN pressure belts on the globe"** (Majid printed 3.13): one Equatorial
   Low, two Subtropical Highs, two Sub-Polar Lows, two Polar Highs. ✗ C1 tabulates **four** (N-31).
3. ✅ **Genesis split:** **thermally induced** — Equatorial Low, Polar Highs; **dynamically induced**
   — Subtropical Highs, Sub-Polar Lows. The dynamic ones exist because of rotation, not temperature.
4. ✅ **The subtropical high subsides** because of "**convergence of winds at higher altitudes**",
   giving "**contraction of air volume, increase density**" — and therefore a **subsidence
   inversion** (Lesson 5) and the world's hot deserts.
5. ⇄ **N-32: three namings, three bands.** Leong — Equatorial Low **0**, Sub-Tropical High **30°**,
   "**Temperate Low**" **60°**. Majid — **5°N-5°S**, **25-35°**, "**Sub-Polar Low**" **60-65°**. Shown
   as alternative conventions.
6. ✅ **The Equatorial Low is "not stationary and shifts north and south with the change in
   seasons"** (Majid printed 3.13).
7. ✅ **Trade winds are "the most regular of all the planetary winds"** (Leong scan PDF p.117).
8. ✅ **Westerlies: South-Westerlies in the Northern Hemisphere, North-Westerlies in the Southern**
   (Leong). ✗ C1 names only the Northern.
9. ✅ **40°S-60°S: Roaring Forties, Furious Fifties, Shrieking or Stormy Sixties** — strongest because
   there is almost no land, so the friction term is minimal.
10. ✅ **Winds are named for the direction they come FROM** (Majid printed 3.6). The South-Westerlies
    blow **towards** the north-east.
11. ✗ **N-16: the trade-wind deserts are ✅ Sahara, Kalahari, ATACAMA and the GREAT AUSTRALIAN**
    (Leong scan PDF p.117). ✗ C1 substitutes the **Thar**, which is a **monsoon-margin and
    rain-shadow** desert.
12. ✅ **The 30°-40° migration band** alternates between Westerlies in winter and Subtropical High in
    summer, giving **California, Iberia, central Chile, southern Africa and south-western Australia**
    winter-only westerlies.
13. ✅ **Hadley, 1735** is credited by Majid for the original single-cell model; the three-cell
    arrangement develops it. Its known limit — that the middle cell is not a simple direct loop — is
    the gateway to Lessons 18 and 19.
14. **The polar front at the sub-polar low is where Lesson 14's cyclones form and Lesson 18's
    polar-front jet runs.**
15. ↪ **Three route-outs:** the monsoon → Topic 16 · the Thar as a desert → Topic 07 · the
    Mediterranean climate → `Geography/basic/19_Mediterranean-Climate`.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The subtropical high-pressure belt exists because the air there is cold and heavy, and
it produces the world's deserts including the Thar." Correct every element of this statement.

**Model answer:** Three errors. **First, the cause is wrong.** The subtropical high is ✅ "**dynamically
induced**" (Majid printed 3.13-3.14), not thermal. Poleward-moving upper air is deflected by the
Coriolis force until, near 25-35°, it is flowing nearly west-to-east, converges aloft and must
subside; ✅ the descent produces "**contraction of air volume, increase density**". The surface beneath
is frequently among the hottest on Earth, so "cold and heavy" inverts the mechanism. Only the
**Equatorial Low** and the **Polar Highs** are thermally induced. **Second, the count is implicitly
wrong** if the belt is treated as a single feature: there are **two** subtropical highs, one in each
hemisphere, within ✅ Majid's total of "**seven pressure belts on the globe**". **Third, the desert
list is wrong.** ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.117, gives the trade-wind hot deserts as "**Sahara, Kalahari,
Atacama and the Great Australian Deserts**". The **Thar is not among them** and is better described
as a **monsoon-margin and rain-shadow** desert, because the belt structure over South Asia is
seasonally displaced far enough that subtropical subsidence does not hold there year-round. The
correct mechanism sentence is: subsidence under a dynamically induced high produces a persistent
inversion, which suppresses ascent and therefore precipitation, and the deserts follow — most purely
on **west coasts**, where a cold offshore current adds a second inversion.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a pressure belt's name tells you its cause. "High" describes the
pressure, not the temperature; two of the seven belts exist purely because the Earth rotates, and
asking whether a belt is thermally or dynamically induced is the first question to ask of any of
them.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 8

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine the planetary pressure belts and wind systems,
distinguishing their modes of genesis, and account for the seasonal migration of the belts and its
consequences.

**Model:** ✅ Majid, printed 3.13, states that "in all, there are **seven** pressure belts on the
globe": one **Equatorial Low**, two **Subtropical Highs**, two **Sub-Polar Lows**, two **Polar
Highs**. He divides them "on the basis of **mode of genesis**". The **Equatorial Low** and the
**Polar Highs** are **thermally induced** — hot air rises, cold air sinks. The **Subtropical Highs**
and **Sub-Polar Lows** are ✅ "**dynamically induced**": poleward upper flow, deflected by Coriolis,
converges near **25-35°** and subsides, with ✅ "contraction of air volume, increase density",
producing the subsidence inversion that makes the world's hot deserts — ✅ Leong's **Sahara,
Kalahari, Atacama and Great Australian**.

Between them run the planetary winds: the **trades**, ✅ "the most regular of all the planetary
winds"; the **Westerlies**, named by ✅ Leong as **South-Westerlies** in the north and
**North-Westerlies** in the south, reaching their greatest strength between **40°S and 60°S** — the
**Roaring Forties, Furious Fifties and Shrieking Sixties** — because there is almost no land to
supply friction; and the **polar easterlies**.

The belts **migrate** with the Sun: ✅ the Equatorial Low is "not stationary and shifts north and
south with the change in seasons", and ✅ the band **30°-40°** alternates between Westerlies in winter
and the Subtropical High in summer. Hence California, Iberia, central Chile, southern Africa and
south-western Australia receive winter-only westerly rain. *(219 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Seven-belt inventory with latitudes **5** · genesis thermal versus dynamic
**4** · planetary winds named for both hemispheres **3** · migration band and its consequence **3**

Progress: 9 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **diurnal reversal clock**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 8's winds blow for months across thousands of kilometres. This lesson's winds blow for hours
across a few dozen, and then **reverse**.

The reason is Lesson 3's land-sea asymmetry read on a **daily** rather than a seasonal clock. By day
the land heats faster than the water beside it; the air over the land expands, rises, and air from
over the water moves in beneath it — **a sea breeze**. By night the land cools faster; the flow runs
the other way — **a land breeze**. Nothing has changed except the sign of the temperature difference.

The same thing happens on a slope. By day the valley wall is heated and the air on it rises **up**
the slope. By night the wall cools, the air on it densifies, and it drains **down**. And here is the
naming trap that the examiner likes: **the wind that blows UP the slope by day is called the VALLEY
wind**, because — as Lesson 8 established — winds are named for where they come **from**, and that
daytime upslope wind comes from the valley.

**Every wind in this lesson is a daily reversal driven by differential heating, and every one of
them is small.** That is the discrimination the owned 2026 Mains demand in Lesson 10 turns on: these
are **not** the same kind of wind as the Foehn, and the difference is mechanism, not magnitude.

reads the **sign change**, which is the whole mechanism; the Loo is placed beside it precisely
because it does not fit.

### ✅ V-04 — the mountain-and-valley passage, and the tier that can be raised

✗ **C1's katabatic and anabatic block at lines 81-98 is tagged ⚠️** — the tier reserved for standard
knowledge with no book citation. That tier can be **raised**, and this edition raises it, on two
pieces of ✅ Majid.

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.24 (scan PDF p.97), in full:**

> "In the daytime, the valley and slopes of mountains receive heat from the sun. The air near the
> surface heats, expands, and rises up the mountains. This breeze, called the **valley wind for the
> place of origin**, is a warm wind and a **daytime or late afternoon** phenomenon. The clouds often
> seen forming over the hills in the afternoon are the result of condensation … At night the valley
> walls become cool. As the air at the surface cools, it flows down the slope **due to greater
> density**. This is the **mountain wind, also known as the night breeze**."

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.52 (scan PDF p.125), glossary:** "**Katabatic: Air drainage from elevated
regions, flowing as gravity winds.**"

**Between them these two passages state the mechanism, the direction, the timing, the naming
convention and the formal term.** ✗ C1's ⚠️ was conservative; the material is in the book.

⚠️ **And the passage carries an applied geography point that is worth more than the terminology.** ✅
Majid, same page: "In some mountain regions subject to frost, **the valley slopes are the preferred
places of orchards. The air moving up and down the valley slopes reduces the chance of the stagnant
conditions conducive to frost formation.**" **A land-use pattern produced by a daily wind reversal.**
It is the same observation as Lesson 5's Alpine thermal belt approached from the wind side instead
of the temperature side, and a candidate who can connect the two has connected two chapters.

### ✅ V-08 — the scale statement, and why it is the discriminator

✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.118**: the land and sea breeze "does not normally exceed **15 miles from the
coast**" (≈ 24 km).

**This is the only scale figure in either book for a thermally driven local circulation**, and it
does a job out of proportion to its size. It is the **test that separates a local wind from a
planetary one**.

| Property | Thermally driven local circulation | Planetary wind (Lesson 8) |
|---|---|---|
| **Horizontal scale** | ✅ "**does not normally exceed 15 miles from the coast**" (≈ 24 km) | Thousands of kilometres |
| **Duration** | Hours; **reverses within 24 hours** | Months; **seasonal at most** |
| **Driver** | A **local** temperature contrast between two adjacent surfaces | The planetary pressure-belt structure |
| **Coriolis** | Too small and too short-lived for deflection to matter much | Fully deflected; the belts exist because of it |
| **Reverses?** | **Yes, daily** | No |

⚠️ **The reversal, not the size, is the defining property.** A sea breeze that persisted for a week
would not be a sea breeze; it would be a monsoon — which is exactly the distinction that keeps the
monsoon out of this file. ✅ Majid's printed 3.21 treats the monsoon as a **periodic wind** and that
page is Topic 16's.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the Loo, taught with its evidence base in view

The Loo is the most-taught local wind in Indian syllabi and the **thinnest-sourced object in this
entire file**. This edition teaches it and says so.

```text
   THE LOO — WHAT IS ACTUALLY KNOWN, AND FROM WHERE

   ✅ WHAT THE BOOK SAYS, IN FULL:
      Majid IWG, printed 3.6 (scan PDF p.79), ONE SENTENCE, inside the
      six-controls-of-temperature discussion. It names the Loo as a local
      wind of the northern Indian plains. IT GIVES NO TIME OF DAY, NO
      SEASON, NO SPEED, NO MECHANISM AND NO SPATIAL EXTENT.

   ✗ WHAT C1 CLAIMS, AND HOW IT IS TAGGED:
      C1 line 265 states that the Loo "blows strongest 3-6 pm, May-June"
      AND TAGS IT ✅ — the tier C1 reserves for "from source book".
      NO BOOK IN THIS EVIDENCE BASE STATES IT.

   ✓ WHAT THE OTHER OWNER SAYS:
      C2 line 36 explicitly warns that "the old textbook gradient and
      clock-time should not be universalised."

   ⚠️ WHAT WAS SOUGHT AND NOT FOUND:
      U-14 — NO FIRST-PARTY IMD SOURCE DEFINING THE LOO AS A PHENOMENON
      WAS RETRIEVED THIS PASS. There is no official definition, no
      threshold and no criterion for it, in contrast to the heat wave,
      which has all three (Lesson 20).

   ✗ N-26, RESOLVED IN C2'S FAVOUR AND RE-TIERED:
      The clock-time and the month window are DOWN-TIERED from ✅ to ⚠️.
      They are reasonable, widely repeated, and UNSOURCED IN THIS BASE.

   ⚠️ WHAT MAY THEREFORE BE SAID — the mechanism, stated as mechanism:
      ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ A flat, dry, sparsely vegetated plain under a high late-spring │
      │ Sun heats intensely (Lesson 2: low albedo of dry soil, no      │
      │ latent-heat buffer because there is no moisture to evaporate). │
      │ Lesson 3's heat march puts the maximum over Rajasthan in May   │
      │ and Punjab-Haryana in June — ✅ Khullar printed 135 — so the   │
      │ thermal low is at its strongest and furthest north then.       │
      │ Air drawn across that surface arrives ALREADY DRY and is       │
      │ HEATED FURTHER IN TRANSIT, with no moisture source to modify   │
      │ it. The result is a hot, dry, desiccating westerly.            │
      │ ✅ Khullar printed 135 gives the setting's quantities: night   │
      │ minima "rarely below 20 °C" and a diurnal range "as high as    │
      │ 18 °C IN RAJASTHAN."                                           │
      └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   ⇒ AND THE CATEGORY POINT, WHICH IS WHY IT IS HERE AND NOT IN LESSON 10:
     THE LOO IS NOT A FOEHN. It has NO MOUNTAIN, NO WINDWARD ASCENT, NO
     RAINOUT AND NO LEEWARD COMPRESSIVE DESCENT. It is hot because its
     SOURCE SURFACE IS HOT, not because it has descended. Lesson 10's
     owned demand turns on exactly this distinction.
   ⇒ IT IS ALSO NOT A DIURNAL REVERSAL. It does not blow the other way at
     night. It is a REGIONAL-PRESSURE-DRIVEN wind over a heated surface —
     a third category, which is why the comparison in Lesson 10 needs
     three columns and not two.
```

↪ **Route-out, at the point of maximum temptation.** ⚠️ **Heat-wave health impact, heat mortality,
Heat Action Plans, cooling shelters and wet-bulb temperature as a survivability threshold** belong to

Lesson 20 owns the IMD criterion; Disaster Management owns the consequence and the plan.** ⚠️

a physiological threshold is routed**, and the ledger routes the 2025 Prelims demand on it to

↪ **Second route-out.** ⚠️ **The urban heat island as an urban-form and planning problem** — built
form, materials, green cover, master plans — belongs to
`Geography/basic/28_Human-Settlements-and-Urbanisation`. The **circulation mechanism** by which a
warm urban surface can drive a weak local inflow is this lesson's physics; the planning is not.

### ✗ N-34 — Majid's Haboob row, shown as the book's claim

✅ **Majid's Table 3.1, printed 3.25 (scan PDF p.98)**, describes the **Haboob** of the southern
Sahara and Sudan as a "**Hot, damp wind often containing sand and dust**", with a duration of
"**3 hrs**", an "average frequency of **24 per year**", occurring in early summer "**with the advance
of the ITCZ**".

⚠️ **The standard description is of a dry phenomenon.** A haboob is a **density current** — a cold
outflow of air from a thunderstorm's downdraught spreading along the ground and lifting a wall of
dust ahead of it. It is **dry**, and it is **cold relative to the air it displaces**, which is what
makes it propagate as a current at all. "Hot, damp" describes neither.

**✗ N-34, applied as the two-books rule requires.** The cell is shown **as the book's attributed
claim** with the standard description beside it, and **neither is asserted as settled**, because no
official source was retrieved to adjudicate a textbook table entry. ⚠️ The duration and frequency
figures are Majid's and are quoted as his.

### ✗ N-13 — the Berg wind is in the wrong continent

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.6 (scan PDF p.79)**, lists "local winds like **Gibli (Libya)**, **Chinook
(USA-Canada)**, **Mistral (France)**, and **Berg (Germany)**".

⚠️ **The Berg wind is southern African.** It is a foehn-type wind that descends from the interior
plateau over the escarpment towards the coast — the name is Afrikaans for *mountain*. It has no
German location.

**✗ N-13, applied.** On map asset **M6** the Berg wind is marked at its **true location on the
southern African escarpment**, with the book's "Germany" attribution **flagged on the map** as the
source's claim. The correction is carried into Lesson 10 as well, because the Berg is mechanically a
member of the Foehn family and not of this lesson's.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — the three categories of non-planetary wind, which Lesson 10 will need.**

| Category | Driver | Reverses daily? | Mountain required? | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Thermal local circulation** | A **local** surface temperature contrast | **Yes** | No | Sea and land breeze; valley and mountain wind |
| **Relief-forced descent** | **Compressive warming** of air forced down a lee slope | No | **Yes** | Chinook, Foehn, Zonda, Santa Ana, Berg — **Lesson 10** |
| **Regional-pressure wind over a heated surface** | A **regional** pressure gradient drawing air across a hot, dry surface | No | No | **The Loo** |

⚠️ **The three-category split is the answer plan for the owned 2026 GS-I Q14 demand**, which asks for
a comparison of the Loo, the Chinook and the Foehn. Two of the three are the same mechanism under
different names; the third is a different mechanism altogether. A candidate who notices that has the
classification the *compare* directive is asking for.

**Objection.** "A wind that lasts six hours and reaches 24 kilometres is not worth a lesson in a
Mains edition."

**Reply.** Three of its consequences are examined. The **valley wind builds the afternoon cloud** on
hill slopes, which is Lesson 12's material. The **mountain wind builds the valley inversion and the
frost pool**, which is Lesson 5's and which determines a **settlement and orchard pattern** that ✅
Majid states twice in two chapters. And the **Loo** is one of the three terms in an owned 15-mark
Mains comparison. The scale of a wind is not the scale of its consequences.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-14 is not resolved**: there is no first-party definition of the Loo, so
every quantitative statement about it in general circulation — its hours, its months, its speeds —
rests on repetition rather than on a source. This edition teaches the **mechanism**, quotes the
**one sentence** that exists, re-tiers the clock-time, and declines to supply numbers it cannot
attribute.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Thinking the valley wind blows down the valley.** It blows **up**, by day. ✅ Majid: "called the
  valley wind **for the place of origin**". Winds are named for where they come from.
- ❌ **Treating anabatic and katabatic as exotic terms.** ✅ Majid's glossary defines katabatic as
  "**air drainage from elevated regions, flowing as gravity winds**"; the mountain wind **is** a
  katabatic flow.
- ❌ **Giving the Loo a clock time and a month on a book's authority.** ✗ C1 tags it ✅; ✅ Majid's one
  sentence gives neither; ✓ C2 warns against universalising it (N-26, U-14).
- ❌ **Classifying the Loo with the Foehn.** No mountain, no ascent, no rainout, no compressive
  descent. Hot **because its source surface is hot**.
- ❌ **Classifying the Loo as a diurnal reversal.** It does not blow the other way at night.
- ❌ **Quoting Majid's Haboob as a "hot, damp" wind without qualification.** It is a dry
  thunderstorm-outflow density current; both descriptions shown, neither asserted (N-34).
- ❌ **Placing the Berg wind in Germany.** ✅ Majid prints it; it is **southern African** (N-13).
- ❌ **Letting a sea breeze run 200 km inland.** ✅ Leong: "**does not normally exceed 15 miles from
  the coast**" (≈ 24 km).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Local winds are **salient features of world's physical geography** at the smallest
scale the clause reaches, and they supply the mechanism for two land-use patterns and one owned
Mains comparison.

**Owned demands served.** None anchored here. This lesson supplies **one of the three terms of O7 —
2026 GS-I Q14**, whose model answer is written in **Lesson 10** and in the final arc; the Loo column
of that comparison is built here. **O7 is a subjective Mains demand and receives a full 250-word
model; no objective key is disclosed anywhere.**

**Answer use.** When a question names a local wind, **state its category before its description**.
*"The Loo is not a foehn-type wind: it has no windward ascent and no leeward compressive descent, and
is hot because the plain it crosses is hot."* That single sentence does the classification work that
the *compare* directive rewards, and it takes ten seconds.

### Revision points

1. **A thermal local circulation is defined by its daily reversal**, not by its size. If it does not
   reverse within 24 hours it is something else.
2. ✅ **Sea breeze by day, land breeze by night**; the land breeze is weaker because the night-time
   contrast is smaller.
3. ✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.118:** the land and sea breeze "**does not normally exceed 15 miles from the
   coast**" (≈ 24 km) — **the only scale figure in either book** and the discriminator against a
   planetary wind.
4. ✅ **Majid printed 3.24: the VALLEY WIND blows UP by day, "called the valley wind for the place of
   origin … a warm wind and a daytime or late afternoon phenomenon"**; the **MOUNTAIN WIND, "also
   known as the night breeze"**, drains **down** at night "**due to greater density**".
5. **Anabatic = upslope; katabatic = downslope.** ✅ Majid's glossary, printed 3.52: "**Katabatic: Air
   drainage from elevated regions, flowing as gravity winds**" — on which ✗ **C1's ⚠️ tier is raised
   to ✅** (V-04).
6. ✅ **The valley wind builds afternoon hill cloud**: "the clouds often seen forming over the hills
   in the afternoon are the result of condensation".
7. ✅ **The mountain wind builds Lesson 5's valley inversion and frost pool** — and therefore the
   land-use pattern: "**the valley slopes are the preferred places of orchards**" because moving air
   "reduces the chance of the stagnant conditions conducive to frost formation".
8. 🇮🇳 **The Loo rests on ✅ one sentence in Majid printed 3.6**, which gives **no time, no season, no
   speed and no mechanism**. ✗ **C1 line 265's "3-6 pm, May-June" is tagged ✅ but is unsourced**; ✓
   **C2 line 36 warns against universalising it**; **U-14: no first-party IMD definition was
   retrieved** (N-26).
9. **The Loo's mechanism as it may honestly be stated:** a flat, dry, sparsely vegetated plain heats
   intensely under a high late-spring Sun; air drawn across it is already dry and is heated further
   in transit with no moisture to modify it.
10. ✅ **Khullar printed 135 gives the setting:** night minima "rarely below 20 °C", diurnal range "as
    high as **18 °C in Rajasthan**".
11. **Three categories of non-planetary wind:** **thermal local** (reverses daily) · **relief-forced
    descent** (Lesson 10) · **regional-pressure wind over a heated surface** (the Loo). **The Loo is
    in the third.**
12. ✗ **N-34: Majid's Table 3.1 calls the Haboob "Hot, damp"**, with "**3 hrs**" duration and "**24
    per year**"; a haboob is standardly a **dry thunderstorm-outflow density current**. Both shown,
    neither asserted.
13. ✗ **N-13: Majid printed 3.6 places the Berg wind in "Germany"**; it is **southern African**, a
    foehn-type descent off the interior escarpment. Marked at its true location on M6.
14. ↪ **Heat-wave health impact, Heat Action Plans and wet-bulb survivability are Disaster
    Management's**; the **urban heat island as planning** is `Geography/basic/28`'s.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Loo is a hot local wind of the north Indian plains, like the
Chinook, blowing between 3 and 6 pm in May and June." Identify every problem and state what can be
defended.

**Model answer:** Three problems, of three different kinds. **First, the comparison is a category
error.** The Chinook is a **relief-forced descending** wind: air is compelled down a lee slope and is
warmed by **compression**, which is Lesson 10's mechanism. The Loo has **no mountain, no windward
ascent, no rainout and no leeward descent**. It is hot because the surface it crosses is hot, not
because it has descended. Grouping them conceals exactly the distinction a *compare* directive
rewards. **Second, the timing is unsourced.** ✅ Majid's single Loo sentence at printed 3.6 is the
only book statement in this evidence base, and it gives **no time of day, no season, no speed and no
mechanism**; ✗ the controlling owner tags "3-6 pm, May-June" as book-derived when no book states it,
and ✓ the advanced owner itself warns that "the old textbook gradient and clock-time should not be
universalised". **U-14 records that no first-party IMD definition of the Loo was retrieved**, so
there is no official criterion either. **Third, the mechanism is missing.** What can be defended is
this: a flat, dry, sparsely vegetated plain under a high late-spring Sun absorbs strongly and has no
moisture to divert energy into latent heat, so it heats intensely; air drawn across it by a regional
pressure gradient arrives dry and is heated further in transit. ✅ Khullar, printed 135, supplies the
setting — night minima "rarely below 20 °C" and a diurnal range "as high as 18 °C in Rajasthan".

**Misconception to avoid:** That all hot winds share a mechanism. Three distinct drivers produce hot
local winds — a **daily thermal reversal**, a **compressive leeward descent**, and a **regional flow
across a heated surface** — and the owned 2026 comparison demand is built precisely on a candidate's
ability to keep them apart.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 9

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Examine thermally driven local circulations, and explain
why the Loo cannot be classified among them.

**Model:** A thermal local circulation is defined by a **daily reversal** driven by a local
temperature contrast. By day land heats faster than adjacent water and the **sea breeze** blows
onshore; by night the sign inverts and the **land breeze** blows offshore. ✅ Leong fixes its scale:
it "does not normally exceed **15 miles from the coast**". On slopes, ✅ Majid names the daytime
upslope flow the **valley wind** "for the place of origin" and the night-time drainage the **mountain
wind**, "**the night breeze**" — a katabatic "**air drainage from elevated regions**". These build
hill cloud and the valley frost pool, so ✅ "the valley slopes are the preferred places of orchards".

The **Loo** fits none of this. It **does not reverse**; it has **no slope and no shoreline**; and it
is hot because the plain it crosses is hot — a regional-pressure wind over a heated surface.
*(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Diurnal reversal mechanism **4** · naming-convention trap **2** · scale limit
stated **2** · Loo grounded honestly **2**

## Lesson 10 — Winds that come down a mountain: the Foehn family, and the correction C1 needs

Progress: 10 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **windward-leeward adiabatic staircase**

### The intuition, before any term

Air rising over a mountain cools. Everyone knows that. So why is the air coming **down** the other
side **hot** — hot enough, in the cases both books record, to raise a valley's temperature by tens of
degrees within minutes and to strip half a metre of snow in a day?

Here is the thing to hold on to, and it is the correction this lesson exists to make. **Descending
air is warm for the same reason a bicycle pump gets hot: it is being compressed.** As a parcel
sinks, the pressure around it rises (Lesson 6), the parcel is squeezed, and squeezing a gas raises
its temperature. **That is the primary term.** It requires no rain, no cloud and no mountain —
Lesson 5's subsidence inversion under a subtropical high is the same physics with no mountain at all.

What the mountain adds is an **amplifier**. If the air rained out on the way up, it released latent
heat on the windward side and so cooled more slowly going up than it will warm going down. That
asymmetry makes the descending air **even hotter**, and drier, than it would otherwise be.

**Compression is the engine. Rainout is the supercharger.** C1 has these the wrong way round, and
both of its own named books say so.

### The primary visual — the adiabatic staircase

```text

                              ☁ CONDENSATION LEVEL and above
                           ╭─────────╮
                   ░░░░░░░░│  CREST  │
               ░░░rain░░░░░╰─────────╯▓

        ╭───────╮  ░░   ╱              ▓▓▓

      ╭─┴───╮   │   ╱                    ▓▓▓▓  ████ THE PRIMARY  ████
    ╭─┴─╮   │   │ ╱                       ▓▓▓▓ ████ TERM — AIR   ████
  ╭─┴─╮ │   │   ╱                          ▓▓▓▓████ IS SQUEEZED  ████

  ascending, cooling at the                   ▓▓▓▓█ WAY DOWN     ████

   WHAT THE TWO BOOKS ACTUALLY SAY — QUOTED, NOT PARAPHRASED:

      The wind "develops with the uplift of relatively MILD STABLE AIR."
      "the low pressure trough PULLS THE WIND DOWN the eastern slope of
       mountains to its original altitude."

      "The Chinook [is] STRENGTHENED IF PRECIPITATION TAKES PLACE in the air

   ✅ LEONG, scan PDF p.119 (folio clipped, U-03):
      "In descending the northern slope, the wind experiences an INCREASE IN
       PRESSURE AND TEMPERATURE. THE AIR IS COMPRESSED AND WARMED. Most of its
       moisture is lost and the wind reaches the valley bottom as a dry, hot
       wind — THE FOHN."

   ✗ WHAT C1 CLAIMS, lines 116-122:
      That the saturated-then-dry adiabatic asymmetry is "THE SINGLE MECHANISM
      EXAMINERS TEST" — and attributes the claim to those same two books.
   ⇒ N-15: TWO BOOKS, TWO INDEPENDENT PASSAGES, ONE WORD: COMPRESSION.
     THE OWNER IS CORRECTED, OPENLY, AND ITS OWN BOOKS ARE THE AUTHORITY.
```

**Caption.** One ridge, two limbs, two rates and two arrows of different weight. The thick arrow is
compression; the thin one is rainout. C1 has drawn them the other way round.

### Why the correction matters physically, and not merely bibliographically

It would be easy to treat N-15 as pedantry — both accounts predict a hot dry lee wind, so why
quarrel? Three reasons, and each is testable.

1. **The asymmetry account cannot explain a dry Foehn.** Foehn events occur with **little or no
   windward precipitation**. ✅ Majid is explicit that the wind "develops with the uplift of
   relatively **mild stable air**" and that precipitation **strengthens** it rather than creating it.
   If rainout were the mechanism, a rainless Foehn would be impossible. They are not.
2. **The compression account explains the non-mountain cases and the asymmetry account cannot.** The
   subsidence inversion under a subtropical high (Lesson 5), the warming in the descending limb of
   Lesson 8's Hadley cell, and the warm core of Lesson 19's Tibetan anticyclone are **all the same
   physics with no windward slope anywhere**. One mechanism covering four phenomena is worth more
   than one covering a special case.
3. **The two accounts assign the heat to different places.** Under the asymmetry account the heat is
   **latent heat released on the windward side**, carried over the crest. Under the compression
   account the heat is **generated during the descent itself**, by the work the atmosphere does on
   the parcel. **The second is correct and the first is an amplifier of it.**

⚠️ **The clean formulation for an answer:** *descent warms air by compression at the dry adiabatic
rate; if the air rained out on the windward side it ascended at the slower saturated rate, so it
arrives at the lee foot warmer and drier than it left the windward foot.* **Both terms, in the right
order, in one sentence.**

This is where the lesson earns its marks, because almost none of it is in the owner.

| **The Spearfish case** | **Spearfish, South Dakota, 1943** — a rise from **−20 °C to 7 °C**, that is **27 °C in two minutes** |
