### ✗ N-14 — Majid contradicts himself on the Chinook's name, within two pages

| Where | ✅ What is printed |
|---|---|
| ✅ Majid, **body text, printed 3.24** | "In the language of Red Indians Chinook means '**Snow eating wind**'" |
| ✅ Majid, **Table 3.1, printed 3.25** | "Chinook, in the language of Red Indians — **ice-eater**" |
| ✅ Leong, **scan PDF p.119** | "**the snow-eater**" |

**✗ N-14, applied under the self-contradiction rule.** **Both cells are shown and neither is asserted
as the book's settled claim.** Two of the three readings agree on *snow*; the third, from the same
author on the facing page, says *ice*. No official source adjudicates a textbook etymology and none
was sought. ⚠️ The name derives from the **Chinook peoples of the Columbia River region**; that
context is ambient and is not developed.

### ✅ V-03 — Table 3.1 in full, because the family is the answer

✅ **Majid IWG, Table 3.1 "Characteristics of Selected Local Winds", printed 3.25-3.26 (scan PDF
p.98-99), after Oliver *et al.*, 2002, pp. 98-99.** ✗ C1 reproduces none of it and omits **Zonda** and
**Santa Ana** entirely.

| Wind | Region | ✅ Character, as Majid prints it |
|---|---|---|
| **Bora** | Adriatic | Cold gusty north-easterly; **Trieste 36 days a year**; mean **50 km/h** in winter and **40 km/h** in summer; up to **100 km/h** in winter |
| **Chinook** | Rockies, USA-Canada | The lee-descent wind; see the evidence table above |
| **Etesian** | Eastern Mediterranean | Cool dry north-easterly of summer and early autumn |
| **Fohn** | Alpine lands — **and Santa Ana, Southern California** | "May attain **10-20 °C** in spring with **relative humidity of 10 per cent**. Predominant in spring" |
| **Haboob** | Southern Sahara, Sudan | "Hot, damp wind often containing sand and dust"; **3 hrs**; **24 per year**; early summer with the advance of the **ITCZ** — ✗ **N-34, see Lesson 9** |
| **Harmattan** | West Africa | "also known as **doctor** as it reduces the relative humidity in the coast countries of **Gulf of Guinea**" |
| **Khamsin** | Egypt and the eastern Sahara | "Regularly blows at a **50-day period (Khamsin = 50)**. Temperature varies between **40-50 °C**", with **Gibli**, **Sirocco** and **Leveche** as adiabatic variants |
| **Levanter** | Strait of Gibraltar | Damp, foggy, for perhaps two days |
| **Mistral** | Rhône Valley | "**master wind**"; "may reach a speed of **100 km per hour**", with the **Bise** noted |
| **Norther** | Texas, Gulf of Mexico to the western Caribbean | Cold outbreak |
| **Pampero** | Argentina, the Pampas | "**Southern Hemisphere equivalent of the Norther**" |
| **Zonda** | Argentina, lee of the Andes | "can attain **120 kilometer per hour** … comparable to Chinook and Fohn" |

⚠️ **Read the table for its mechanism groupings, not as a list to memorise.** The **Fohn row is a
family** — Foehn, Chinook, Zonda, Santa Ana, and the Berg wind of N-13 — all relief-forced
compressive descents under different local names. The **Bora, Mistral and Norther** are a different
family: **cold** outbreaks, in which dense cold air spills through or around relief and arrives cold
despite descending, because it started very cold indeed. The **Khamsin, Sirocco, Gibli, Leveche and
Harmattan** are a third: hot **source-region** winds, like the Loo, carrying the character of the
desert they crossed.

**That three-way grouping is the structure of the owned comparison.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why India has no Chinook belt, and what it has instead

```text
   THE QUESTION AN INDIAN STUDENT SHOULD ASK:
   India has the greatest mountain wall on Earth. Where is India's Chinook?

   THE ANSWER IS A CONFIGURATION ARGUMENT, AND IT HAS THREE PARTS.

   [1] ✅ MAJID'S CHINOOK GEOGRAPHY IS A NARROW LEE ZONE: "a narrow zone
       about 300 KILOMETERS EAST OF THE CREST OF THE ROCKIES FROM NEW
       MEXICO NORTH INTO CANADA." The Rockies run NORTH-SOUTH; the
       prevailing mid-latitude WESTERLIES (Lesson 8) run WEST-EAST;
       THE RANGE LIES ACROSS THE FLOW. Air is forced over it CONSTANTLY,
       and the lee slope is a PERMANENT DESCENT ZONE.

   [2] THE HIMALAYA RUNS WEST-EAST — ALONG, not across, the winter
       westerlies. And INDIA LIES ON ITS SOUTHERN, WINDWARD-FACING SIDE
       with respect to the summer flow and in its SHELTER with respect to
       the winter northerlies. The Indian plains are NOT the lee foot of a
       range lying across the prevailing flow.
       ⇒ NO SUSTAINED COMPRESSIVE DESCENT ZONE OVER THE INDIAN PLAINS.
       ⇒ NO INDIAN CHINOOK BELT.
       ⇄ CROSS-REFERENCE: this is HALF of the owned 2021 GS-I Q14 demand
         on MOUNTAIN ALIGNMENT AND LOCAL WEATHER. LESSON 13 IS THE PRIMARY
         OWNER OF THAT DEMAND AND CARRIES ITS MODEL ANSWER. THIS LESSON
         CARRIES ONLY THE FOEHN-AND-RAIN-SHADOW CONSEQUENCE. LESSON 19
         CARRIES THE HIMALAYAN-BARRIER-AND-JET CONSEQUENCE.
         ❗ NO ONE OF THE THREE LESSONS ANSWERS THE WHOLE STEM.

   [3] WHAT INDIA HAS INSTEAD: RAIN SHADOW ON A MASSIVE SCALE.
       The same adiabatic staircase, but read for its MOISTURE rather than
       its TEMPERATURE. Ascent on the windward side rains out; descent on
       the lee side warms and DRIES. Where India's ranges DO lie across a
       moist flow — the WESTERN GHATS across the summer south-westerlies —
       the result is a drenched windward slope and a dry leeward plateau.
       ⇒ THE MECHANISM IS THIS LESSON'S. THE RAINFALL GEOGRAPHY IS LESSON
         13'S, AND THE MONSOON THAT SUPPLIES THE MOISTURE IS TOPIC 16'S.

   AND A SMALLER INDIAN CASE WORTH ONE SENTENCE:
   Local descending warm winds are reported on the lee of Himalayan and
   trans-Himalayan ranges. ⚠️ NO FIRST-PARTY INDIAN SOURCE FOR ANY SUCH
   WIND WAS RETRIEVED THIS PASS, so this edition names the MECHANISM as
   available and asserts NO Indian foehn name, frequency, magnitude or
   location. The honest position is a stated mechanism and a named gap.
```

↪ **Route-out.** ⚠️ **Avalanche initiation, snowpack stability, snow mass balance and the Himalayan
cryosphere** belong to `Geography/basic/06_Landforms-of-Glaciation` and
`Geography/advanced/06_Himalayan-Glaciers-GLOF`. ✅ Leong's "**melts snow and causes avalanches**" is
quoted here as a **property of the wind**; the **snowpack** and its failure are Topic 06's.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**The three-column comparison the owned demand requires** — built here, with the model answer in the
final arc.

| | **Loo** | **Chinook** | **Foehn** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Region of prevalence** | Satluj-Ganga plain, north-west India and Pakistan | ✅ "a narrow zone about **300 km east of the crest of the Rockies**, from New Mexico north into Canada" | ✅ The **Alpine** leeward valleys; and by family ✅ **Zonda** (Andean lee), ✅ **Santa Ana** (southern California), **Berg** (southern African escarpment, N-13) |
| **Mechanism** | **Regional pressure gradient across an intensely heated, dry plain.** No ascent, no descent, no mountain | ✅ **Compression**: "the **primary source of heat** … is the **heat of compression**"; the trough "**pulls the wind down** the eastern slope"; it heats at the ✅ **dry adiabatic rate of 10 °C/km**; ✅ **strengthened** by windward precipitation | **Identical to the Chinook.** ✅ Leong: "the air is **compressed and warmed**. Most of its moisture is lost" |
| **Thermal character** | Hot because its **source surface** is hot; dry because its source is dry | ✅ Spearfish 1943: **−20 °C to 7 °C, 27 °C in two minutes**; ✅ Leong: **35 °F in 15 minutes** (≈ 19 °C) | ✅ Leong: **15-30 °F in an hour** (≈ 8-17 °C); ✅ Table 3.1: "may attain **10-20 °C** in spring with **relative humidity of 10 per cent**" |
| **Climatic impact** | Desiccation, heat stress, raised evaporative demand on a standing crop. ⚠️ **Criteria and health effects are Lesson 20's and Disaster Management's** | ✅ "**remove 15 cm (6 inch) of snow a day**", up to "**half metre … in a single day**"; ✅ "**the occurrence of frequent Chinooks means winter is mild**"; ✅ **160 km/h** gusts | ✅ "**melts snow and causes avalanches**"; ✅ Zonda "**120 km per hour**"; the ✅ **Chinook wall cloud** as the visible signature |
| **Reverses daily?** | No | No | No |
| **Classification** | **Regional-pressure wind over a heated surface** | **Relief-forced compressive descent** | **Relief-forced compressive descent** — *the same wind as the Chinook under a different name* |

⚠️ **The decisive line of the whole comparison:** ✅ **Leong's own 1967 Cambridge question, reproduced
at scan PDF p.122 (printed 115), asks about "(c) Fohn (Chinook) winds"** — **the examiner of that
paper treated them as one object.** A *compare* answer that treats all three as three different winds
has missed the classification; one that treats two of them as one wind and the third as a different
mechanism has found it.

**Objection.** "If the Foehn and the Chinook are the same wind, the owned question is trivial."

**Reply.** The question is *compare … with respect to their regions of prevalence, their nature and
their climatic impacts*, and the three-column structure above shows why that is not trivial. Two
columns differ **only in region and in local evidence**, while the third differs **in mechanism**.
The marks lie in holding that asymmetry visibly throughout — a *compare* directive requires a
**classification sustained across all three columns**, not three descriptions in sequence. The model
in the final arc is built to that construction and shares its spine with none of the other six owned
models.

**Unresolved residual.** **N-14 stands unresolved**: ✅ Majid's own body text and his own table give
the Chinook's meaning as *snow-eating* and *ice-eater* respectively, two pages apart. Both are shown.
And the **Indian foehn gap** is stated rather than filled: the mechanism is available, no first-party
Indian instance was retrieved, and none is invented.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Making the saturated-then-dry asymmetry the mechanism.** ✗ C1 does this and both of its named
  books contradict it. ✅ **Compression is primary; rainout is an amplifier** (N-15).
- ❌ **Requiring windward rain for a Foehn.** ✅ Majid: the wind "develops with the uplift of
  relatively **mild stable air**" and is "**strengthened if precipitation takes place**". Rainless
  Foehn events occur.
- ❌ **Treating the Foehn and the Chinook as different mechanisms.** They are the same wind under
  different names; ✅ Leong's 1967 Cambridge paper pairs them in one clause.
- ❌ **Omitting Zonda and Santa Ana.** ✗ C1 does; ✅ Majid's Table 3.1 places **Santa Ana** under the
  **Fohn** row and gives **Zonda** at **120 km/h**.
- ❌ **Classifying the Loo with them.** No mountain, no ascent, no compressive descent.
- ❌ **Asserting an etymology for the Chinook.** ✅ Majid says **snow-eating** in his text and
  **ice-eater** in his table, two pages apart (N-14).
- ❌ **Quoting Leong's figures without conversion.** **15-30 °F ≈ 8-17 °C; 35 °F ≈ 19 °C** (N-36).
- ❌ **Developing avalanche risk.** ✅ Leong's avalanche clause is a property of the wind; the snowpack
  is `Geography/advanced/06_Himalayan-Glaciers-GLOF`'s.
- ❌ **Claiming an Indian Chinook.** No first-party Indian instance was retrieved; the mechanism is
  stated and no name, place or magnitude is asserted.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Relief-forced local winds are **salient features of world's physical geography**, and
the adiabatic staircase is the same derivation that Lesson 13's orographic rainfall uses for
moisture instead of temperature.

**Owned demands served — one anchored, one shared.**

| Demand | Directive | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| **O7 — 2026 GS-I Q14** · "Compare the Loo, Chinook and Foehn winds with respect to their regions of prevalence, their nature and climatic impacts" | ***Compare*** · 15 marks · 250 words | **Anchored here.** The three-column table above is the answer's skeleton; the model is in the final arc. Its spine is a **sustained classification**, shared with none of the other six owned models |
| **O1 — 2021 GS-I Q14** · mountain-range alignment and its impact on local weather | *Briefly mention and explain* · 15 marks · 250 words | ⇄ **SPLIT DEMAND.** This lesson carries **only the Foehn-and-rain-shadow consequence**. **Lesson 13 is the primary owner and carries the model**; **Lesson 19 carries the Himalayan-barrier-and-jet consequence.** **No one of the three lessons answers the whole stem** |

**Answer use for a *compare* directive.** Choose the **axes first** and hold them across every term.
Here the axes are given in the stem — region, nature, climatic impact — so the construction is a
matrix, not a narrative. The analytical move that lifts the answer is to **state the classification
explicitly**: two of the three are one mechanism, the third is another.

### Revision points

1. **Descending air warms by COMPRESSION.** That is the primary term and it needs no mountain and no
   rain.
2. ✅ **Majid printed 3.24: "The PRIMARY SOURCE OF HEAT in the Chinook is the HEAT OF COMPRESSION."**
   ✅ **Leong scan PDF p.119: "The air is COMPRESSED AND WARMED."** ✗ **C1 lines 116-122 claim the
   adiabatic asymmetry is "the single mechanism examiners test" (N-15).**
3. ✅ **Rainout is a strengthening term:** the Chinook is "**strengthened if precipitation takes
   place** in the air stream as it rises over the windward side".
4. ✅ **The descending air heats at the DRY ADIABATIC RATE OF 10 °C/KILOMETER** (Majid printed 3.24).
5. ✅ **The wind "develops with the uplift of relatively MILD STABLE AIR"** and "the low pressure
   trough **pulls the wind down** the eastern slope" — so the descent is **forced**, not passive.
6. ✅ **Spearfish, South Dakota, 1943: −20 °C to 7 °C — 27 °C in two minutes.**
7. ✅ **Chinook belt: "a narrow zone about 300 kilometers east of the crest of the Rockies from New
   Mexico north into Canada."** Visible signature: the **Chinook wall cloud**. Gusts to **160 km/h**.
8. ✅ **Snow removal: "15 cm (6 inch) of snow a day … half metre of snow in a single day."**
9. ✅ **Leong: "15° to 30 °F within an hour" (≈ 8-17 °C) in the Alps; "35 °F within 15 minutes"
   (≈ 19 °C) in the Rockies; "melts snow and causes avalanches"; "the snow-eater"; "frequent Chinooks
   means winter is mild."**
10. ✅ **The family: Foehn (Alps) · Chinook (Rockies) · Zonda (Argentina, 120 km/h) · Santa Ana
    (southern California, under Table 3.1's Fohn row) · Berg (southern African escarpment — ✗ Majid
    prints "Germany", N-13).** ✗ C1 omits Zonda and Santa Ana.
11. ✅ **Table 3.1 Fohn row: "may attain 10-20 °C in spring with relative humidity of 10 per cent."**
12. ✗ **N-14: ✅ Majid's body text says the Chinook means "Snow eating wind" (printed 3.24) and his
    own Table 3.1 says "ice-eater" (printed 3.25).** Both shown; neither asserted.
13. ✅ **Leong's 1967 Cambridge question (scan PDF p.122, printed 115) pairs them as "Fohn (Chinook)
    winds"** — the examiner treated them as one object.
14. **Three families in Table 3.1:** **compressive descents** (Fohn, Chinook, Zonda, Santa Ana,
    Berg) · **cold outbreaks** (Bora, Mistral, Norther, Pampero) · **hot source-region winds**
    (Khamsin, Sirocco, Gibli, Leveche, Harmattan — and, by mechanism, the **Loo**).
15. 🇮🇳 **India has no Chinook belt** because the Himalaya runs **along** rather than **across** the
    prevailing flow and India lies on its southern side. **What India has instead is rain shadow** —
    the same staircase read for moisture. ⇄ **O1 is split; Lesson 13 is primary.**

### Concept check

**Question:** "The Foehn is warm because the air released latent heat as it rained out on the
windward slope, so it descends warmer than it rose." Evaluate this explanation against the two named
source books.

**Model answer:** The explanation **names an amplifier and omits the engine**, and both of the named
books say so. ✅ Majid, printed 3.24, states flatly that "**the primary source of heat in the Chinook
is the heat of compression**", adds that the wind "develops with the uplift of relatively **mild
stable air**", that "the low pressure trough **pulls the wind down** the eastern slope of mountains",
that "the subsiding air heats at the **dry adiabatic rate of 10 °C/kilometer**", and — decisively —
that the Chinook is "**strengthened if precipitation takes place** in the air stream as it rises over
the windward side". ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.119, agrees independently: "the wind experiences an increase
in pressure and temperature. **The air is compressed and warmed.**" **Rainout is therefore a
strengthening term, not the mechanism.** Three consequences follow. **Rainless Foehn events are
possible**, which the latent-heat account cannot allow. **The same physics operates without any
mountain at all** — the subsidence inversion under a subtropical high, and the warm core of the
Tibetan anticyclone. And the **heat is generated during the descent**, by compression, rather than
carried over the crest. The correct one-sentence formulation keeps both terms in order: descent warms
air by compression at the dry adiabatic rate, and windward rainout, where it occurs, makes the
descending air warmer and drier still because it ascended at the slower saturated rate.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a correction of this kind is bibliographic rather than physical.
The two accounts place the heat in **different locations** — one on the windward side, one in the
descent itself — and only the compression account generalises to the subsidence inversion, the
Hadley cell's descending limb and the Tibetan warm-core anticyclone of Lesson 19.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 10

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Explain the mechanism of relief-forced descending winds,
distinguishing the primary thermodynamic term from its amplifier, and illustrate with named regional
examples.

**Model:** A relief-forced descending wind is warm because **descent compresses it**. As a parcel
sinks, surrounding pressure rises and the atmosphere does work on it, raising its temperature at the
**dry adiabatic rate**. ✅ Majid is explicit: "**the primary source of heat in the Chinook is the heat
of compression**", with "the subsiding air heat[ing] at the **dry adiabatic rate of 10
°C/kilometer**", the descent forced because "the low pressure trough **pulls the wind down** the
eastern slope". ✅ Leong concurs: "**the air is compressed and warmed**."

The **amplifier** is windward rainout. If the ascending air saturated and precipitated, it cooled at
the slower **saturated** rate going up and warms at the faster **dry** rate coming down, arriving
hotter and drier. ✅ Majid places it correctly: the Chinook is "**strengthened if precipitation takes
place**". It is not the mechanism — the wind ✅ "develops with the uplift of relatively **mild stable
air**".

Named cases carry the magnitudes. ✅ **Spearfish, South Dakota, 1943**: **−20 °C to 7 °C in two
minutes**. ✅ The Chinook belt is "a narrow zone about **300 km east of the crest of the Rockies**",
gusting to **160 km/h** and removing ✅ "**half metre of snow in a single day**" — "the
**snow-eater**", so that ✅ "frequent Chinooks means winter is mild". ✅ The **Foehn** of the Alps
raises temperature **15-30 °F in an hour**; the ✅ **Zonda** reaches **120 km/h**; ✅ **Santa Ana**
sits in the same row. *(246 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Compression named as the primary term **5** · rainout placed as an amplifier
**5** · regional name family complete **3** · Loo separated on mechanism **2**

Progress: 11 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **saturation-curve phase diagram with a dew-point drop line**

### The intuition, before any term

A cold glass of water sweats in a Chennai afternoon. The water did not come through the glass. It
was **already in the air**, invisible, and the glass made it visible by **cooling the air touching
it** until that air could no longer hold what it had.

That is the whole of moisture physics in one observation, and it contains the three ideas this
lesson needs. **Air holds water vapour.** **How much it can hold depends on its temperature** —
warmer air can hold more. **Cool it enough and it must give some back**, as liquid, on whatever
surface is doing the cooling.

The temperature at which it must start giving the water back is the **dew point**, and everything
from dew on a lawn to the base of a thunderstorm 12 km high is the same event happening at a
different altitude.

The one move that trips candidates is this: **relative humidity can change without any water moving
at all.** Heat a sealed room and its relative humidity falls, though not a molecule of vapour has
entered or left. **Relative humidity is a ratio, and its denominator moves.** Hold that and the owned
demands in this lesson become straightforward.

### The primary visual — the saturation curve and the dew-point drop

```text

   Vertical axis: water-vapour content the air can hold at saturation.
   Horizontal axis: temperature. THE CURVE RISES STEEPLY — warm air holds
   disproportionately more. ✅ Majid printed 3.37: "COLD AIR CANNOT CONTAIN AS
   MUCH WATER VAPOUR AS WARM AIR."

           │                             ╱╱╱               the CEILING on

           │            ╱╱╱           ┌────┤  warm, UNSATURATED:
           │        ╱╱╱               │    │  it holds less than its ceiling
           │     ╱╱╱   ●◄─────────────┘    │  → RH is LOW, because the

   ══ THE TWO MOVES ON THIS PLANE — the discrimination that is tested ════════

     │   is at its LOWEST, so the SAME vapour    HIGHEST VALUES ARE OBSERVED
```

**Caption.** One curve, two kinds of move, and a 24-hour strip in which humidity and temperature run
in **antiphase** without any water changing hands.

### Absolute, specific and relative — three quantities, kept apart

| Quantity | What it measures | Unit | Does it change when air is simply heated or cooled? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Absolute humidity** | Mass of water vapour **per unit volume** of air | ✅ Leong, scan PDF p.103: **grams per cubic metre** | **Changes with volume**, so warming air expands it and dilutes it. No water has moved |
| **Specific humidity** | Mass of water vapour **per unit mass** of air | g/kg | **No.** This is why it is the meteorologist's preferred measure — it is conserved as a parcel rises and expands |
| **Relative humidity** | Vapour content as a **percentage of the saturation ceiling** at that temperature | per cent | **Yes, strongly** — because the **denominator** moves with temperature |

⚠️ **This table settles more Prelims traps than any other in the lesson.** Note the subtlety in row
one: **absolute humidity is per unit volume**, so it changes when a parcel expands even though no
vapour has been gained or lost. **Specific humidity does not.** A candidate who treats "absolute" as
meaning "unchanging" has it backwards.

### ✅ V-07 — the hydrological shares and the daily cycle, as Majid states them

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.35-3.37 (scan PDF p.108-110):**

- "**97 per cent** of the Earth's water is in the oceans … the remaining **3 per cent** is mostly ice
  … Rivers, lakes, and soil moisture account for **less than one per cent** … The atmosphere contains
  only **0.35 per cent** of all water available."
- "**Relative humidity commonly displays a daily cycle which has phase opposite to that of
  temperature so that the highest values are observed near dawn and the lowest during the**
  [afternoon]."
- "**Cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air.**"

⚠️ **The 0.35 per cent figure is the one to reflect on**, because it is the lesson's central
paradox. The atmosphere holds a third of one per cent of the planet's water, and that trivial
reservoir delivers every drop of rain that has ever fallen. The explanation is **turnover**: the
atmospheric store is small but it is replaced very rapidly. **A small, fast store can deliver a large
flux.** This is the same stock-versus-flow discrimination that Lesson 2's latent-heat term relies on.

### ✅ The dawn maximum — the exact form of C1's trap, and the owned 2024 anchor

✗ **C1, line 230**, carries the relative-humidity trap in a general form. ✅ **Majid's sentence is
precise**, and the precision is the content.

```text
   WHY RELATIVE HUMIDITY PEAKS AT DAWN — THE FULL CHAIN

   [1] THROUGH THE NIGHT the surface loses longwave radiation (Lesson 2) and
       cools the air in contact with it (Lesson 5). TEMPERATURE FALLS, hour
       by hour, reaching its MINIMUM AROUND SUNRISE — not at midnight, because
       the loss continues for as long as it exceeds the (zero) input.
                       ↓
   [2] AS TEMPERATURE FALLS, THE SATURATION CEILING FALLS WITH IT.
       ✅ "Cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air."
                       ↓
   [3] THE VAPOUR CONTENT HAS NOT CHANGED. No source has added water; no
       condensation has yet removed it. THE NUMERATOR IS CONSTANT.
                       ↓
   [4] THEREFORE THE RATIO RISES. Same numerator, falling denominator.
       ✅ "the HIGHEST VALUES ARE OBSERVED NEAR DAWN."
                       ↓
   [5] IF THE CEILING FALLS FAR ENOUGH TO MEET THE CONTENT — that is, if the
       temperature reaches the DEW POINT — CONDENSATION BEGINS.
       On the ground it is DEW. In the air it is FOG. On a cold surface below
       0 °C it is FROST. THREE NAMES, ONE EVENT.
                       ↓
   [6] AFTER SUNRISE the surface warms, the ceiling rises faster than any
       evaporation can fill it, AND RELATIVE HUMIDITY COLLAPSES THROUGH THE
       MORNING to its minimum in the MID-AFTERNOON — the moment at which
       TEMPERATURE peaks.
   ⇒ TEMPERATURE AND RELATIVE HUMIDITY RUN IN ANTIPHASE OVER 24 HOURS,
     WITH NO NET MOVEMENT OF WATER REQUIRED TO PRODUCE THE CYCLE.
```

### P1 — why dew does not form on a cloudy night

This is **the owned 2019 Prelims Q44** demand, and the answer is a Lesson 2 and Lesson 5 argument
wearing a Lesson 11 costume.

```text
   THE DEWLESS CLOUDY NIGHT — THE COMPLETE CAUSAL CHAIN

   CLEAR NIGHT                          │  CLOUDY NIGHT
   ───────────────────────────────────  │ ────────────────────────────────────
   The surface radiates longwave        │  The surface radiates longwave
   upward. With a clear sky, much of    │  upward. THE CLOUD ABSORBS IT and
   it escapes to space through the      │  RE-RADIATES A LARGE PART OF IT BACK
   atmosphere and through the           │  DOWNWARD (Lesson 2, stage [6]).
   atmospheric window.                  │
           ↓                            │          ↓
   NET LOSS IS LARGE.                   │  NET LOSS IS MUCH SMALLER.
           ↓                            │          ↓
   SURFACE TEMPERATURE FALLS FAST AND   │  SURFACE TEMPERATURE FALLS SLOWLY
   FAR, through the night.              │  AND LITTLE.
           ↓                            │          ↓
   IT REACHES THE DEW POINT of the air  │  IT DOES NOT REACH THE DEW POINT.
   touching it.                         │
           ↓                            │          ↓
   CONDENSATION ON THE SURFACE = DEW.   │  NO CONDENSATION. NO DEW.

   ⇒ THE ANSWER IS NOT "the cloud blocks the dew." Nothing falls from above.
     Dew CONDENSES OUT OF THE AIR TOUCHING A COLD SURFACE.
   ⇒ THE CLOUD PREVENTS DEW BY PREVENTING THE COOLING THAT WOULD CAUSE IT.
   ⇒ THE SAME LOGIC IS MAJID'S SECOND GROUND-INVERSION CONDITION (Lesson 5):
     "cloudless clear sky". ONE MECHANISM, TWO SYLLABUS OBJECTS.

   AND THE SECOND CONDITION THAT MUST BE NAMED FOR A COMPLETE ANSWER:
   CALM. A breeze MIXES the shallow chilled layer with warmer air above,
   preventing the surface layer from reaching its dew point — which is why
   ✅ Majid's FOURTH ground-inversion condition is "calm atmosphere or slow
   movement of air". DEW NEEDS A CLEAR SKY AND STILL AIR, TOGETHER.
```

### P9 — water vapour with altitude

This is **the owned 2024 Prelims Q12** demand, and it needs three separate statements.

| Statement | Reason |
|---|---|
| **Vapour content decreases sharply with altitude** | The source is the **surface** — ocean, soil, vegetation. Vapour is supplied from below and mixed upward; the further from the source, the less there is |
| **It decreases faster than air density does** | Because temperature also falls with height (Lesson 4), so the **saturation ceiling** falls, and air that rises and cools **condenses out** its excess rather than carrying it. Each ascent strips the parcel |
| **It is therefore concentrated in the lowest few kilometres** | Which is why the troposphere is both the overturning layer and the wet layer, and why the stratosphere's dryness is a **consequence** of the tropopause's cold trap rather than an independent fact |

⚠️ **The precise and testable form:** the atmosphere's water vapour is overwhelmingly a **lower
tropospheric** quantity, and the mechanism that keeps it there is **condensation during ascent**, not
gravity.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — why Indian relative humidity collapses between dawn and afternoon

```text
   ONE INDIAN DAY, ONE AIR MASS, TWO HUMIDITY REGIMES

   A PRE-MONSOON DAY, INLAND NORTHERN INDIA
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   0600   Temperature at its minimum. ✅ Khullar, printed 135: night minima
          "rarely below 20 °C" in the hot season. Saturation ceiling LOW.
          → RELATIVE HUMIDITY HIGH, and possibly at saturation — FOG or DEW.
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   1500   Temperature at its maximum. ✅ Khullar: the diurnal range "may be
          as high as 18 °C IN RAJASTHAN". The ceiling has risen enormously.
          THE AIR'S VAPOUR CONTENT IS ALMOST UNCHANGED — this is an inland,
          continental air mass with no maritime supply.
          → RELATIVE HUMIDITY COLLAPSES. The air feels bone dry.
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ⇒ THE SAME AIR, THE SAME WATER, A COMPLETELY DIFFERENT HUMIDITY.
     NOTHING MOVED EXCEPT THE DENOMINATOR.

   AND THE CONTRAST THAT MAKES IT A GEOGRAPHY POINT:
   A COASTAL STATION on the same day has a MARITIME SUPPLY. Its vapour
   content is continuously replenished by evaporation from the sea and by
   Lesson 9's SEA BREEZE, so BOTH the numerator and the denominator rise
   together and the relative humidity stays high all day.
   ⇒ THE DIURNAL COLLAPSE OF RELATIVE HUMIDITY IS A CONTINENTALITY
     SIGNATURE, exactly as Lesson 3's diurnal temperature range was.
     Two different instruments reading the same underlying property.

   ⚠️ THE WET-BULB TEMPERATURE, DEFINED ONCE AND ONCE ONLY:
   The temperature a thermometer reads when its bulb is kept wet and
   ventilated, so that evaporation cools it. The DRIER the air, the MORE
   evaporation, the LOWER the wet-bulb reading relative to the dry-bulb.
   AT SATURATION THE TWO READINGS COINCIDE. That difference — the
   wet-bulb depression — is how ✅ Majid's "wet-and-dry-bulb thermometer"
   (Table 3.3) measures humidity at all.
   ↪ WET-BULB AS A PHYSIOLOGICAL SURVIVABILITY THRESHOLD IS ROUTED to
     Disaster-Management/basic/09_Drought-Heat-Waves-and-Slow-Onset-Risk,
     to which the ledger routes the 2025 Prelims demand on it BY NAME.
     THE PSYCHROMETRIC QUANTITY IS DEFINED HERE; THE THRESHOLD IS NOT OURS.
```

↪ **Route-out.** ⚠️ **Evapotranspiration, soil moisture and the water balance** belong to
`Geography/basic/04_Weathering-MassMovement-Groundwater`. **This lesson owns vapour in the air; Topic
04 owns water in the ground and the accounting between them.**

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — four condensation products, one event.**

| Product | Where the cooling happens | Where the water ends up |
|---|---|---|
| **Dew** | At a **surface** cooled below the dew point | On the surface, as liquid |
| **Frost** | At a surface cooled below the dew point **and below 0 °C** | On the surface, as ice — note it is deposition, not frozen dew |
| **Fog** | In a **layer of air** cooled below its dew point | Suspended droplets in the air, at the ground |
| **Cloud** | In a **parcel lifted** until it cools to its dew point | Suspended droplets aloft — Lesson 12 |

⚠️ **Fog and cloud are the same object at different heights**, and the only difference is whether the
cooling was achieved by **radiative loss at the ground** or by **lifting**. A candidate who says that
has connected Lessons 5, 11 and 12 in one clause.

**Objection.** "Relative humidity is the number everyone uses, so it must be the useful one."

**Reply.** It is the number everyone **feels**, because human thermal comfort depends on the
evaporation rate and therefore on how close the air is to saturation. It is a poor measure of **how
much water is present**, because its denominator moves. For tracking an air mass across a continent
— which is what Lesson 14's fronts and Lesson 20's western disturbance require — **specific
humidity** is the conserved quantity. **Use relative humidity for what the air does to a person and
specific humidity for what the air is.**

**Unresolved residual.** None material. ✅ Majid's printed 3.36-3.37 supplies the daily cycle, the
cold-air statement and the water shares; ✅ Leong supplies the absolute-humidity unit. The only
refusal is the deliberate one: **no wet-bulb health threshold is stated**, because the ledger routes
that demand out by name.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **"Warm air holds more moisture because it has more room between molecules."** The reason is
  **thermodynamic** — the saturation vapour pressure rises steeply with temperature. ✅ Majid states
  the fact: "**cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air**".
- ❌ **Treating relative humidity as a measure of water content.** It is a **ratio**, and the
  denominator moves with temperature.
- ❌ **Assuming absolute humidity is unchanged by heating.** It is measured **per unit volume**
  (✅ Leong, grams per cubic metre), so expansion dilutes it. **Specific humidity** is the conserved
  measure.
- ❌ **Saying a cloudy night "blocks the dew".** Nothing falls. The cloud **re-radiates longwave
  downward**, so the surface never reaches its dew point (P1).
- ❌ **Omitting calm from the dew explanation.** Wind mixes the chilled layer away; ✅ Majid's fourth
  ground-inversion condition is "calm atmosphere or slow movement of air".
- ❌ **Saying relative humidity peaks when it rains.** ✅ Majid: it peaks **near dawn**, in antiphase
  with temperature, with no water added.
- ❌ **Treating vapour's decline with height as a density effect.** It declines because the **source
  is the surface** and because **ascent condenses the excess out**.
- ❌ **Stating a wet-bulb survivability threshold.** Defined here as a psychrometric quantity only;
  the threshold is `Disaster-Management/basic/09`'s.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Atmospheric moisture is a **salient feature of world's physical geography**, and the
dew point is the single variable that connects the radiation lessons to the cloud and precipitation
lessons.

**Owned demands served — two.**

| Demand | What it tests | What this lesson supplies |
|---|---|---|
| **P1 — 2019 Prelims Q44** · absence of dewdrop formation on cloudy nights | Whether the candidate knows **dew is a surface condensation**, not a fall | The full chain: cloud re-radiates longwave down → surface does not cool to the dew point → no condensation. Plus the **calm** condition |
| **P9 — 2024 Prelims Q12** · water-vapour characteristics with altitude | The **profile** and its reason | Surface source + condensation during ascent → vapour concentrated in the lowest troposphere; falls faster than density |

**Answer neutrality.** Both are **objective** demands. **P1's key is not available locally and none
is inferred. P9's official Set-A key is recorded as held locally and is not disclosed.** No option
letter and no elimination chain appears.

**Answer use.** Any moisture answer should distinguish the **two moves on the saturation plane** in
one sentence: *"Cooling air at constant vapour content raises its relative humidity without adding
water; only evaporation or condensation changes the vapour content itself."* That sentence disposes
of most of the traps in this lesson and sets up Lessons 12 and 13 at no extra cost.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **"Cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air"** (Majid printed 3.37). The
   saturation ceiling rises steeply with temperature.
2. **Dew point:** the temperature to which air must be cooled, at constant pressure and constant
   vapour content, for **saturation** to occur (✅ Majid's glossary, printed 3.52).
3. **Two moves on the saturation plane:** a **horizontal** move changes temperature and therefore
   **relative** humidity only; a **vertical** move changes the **vapour content** and requires
   evaporation or condensation.
4. **Three humidity measures:** **absolute** (mass per unit **volume** — ✅ Leong, **grams per cubic
   metre**, scan PDF p.103) · **specific** (mass per unit **mass**, conserved during ascent) ·
   **relative** (a percentage of the ceiling).
5. ✅ **"Relative humidity commonly displays a daily cycle which has phase opposite to that of
   temperature so that the highest values are observed near dawn and the lowest during the**
   [afternoon]" (Majid printed 3.36-3.37).
6. **Relative humidity peaks at dawn because the ceiling falls, not because water is added.** Same
   numerator, smaller denominator.
7. ✅ **Water shares (Majid printed 3.35):** oceans **97 per cent**; the remaining **3 per cent**
   mostly ice; rivers, lakes and soil moisture **less than one per cent**; the **atmosphere only 0.35
   per cent**.
8. **A small, fast store delivers a large flux** — the atmosphere holds 0.35 per cent of the planet's
   water and delivers all of its rain, because its turnover is rapid.
9. **Dew, frost, fog and cloud are one event at four locations.** Dew and frost at a **surface**; fog
   in a **layer**; cloud in a **lifted parcel**.
10. **P1's answer:** on a cloudy night the cloud **absorbs and re-radiates the surface's longwave
    emission downward**, so the surface never cools to the dew point. **Calm** is the second
    necessary condition.
11. **P9's answer:** vapour declines sharply with height because its **source is the surface** and
    because **ascent condenses the excess out** — it declines faster than density does.
12. **Wet-bulb temperature:** the reading of a wet, ventilated bulb, depressed below the dry-bulb by
    evaporation; the two coincide at saturation. ↪ **Its survivability threshold is Disaster
    Management's.**
13. 🇮🇳 **An inland Indian day shows the collapse:** high relative humidity at dawn, a collapse by
    mid-afternoon, with ✅ Khullar's diurnal range "as high as **18 °C in Rajasthan**" and night minima
    "rarely below **20 °C**". A **coastal** station, continuously resupplied, does not collapse.
14. **The diurnal collapse of relative humidity is a continentality signature**, like Lesson 3's
    diurnal temperature range.

### Concept check

**Question:** A sealed room at 30 °C has a relative humidity of 50 per cent. It is cooled to 18 °C
with nothing entering or leaving. Explain what happens to each humidity measure, and connect the
result to why dew forms on clear nights but not on cloudy ones.

**Model answer:** **Specific humidity is unchanged** — the mass of vapour per unit mass of air cannot
alter in a sealed room. **Absolute humidity is essentially unchanged too**, since the volume is
fixed; were the air free to contract, the figure would rise, because absolute humidity is measured
per unit **volume** (✅ Leong gives it in **grams per cubic metre**). **Relative humidity rises
sharply.** ✅ Majid, printed 3.37, supplies the reason: "**cold air cannot contain as much water
vapour as warm air**", so cooling lowers the **saturation ceiling** while the vapour content stays
put. The ratio has a constant numerator and a shrinking denominator. If cooling continues until the
ceiling meets the content — the **dew-point temperature**, which ✅ Majid's glossary defines as the
temperature at which saturation occurs at constant pressure and vapour content — relative humidity
reaches 100 per cent and further cooling forces **condensation**, appearing on the coldest surfaces
first. **The connection to dew is exact.** On a clear night the ground radiates longwave freely to
space, cools rapidly, and the air touching it is chilled **below its dew point**, so vapour condenses
on to it as dew — or, below 0 °C, as frost. On a cloudy night the cloud absorbs that longwave and
re-radiates much of it downward, so the surface cools far less and **never reaches the dew point**.
The cloud does not block falling dew; it prevents the cooling that would create it. A **calm** is
equally necessary, since wind mixes the chilled layer away.

**Misconception to avoid:** That relative humidity measures how much water is in the air. It
measures how close the air is to its ceiling, and the ceiling moves with temperature — which is why
the same air can read 95 per cent at dawn and 25 per cent the same afternoon without gaining or
losing a single gram of vapour.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 11

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine the relationship between temperature, saturation
and the dew point, and explain why relative humidity and temperature run in opposite phase over a
24-hour cycle.

**Model:** Air's capacity to hold water vapour is fixed by its **temperature**: ✅ Majid, printed 3.37,
"**cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air**". The **saturation curve** rises
steeply with temperature, so a parcel's **relative humidity** — its content as a share of that
ceiling — depends on two quantities, only one of which involves water.

Cooling a parcel at constant vapour content is a **horizontal move** toward the curve: nothing is
added, the ceiling descends, and the ratio climbs. The temperature at which the ceiling meets the
content is the **dew point**, which ✅ Majid's glossary (printed 3.52) defines as the temperature at
which saturation occurs at constant pressure and vapour content. Below it, condensation is
compulsory — as **dew** on a surface, **frost** below 0 °C, **fog** in a layer, **cloud** in a lifted
parcel.

Hence the daily antiphase. ✅ Majid records that relative humidity "displays a daily cycle which has
**phase opposite to that of temperature**, so that the **highest values are observed near dawn** and
the lowest during the [afternoon]". Through the night, radiative loss lowers temperature and with it
the ceiling, while the vapour content is unchanged; by mid-afternoon the ceiling has risen far faster
than evaporation can fill it.

In India the collapse is a **continentality signature**: inland air, with no maritime resupply,
swings through ✅ Khullar's **18 °C** Rajasthan diurnal range, while a coastal station, continuously
replenished, does not. *(237 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Saturation and dew point defined **4** · absolute-versus-relative discipline
**4** · dawn-maximum reasoning **4** · cloudy-night dew case **3**

| Wet-bulb temperature as a physiological survivability threshold; heat-stress limits | `Disaster-Management/basic/09_Drought-Heat-Waves-and-Slow-Onset-Risk` |

### The intuition, before any term

A cloud is not a thing in the sky. **A cloud is a place where air has been lifted far enough to cool
to its dew point.** The water was already there, invisible; the lifting made it visible. Stop the
lifting and the cloud evaporates where it stands.

Which means a cloud's **shape** is a record of **how** the air got lifted, and its **height** is a
record of **how far**. That is why cloud classification is useful rather than decorative: a
meteorologist reading a sky is reading the **lifting mechanism**. Flat layered cloud means gentle,
widespread, slow ascent — a front sliding over, which is Lesson 14. Lumpy heaped cloud means
vigorous local convection — a thermal rising, which is Lesson 17. Wispy fibrous cloud at great
height means ice crystals in air so cold that no liquid survives.

Two Latin words carry almost all of it. **Stratus** means layer. **Cumulus** means heap. Add
**cirrus** for a curl of hair and **nimbus** for rain, and the ten-genus system is four roots in
combination.

**Caption.** Ten genera in four bands, one genus spanning all of them, and a margin in which the
**same cloud property — height — produces opposite signs of radiative effect.**

### ✅ D-13 — the ten genera, as Leong prints them

✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.109**, gives the complete list, classified — in his own words — "**by form, by
height and by appearance**":

**Cirrus (Ci) · Cirrostratus · Cirrocumulus · Altostratus · Altocumulus · Stratocumulus ·
Nimbostratus · Cumulus · Cumulonimbus · Stratus (St).**

✅ **Leong, scan PDF p.106**, adds the band: "**High Clouds: mainly cirrus (Ci) of feathery form at
20-40,000 feet above ground**" — ≈ **6,000-12,000 m** (N-36).

⚠️ **The naming grammar, which turns ten names into four roots.**

| Root | Meaning | Tells you |
|---|---|---|
| **Cirro-** / **Cirrus** | a curl of hair | **High**, and therefore made of **ice crystals** |
| **Alto-** | high (confusingly, it marks the **middle** band in cloud naming) | **Middle** level |
| **Strato-** / **Stratus** | layer | **Layered** form — so **gentle, widespread ascent** |
| **Cumulo-** / **Cumulus** | heap | **Heaped** form — so **convective, localised ascent** |
| **Nimbo-** / **-nimbus** | rain | It is **precipitating** |

⚠️ So **nimbostratus** reads as *raining layer cloud* — continuous, widespread, frontal rain — and
**cumulonimbus** reads as *raining heap cloud* — intense, localised, convective rain. **The two names
encode the two rainfall mechanisms of Lesson 13**, and a candidate who reads them that way never
confuses a front with a thunderstorm again.

### The owned 2022 demand — high against low, on mechanism

**P3 — 2022 Prelims Q81** asks about the **differential effect of high and low clouds on the Earth's
temperature**. The answer is entirely in the radiative margin above, and it rests on **one
variable**.

```text
   WHY HEIGHT DECIDES THE SIGN — THE ARGUMENT IN FOUR STEPS

   [1] EVERY CLOUD DOES TWO OPPOSING THINGS AT ONCE.
       It REFLECTS incoming SHORTWAVE (a cooling term — Lesson 2's albedo).
       It ABSORBS and re-radiates outgoing LONGWAVE (a warming term).
       THE NET EFFECT IS THE BALANCE OF THE TWO, AND IT CAN GO EITHER WAY.

   [2] THE SHORTWAVE TERM DEPENDS ON THE CLOUD'S OPTICAL THICKNESS.
       A thick, dense, water-droplet cloud reflects a great deal.
       A thin, wispy, ice-crystal cloud reflects relatively little.

   [3] THE LONGWAVE TERM DEPENDS ON THE CLOUD-TOP TEMPERATURE — AND THAT
       IS WHERE HEIGHT ENTERS. Temperature falls with height (Lesson 4),
       so A HIGH CLOUD HAS A VERY COLD TOP. A cold body radiates feebly.
       THEREFORE A HIGH CLOUD ABSORBS THE WARM SURFACE'S LONGWAVE AND
       RE-EMITS TO SPACE ONLY FEEBLY — IT IS A GOOD TRAP.
       A LOW CLOUD HAS A TOP NEARLY AS WARM AS THE SURFACE, SO IT RE-EMITS
       TO SPACE ALMOST AS EFFICIENTLY AS THE SURFACE WOULD — IT TRAPS LITTLE.

   [4] COMBINE THEM.
       HIGH, THIN, ICY  → weak reflection + strong trapping  → NET WARMING
       LOW,  THICK, WET → strong reflection + weak trapping  → NET COOLING

   ⇒ THE ONE-SENTENCE ANSWER: a high cloud warms because its top is cold;
     a low cloud cools because its top is warm. THE SIGN IS SET BY
     CLOUD-TOP TEMPERATURE, AND CLOUD-TOP TEMPERATURE IS SET BY HEIGHT.
   ⚠️ NOTE WHAT IS *NOT* CLAIMED: nothing here states that cloud cover,
     of any kind, is increasing or decreasing. Cloud feedback as a
     CLIMATE-CHANGE question is routed to Environment (Lesson 2).
```

⚠️ **The answer-neutrality position.** **P3's key is not available locally and none is inferred.** The
mechanism above is the **capability** the demand tests; **no option letter and no elimination chain
is written.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the most dangerous cloud in India

```text
   THE PRE-MONSOON CUMULONIMBUS OF THE EASTERN AND NORTH-EASTERN PLAINS

   WHY THIS CLOUD AND NOT ANOTHER — four ingredients, all from earlier lessons:

   [a] A HIGH TROPOPAUSE over tropical and subtropical India (Lesson 4,
       ~16 km at the equator falling to ~11 km in mid-latitudes). THE LID IS
       HIGH, SO THE TOWER CAN BE DEEP.
   [b] INTENSE SURFACE HEATING in the pre-monsoon months — ✅ Khullar's heat
       march puts the maximum over the northern plains in May and June
       (printed 135). STRONG BUOYANCY AT THE BASE.
   [c] A MOISTURE SUPPLY from the Bay of Bengal reaching up the Gangetic and
       Brahmaputra plains, so the rising air is WET, and condensation
       releases LATENT HEAT that feeds further ascent (Lesson 2, stage [11]).
   [d] AN UPPER-LEVEL FLOW to provide shear and organisation (Lesson 18).

   ⇒ THE RESULT IS A CLOUD WHOSE BASE IS NEAR THE GROUND AND WHOSE TOP IS AT
     THE TROPOPAUSE — a single convective cell occupying almost the entire
     depth of the weather layer, with an ANVIL spreading at the lid because
     ✅ it cannot continue into the stable stratosphere.

   ⇒ ITS HAZARD SET IS THE WHOLE OF LESSON 17 IN ONE OBJECT:
     intense short-duration rainfall · lightning · hail · damaging squalls ·
     and, where the geometry allows, the rotating updraught of a supercell.
     THE PRE-MONSOON SQUALL OF THE EASTERN PLAINS — THE KALBAISAKHI OR
     NOR'WESTER — IS BUILT ON EXACTLY THIS CLOUD.
     ⚠️ U-09: NO OFFICIAL INDIAN CLIMATOLOGY FOR THESE EVENTS WAS RETRIEVED.
        NO COUNT, NO RATING, NO DATE AND NO CASUALTY FIGURE IS STATED
        ANYWHERE IN THIS FILE. The association is kept QUALITATIVE.

   ⇒ AND THE CONTRAST THAT MAKES IT A CLOUD LESSON RATHER THAN A STORM
     LESSON: the SAME quantity of water delivered by NIMBOSTRATUS over
     twelve hours is a soaking; delivered by CUMULONIMBUS over forty minutes
     it is a flood. THE GENUS IS A STATEMENT ABOUT RATE, AND RATE IS WHAT
     TURNS RAINFALL INTO A HAZARD. Lesson 17's cloudburst is this sentence
     taken to its limit.
```

↪ **Route-out.** ⚠️ **Cloud seeding and weather modification as a policy instrument** — its
governance, cost and efficacy — belong to `Environment-and-Ecology` and are **stated here once and
dropped**. This lesson owns the cloud; it does not own the attempt to manage it.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — layer against heap, which is the whole classification in two rows.**

| | **Stratiform (layer) cloud** | **Cumuliform (heap) cloud** |
|---|---|---|
| **Lifting** | Gentle, widespread, **slow** — air sliding up a frontal surface | Vigorous, localised, **fast** — buoyant thermals |
| **Appearance** | Flat, featureless, extensive sheets | Lumpy, sharply outlined, with a flat base and a domed top |
| **Vertical extent** | Shallow relative to its width | Deep relative to its width |
| **Precipitation** | **Continuous, light to moderate, long-lasting** — nimbostratus | **Intense, showery, short-lived, localised** — cumulonimbus |
| **Which lesson** | **14** (fronts) and **20** (western disturbances) | **13** (convectional rain) and **17** (severe convection) |

**Objection.** "Ten cloud names is rote learning. No Mains answer needs a genus list."

**Reply.** No Mains answer needs the list; several need the **inference**. A cloud genus is a
statement about the **lifting mechanism** and the **rainfall rate**, and both are analytical. When
Lesson 20 says that a western disturbance's precipitation over the plains is widespread and moderate
while a pre-monsoon squall's is intense and local, the **cloud genus is the evidence for the
difference**. And ✅ Leong's own apparatus — the **okta**, the **isoneph**, the **isohel** — is exactly
the measurement register that Lesson 1 established and that ✗ C1 omits entirely.

**Unresolved residual.** ⚠️ A scan caution rather than a content gap: **Leong's scan PDF p.106 is
duplicated at p.108**, just as p.105 is duplicated at p.107 (H9). The cloud-plate page therefore
appears **twice in the scan and is one page**. The duplication is a **read finding from the renders,
not a hash finding** (U-12), and **no claim is made that any Chapter 13 page is missing** (U-13).

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating an okta as a cloud type.** It is a **unit of cloud amount** — one eighth of the sky
  (✅ Leong, scan PDF p.105).
- ❌ **Confusing isoneph with isohel.** **Isoneph = equal cloudiness; isohel = equal sunshine.** Both
  are in ✅ Majid's Table 3.2.
- ❌ **Reading "alto-" as high.** In cloud naming it marks the **middle** band.
- ❌ **Saying all clouds cool the Earth.** **High, thin, icy clouds warm; low, thick clouds cool**, and
  the sign is set by **cloud-top temperature** (P3).
- ❌ **Explaining the high-cloud warming by "it traps heat".** So does a low cloud. The difference is
  that a high cloud's **cold top re-emits feebly to space**.
- ❌ **Giving four cloud groups and calling it the classification.** ✅ Leong prints **ten genera** (✗
  C1 gives four rows, D-13).
- ❌ **Treating nimbostratus and cumulonimbus as interchangeable rain clouds.** One is continuous and
  frontal; the other is intense and convective. **The names say so.**
- ❌ **Stating a frequency or a trend for Indian pre-monsoon storms.** **U-09:** no official Indian
  climatology was retrieved; the association is kept qualitative.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Cloud is a **salient feature of world's physical geography** and the visible
intermediate between Lesson 11's moisture and Lesson 13's precipitation; the cumulonimbus is also the
structural object behind the geophysical-phenomena clause's convective extremes.

**Owned demand served — one.**

**P3 — 2022 Prelims Q81, high and low clouds and their differential effects on the Earth's
temperature.** The capability tested is the **radiative mechanism**, not a cloud list. ⚠️ **The key is
not available locally and none is inferred. No option letter and no elimination chain appears.**

**Answer use.** Name the **genus** whenever an answer describes rainfall, and let it carry the
mechanism: *"nimbostratus delivers continuous moderate rain from gentle frontal ascent; cumulonimbus
delivers intense localised rain from deep convection, and the difference in rate is what converts
rainfall into a hazard."* One sentence, two mechanisms, and the bridge to Lessons 13, 14, 17 and 20.

### Revision points

1. **A cloud is a place where air has been lifted to its dew point.** Its **form** records **how** it
   was lifted; its **height** records **how far**.
2. ✅ **The ten genera (Leong, scan PDF p.109):** Cirrus (Ci) · Cirrostratus · Cirrocumulus ·
   Altostratus · Altocumulus · Stratocumulus · Nimbostratus · Cumulus · Cumulonimbus · Stratus (St).
   Classified "**by form, by height and by appearance**".
3. ✅ **High clouds: "mainly cirrus (Ci) of feathery form at 20-40,000 feet above ground"** —
   ≈ **6,000-12,000 m** (Leong scan PDF p.106; N-36).
4. **Four roots, ten names:** **cirro-** (high, icy) · **alto-** (middle) · **strato-** (layer, gentle
   ascent) · **cumulo-** (heap, convective ascent) · **nimbo-/-nimbus** (precipitating).
5. **Nimbostratus = raining layer = continuous frontal rain.** **Cumulonimbus = raining heap =
   intense convective rain.** The names encode Lesson 13's mechanisms.
6. **Cumulonimbus is the only genus spanning all three height bands**, base near the ground and top
   at the tropopause, with an **anvil** where the lid stops it.
7. ✅ **Cloud amount is recorded in oktas — eighths of the sky** (Leong scan PDF p.105). An okta is a
   **unit**.
8. ✅ **Isoneph** = equal **cloudiness**; ✅ **isohel** = equal **sunshine**. Both in Majid's Table 3.2.
9. **Every cloud reflects shortwave (cooling) and traps longwave (warming).** The **net sign** is the
   balance.
10. **HIGH, thin, icy → weak reflection + strong trapping → NET WARMING.** **LOW, thick, wet →
    strong reflection + weak trapping → NET COOLING.**
11. **The sign is set by cloud-top temperature, which is set by height.** A high cloud's cold top
    **re-emits feebly to space**; a low cloud's warm top re-emits almost as efficiently as the
    surface.
12. ✗ **D-13: C1's cloud table has four rows — no genus list, no height band, no unit, no isoline.**
13. 🇮🇳 **The pre-monsoon cumulonimbus of the eastern plains** assembles a high lid, intense heating,
    a Bay moisture supply and upper-level flow, and carries the whole of Lesson 17's hazard set.
    ⚠️ **U-09: no Indian count, rating, date or casualty figure is stated.**
14. **Genus is a statement about RATE.** The same water as nimbostratus over twelve hours is a
    soaking; as cumulonimbus over forty minutes it is a flood.
15. ⚠️ **Scan caution: Leong p.106 is duplicated at p.108 and p.105 at p.107** — a read finding from
    renders, not a hash finding (U-12), with **no claim that any page is missing** (U-13).

### Concept check

**Question:** "Clouds cool the Earth by reflecting sunlight, so more cloud always means a cooler
surface." Evaluate, and state what actually determines the sign of a cloud's effect.

**Model answer:** The statement describes **one of two opposing terms** and treats it as the whole.
Every cloud does two things simultaneously. It **reflects incoming shortwave radiation**, which cools
— a high-albedo term from Lesson 2. And it **absorbs the outgoing longwave radiation emitted by the
surface and re-radiates part of it downward**, which warms. The **net** effect is the balance, and
the balance can fall either way. **Height decides it, through cloud-top temperature.** Since
temperature falls with height through the troposphere at about 6.5 °C per kilometre, a **high** cloud
— ✅ Leong places high cloud, "mainly cirrus of feathery form", at **20-40,000 feet**, about
**6,000-12,000 m** — has a very **cold** top, and a cold body radiates feebly. Such a cloud is also
thin and composed of ice crystals, so it reflects relatively little. **Weak reflection plus strong
trapping gives net warming.** A **low** cloud such as **stratocumulus** or **nimbostratus** is thick
and made of water droplets, so it reflects strongly; but its top is nearly as warm as the surface, so
it re-emits to space almost as efficiently as the surface would and traps little. **Strong reflection
plus weak trapping gives net cooling.** The correct generalisation is therefore that **a high cloud
warms because its top is cold and a low cloud cools because its top is warm** — and any claim about
changes in total cloud cover belongs to the Environment owner, not here.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a cloud's effect can be read from its brightness. Brightness
reports only the shortwave term. The longwave term is invisible to the eye and is decided by the
cloud's **altitude**, which is why two clouds of similar appearance at different heights can have
opposite signs.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 12

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Explain how cloud classification by form and height
encodes both the mechanism of uplift and the character of the resulting precipitation.

**Model:** ✅ Leong classifies cloud "**by form, by height and by appearance**", listing ten genera.
**Form encodes the uplift.** *Strato-* means layer, and layered cloud forms where ascent is
**gentle, slow and widespread** — air sliding up a frontal surface. *Cumulo-* means heap, and heaped
cloud forms where ascent is **vigorous, local and buoyant**. **Height encodes the depth of lifting
and the phase of the water**: ✅ high clouds, "mainly **cirrus** of feathery form", lie at **20-40,000
feet** and are **ice**; middle **alto-** and low **strato-** cloud are progressively warmer and
wetter.

Combine the roots and the precipitation follows. **Nimbostratus** — raining layer — gives
**continuous, moderate, long-lasting** rain. **Cumulonimbus** — raining heap — spans all three bands,
reaching the tropopause and spreading an anvil, and gives **intense, short, localised** rain with
hail and lightning. The same water delivered by each is a soaking or a flood. **Genus is a statement
about rate.** *(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Ten genera by form and height **4** · okta and isoneph used **2** ·
high-versus-low radiative contrast **3** · cumulonimbus India case **1**

Progress: 13 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **three-mechanism rainfall triptych with an orographic traverse**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 12 ended with a cloud. A cloud is not rain. A cloud is millions of droplets so small that the
air's own motion holds them up indefinitely — which is why clouds sit in the sky for hours without
falling.

To make rain, two further things must happen. The droplets must **grow** until they are heavy enough
to fall faster than the air rises. And the air must **keep rising**, because the moment ascent stops,
condensation stops and the cloud begins to evaporate.

So the real question is never "how does rain form?" It is **"what is lifting the air, and for how
long?"** There are exactly three answers to that in physical geography, and they give three
completely different kinds of rain. One is violent and brief. One is steady and prolonged. One is
anchored to a place and never moves at all.

**That third one — orographic — is the one that makes geography a subject.** Rainfall caused by heat
or by a front can happen anywhere. Rainfall caused by a mountain happens **on the same slope every
year**, which is why the wettest places on Earth have addresses.

**Caption.** Three mechanisms sharing a baseline, with the orographic panel expanded into the
traverse that produces the Foehn and the rain shadow as two readings of a single event.

### The mountain-alignment argument — the owned 2021 demand's core

**O1 — 2021 GS-I Q14** asks for "the alignment of major mountain ranges of the world and **explain
their impact on local weather**", with the directive **briefly mention and explain**. That directive
has two halves and a candidate must do both: an **inventory**, briefly; and a **consequence**,
explained.

**The analytical key is a single variable: does the range lie ACROSS the prevailing flow, or ALONG
it?**


### ✗ N-30 — "wet day" and "rainy day" are different thresholds from different agencies

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.52 (scan PDF p.125), glossary:** "**A wet-day: when at least 1 mm of
rainfall is recorded.**"

⚠️ **IMD's rainy day is ≥ 2.5 mm.**

**✗ N-30, applied. State both with their agencies; do not merge them.** And note the gap in
provenance honestly: **U-01 records that `mausam.imd.gov.in/responsive/glossary.php` returned HTTP
404 at audit and again at generation**, so the **2.5 mm figure has no first-party anchor in this pass
and is not tagged ✅**. It is carried as a widely used operational threshold whose first-party page
was not retrievable at the generation date.

⚠️ **Why the distinction earns marks.** A rainfall climatology is a count of **days above a
threshold**, so **changing the threshold changes the climatology without changing the weather** —
Lesson 1's theme again. Two stations counted on different thresholds cannot be compared. This is why
Lesson 20 insists that every IMD criterion carries its number.

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the wettest slopes on Earth, and the plateau behind them

```text
   THREE INDIAN OROGRAPHIC CASES, EACH MAKING A DIFFERENT POINT

   [A] THE WESTERN GHATS — THE CLEANEST TEXTBOOK CASE
       A nearly continuous N-S scarp standing ACROSS the summer south-westerly
       flow. Air arriving off the Arabian Sea is forced up the western face.
       → WINDWARD: among the heaviest seasonal rainfall in the country.
       → LEEWARD: the Deccan interior, in a RAIN SHADOW, markedly drier at the
         SAME LATITUDE and only a short distance away.
       ⇒ THE DISCRIMINATION: latitude is identical, distance is small, and the
         difference is an order of magnitude. ONLY ALIGNMENT EXPLAINS IT.
       ↪ THE MONSOON FLOW ITSELF IS Geography/advanced/16's. THIS FILE USES
         ONLY THE FACT THAT A MOIST FLOW ARRIVES; IT DOES NOT EXPLAIN WHY.

   [B] THE SHILLONG PLATEAU — WHY FUNNELLING BEATS HEIGHT
       MAWSYNRAM and CHERRAPUNJI sit on the southern edge of a plateau that is
       NOT especially high by Himalayan standards. Yet they are among the
       wettest recorded places on Earth. WHY?
       ⇒ NOT height alone. THREE GEOMETRIES COMPOUND:
         (i) the plateau's southern face is an ABRUPT SCARP rising directly
             from a very low, very warm, very humid plain — so the forced
             ascent is SUDDEN rather than gradual;
         (ii) the terrain FUNNELS the approaching flow into a narrowing
             valley system, CONCENTRATING the moisture flux into a small area
             before lifting it;
         (iii) the flow arrives from a nearby sea with a long over-water fetch.
       ⇒ THE LESSON: OROGRAPHIC RAINFALL IS A PRODUCT OF MOISTURE FLUX ×
         FORCED ASCENT RATE, NOT OF ALTITUDE. A low scarp in the right place
         beats a high range in the wrong one.
       ⚠️ NO RAINFALL FIGURE IS STATED FOR EITHER STATION. Neither local book
         in this evidence base supplies one for them and no official source was
         retrieved this pass. THE MECHANISM IS TAUGHT; THE NUMBER IS NOT
         INVENTED.

   [C] THE HIMALAYA — A BARRIER FIRST AND A RAIN MAKER SECOND
       Running W-E, the Himalaya lies ALONG the winter westerly flow and
       ACROSS the summer southerly one. It therefore does BOTH jobs, in
       different seasons — which is why it appears in BOTH columns of the
       alignment test.
       → As a BARRIER it separates two entirely different climatic regimes
         and, as Lesson 19 shows, SPLITS AN ENTIRE JET STREAM.
       → As a LIFTING AGENT in summer it wrings out the southerly flow on its
         southern face.
       ⇒ AND IN WINTER IT IS WHERE LESSON 20'S WESTERN DISTURBANCES DELIVER
         THEIR PRECIPITATION AS SNOW: ✅ Khullar, printed folio 134 (folio not
         read, U-06), gives the December-February gradient as "about 60 cm in
         the Himalayan region, 12 cm in Punjab, 5.3 cm at Delhi" — A DECAY
         CURVE AWAY FROM THE MOUNTAIN. Lesson 20 develops it.
```

↪ **Three route-outs, all mandatory.** ⚠️ **The south-west monsoon's rainfall distribution over
India** → `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism`. ⚠️ **The north-east monsoon and Tamil
Nadu's October-December rainfall** → `Geography/basic/16_Tropical-Monsoon-and-Marine-Climate`; ✅
Khullar's "highest seasonal rainfall of about **75 cm between October and December** … along the
south-eastern coast of Tamil Nadu and adjoining parts of Andhra Pradesh" (printed 135) is **stated
once here as a boundary marker and then routed**, not developed. ⚠️ **Rainfall as a weathering and
erosion agent**, including rainwater composition → `Geography/basic/04_Weathering-MassMovement-
Groundwater`, to which the ledger routes the 2024 Prelims Q7 demand by name.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — the fourth mechanism that is not on the triptych.**

⚠️ **Convergence**. Where air flows together horizontally with nowhere to go but up, ascent occurs
without heating, without relief and without a density contrast. It is the mechanism of the equatorial
low (Lesson 8) and of the inflow into a tropical cyclone (Lesson 15). **It is listed here as a
completeness note**: the classical three are the examinable set, but a candidate who writes "three
and only three" has forgotten the one that drives the largest rainfall system on the planet.

**Objection.** "Rain shadow is obvious. A mountain blocks the rain."

**Reply.** A mountain blocks nothing. **Air crosses it.** What changes is the air's **state**: it
arrives at the lee foot having lost moisture on the way up and having been warmed by compression on
the way down (Lesson 10). The lee is dry because the air is **drier and warmer**, not because
anything was intercepted. The distinction matters because it predicts things "blocking" does not —
that the lee is also **hotter**, that the relative humidity there **collapses**, and that the
rainfall maximum on the windward side lies **below the crest** rather than at it.

**Unresolved residual.** **U-01 stands**: the IMD glossary page is still 404, so the rainy-day
threshold has no first-party anchor and is not tagged ✅. And **no rainfall figure is stated for
Mawsynram or Cherrapunji**, because neither local book supplies one and no official source was
retrieved — the mechanism is taught and the number is refused.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Saying a mountain "blocks" rain.** Air crosses it; the lee is dry because the air is
  **depleted and compressively warmed**, not intercepted.
- ❌ **Putting the rainfall maximum at the crest.** It usually lies **below** the crest, because the
  upper slope receives air already stripped of moisture.
- ❌ **Using one lapse rate for the whole traverse.** **Dry below the condensation level, saturated
  above it on the way up, dry again all the way down** (Lesson 4).
- ❌ **Treating the Foehn and the rain shadow as two phenomena.** They are **one traverse read for
  temperature and for moisture**.
- ❌ **Merging the wet day and the rainy day.** ✅ Majid: a wet day is "**at least 1 mm**"; IMD's rainy
  day is **≥ 2.5 mm** — ⚠️ with **no first-party anchor retrieved** (N-30, U-01).
- ❌ **Explaining Mawsynram by altitude.** It is **funnelling, an abrupt scarp and a short sea fetch**
  — moisture flux times forced ascent rate, not height.
- ❌ **Stating a rainfall figure for Mawsynram or Cherrapunji.** None is in this evidence base and
  none is invented.
- ❌ **Explaining the monsoon while explaining monsoon rainfall.** The flow's existence is Topic 16's;
  this lesson owns only what a mountain does to a flow that has arrived.
- ❌ **Claiming to have answered the whole 2021 stem in one lesson.** O1 is split across Lessons 13,
  10 and 19.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Precipitation is a **salient feature of world's physical geography**, and the alignment
argument is the clearest case in the whole topic of physical geography producing a **spatial**
result — which is what the clause is for.

**Owned demand served — one, anchored here.**

**O1 — 2021 GS-I Q14.** *"Briefly mention the alignment of major mountain ranges of the world and
explain their impact on local weather."* **Briefly mention and explain · 15 marks · 250 words.**

**Construction required.** The directive is a **compound**: a brief **inventory** followed by an
**explained consequence**. The inventory must be genuinely brief — a list that consumes half the
answer has failed the "briefly" and starved the "explain". The explanation must rest on **one
organising variable**, and that variable is **alignment relative to the prevailing flow**. This
construction — *short inventory, then a single generalising mechanism applied back across the
inventory* — is shared with none of the other six owned models in this file. ⚠️ **C1 §11 supplies no
spine for this demand; the spine above is built by this edition as an obligation.**

**Model answer (15 marks, 250 words).**

The world's major ranges fall into two alignment classes, and the class decides the weather. The
**Rockies** and the **Andes** run **north-south**; the **Himalaya** and the **Alps** run broadly
**west-east**; the **Western Ghats** and Australia's **Great Dividing Range** run north-south along
their coasts; the **Scandinavian Highlands** run north-east to south-west.

The organising variable is whether a range lies **across** the prevailing flow or **along** it.

A range **across** the flow forces ascent. The parcel cools at the **dry adiabatic rate** to its
condensation level and at the slower **saturated rate** above it, precipitating on the windward
slope — with the maximum usually **below the crest**, since the upper slope receives already-depleted
air. Descending the lee, it warms at the **dry rate**, so the leeward foot is **hotter and drier**
than the windward foot. ✅ Majid records the consequence as "a narrow zone about **300 km east of the
crest of the Rockies**" where the **Chinook** blows, and ✅ Leong lists **Atacama** and the **Great
Australian** deserts among trade-wind deserts in such lee positions. The **Western Ghats** reproduce
it exactly: a drenched western scarp and a dry Deccan at the same latitude.

A range **along** the flow lifts little and instead acts as a **barrier and climatic divide** — the
Himalaya's role in winter, when it separates two regimes and, as it does so, splits the subtropical
jet itself. *(233 words)*

⚠️ **This model is repeated in the final arc**, where all seven owned Mains models are assembled and
their seven distinct constructions are set side by side.

### Revision points

1. **Cloud is not rain.** Rain needs droplet **growth** and **sustained ascent**; stop the ascent and
   the cloud evaporates.
2. **Three lifting mechanisms:** **convectional** (buoyancy) · **orographic** (relief) · **frontal**
   (density contrast). ⚠️ A fourth, **convergence**, drives the equatorial low and the cyclone inflow.
3. **Only orographic rainfall is anchored to a place**, which is why the wettest places on Earth have
   addresses.
4. **The orographic traverse, with all three rates:** **dry** ascent to the condensation level →
   **saturated** ascent above it, precipitating → **dry** descent on the lee, warming and drying.
5. **The rainfall maximum usually lies BELOW the crest**, because the upper slope receives depleted
   air.
6. **The Foehn and the rain shadow are one event**: the same traverse read for **temperature** and
   for **moisture**.
7. ✅ **Leong Fig. 113 (scan PDF p.115)** draws "orographic or relief rain"; ✅ **Majid's glossary
   (printed 3.52)** defines **orographic lifting** and **orographic precipitation** separately.
8. **The alignment test:** a range **across** the flow is a **rain maker and a Foehn maker**; a range
   **along** the flow is a **barrier and a climatic divide**.
9. **Worked inventory:** Rockies N-S across the westerlies → ✅ the Chinook belt "**300 km east of the
   crest**"; Andes N-S → ✅ **Atacama**; Himalaya W-E → barrier in winter, rain maker in summer; Alps
   → ✅ Foehn valleys; Western Ghats N-S → wet scarp, dry Deccan; Great Dividing Range → ✅ **Great
   Australian** deserts inland.
10. ✗ **N-30: ✅ Majid's "wet-day: when at least 1 mm of rainfall is recorded" (printed 3.52) against
    IMD's rainy day at ≥ 2.5 mm** — ⚠️ **the latter has no first-party anchor this pass (U-01)**.
    State both with their agencies; do not merge.
11. **A rainfall climatology is a count of days above a threshold**, so changing the threshold
    changes the climatology without changing the weather.
12. 🇮🇳 **Western Ghats:** identical latitude, short distance, order-of-magnitude difference — only
    alignment explains it.
13. 🇮🇳 **Mawsynram and Cherrapunji are explained by FUNNELLING, an abrupt scarp and a short sea
    fetch — moisture flux × forced ascent rate — not by altitude.** ⚠️ **No rainfall figure is stated
    for either.**
14. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Khullar's December-February gradient: "about 60 cm in the Himalayan region, 12 cm in
    Punjab, 5.3 cm at Delhi"** (printed folio 134, folio not read, U-06) — a decay curve away from
    the mountain, developed in Lesson 20.
15. ⇄ **O1 is a split demand.** **This lesson is primary**; Lesson 10 carries the Foehn consequence;
    Lesson 19 carries the barrier-and-jet consequence. **No lesson claims the whole stem.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Two stations lie at the same latitude, 120 km apart, on opposite sides of a north-south
range standing across a moist westerly flow. One receives very heavy seasonal rain; the other is
semi-arid and noticeably hotter. Explain, and state where on the wet side the maximum will be found.

**Model answer:** The difference is produced entirely by **forced ascent and forced descent**, since
latitude, season and air mass are common to both. On the **windward** side the range compels the
moist flow upward. The parcel cools at the **dry adiabatic rate** until it reaches its **dew point**
at the **condensation level**, then continues upward cooling at the slower **saturated adiabatic
rate**, because condensation releases latent heat. Sustained ascent keeps condensation going, so
droplets grow and **precipitation is heavy and prolonged** — ✅ Leong's Fig. 113, "orographic or
relief rain" (scan PDF p.115). On the **leeward** side the same air is forced down. It is now
**depleted of moisture**, and it warms at the faster **dry adiabatic rate** — ✅ Majid gives
**10 °C/kilometre** (printed 3.24) — so its temperature rises and its relative humidity collapses.
Cloud evaporates and a **rain shadow** results. **The lee is therefore both drier and hotter**, which
is why ✅ the Chinook blows "a narrow zone about **300 km east of the crest of the Rockies**" and why
✅ the **Atacama** and the **Great Australian** deserts sit in such positions. The **maximum lies
below the crest, not at it**: by the upper slope the air has already surrendered most of its
moisture, so the heaviest totals occur on the **middle windward slope**. Nothing was blocked; the
air's **state** was changed in transit.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a rain shadow is an interception effect. The air crosses the range
in full; what has altered is its moisture content and its temperature — which is why the lee is
**hotter** as well as drier, a prediction the "blocking" account cannot make.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 13

**Original question (20 marks, 250 words).** Analyse the three mechanisms of precipitation, and
explain why only one of them produces a rainfall pattern that is fixed in place.

**Model:** Rain requires **sustained ascent** and droplet growth; stop the ascent and the cloud
evaporates. Three mechanisms supply the ascent. **Convectional** lifting is driven by **buoyancy**: a
heated surface makes air less dense and it rises of its own accord, building cumulonimbus and giving
intense, brief, localised rain. **Frontal** lifting is driven by a **density contrast**: warm, less
dense air is undercut by or rides over colder air, giving moderate, prolonged, widespread rain from
stratiform cloud. **Orographic** lifting is driven by **relief**: a barrier across the flow compels
ascent whether the air is buoyant or not.

Only the third is anchored. The first follows the heating and the second follows the front; both
move. A mountain does not. ✅ Leong's Fig. 113 (scan PDF p.115) draws "orographic or relief rain", and
the derivation uses all three lapse rates of Lesson 4: dry ascent to the condensation level,
**saturated** ascent above it while precipitating, then ✅ dry descent at **10 °C/kilometre**, leaving
the lee foot **hotter and drier**. The windward maximum lies **below the crest**, since the upper
slope receives already-depleted air.

Hence addresses. The **Western Ghats** are drenched on their western scarp and the Deccan is dry at
the same latitude; the **Shillong plateau's** abrupt scarp and funnelled approach make Mawsynram and
Cherrapunji exceptional through **moisture flux × ascent rate**, not altitude. ⚠️ A fourth mechanism,
**convergence**, drives the equatorial low. *(235 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Three rainfall mechanisms separated **6** · orographic derivation with both
lapse rates **5** · rain-shadow geography **5** · mountain-alignment inventory **3** · India worked
case **1**

## Lesson 14 — Air masses and fronts: the temperate cyclone machine
