Progress: 18 / 20 · Stage: Advanced · Primary visual grammar: **global jet-ribbon schematic with a definitional-exclusion margin**

### The intuition, before any term

Everything so far has happened near the ground or in a vertical column. Now go to the top of the
troposphere and look sideways.

What is there is a **river**. A narrow, flattened ribbon of air, a few hundred kilometres wide, a
couple of kilometres thick, thousands of kilometres long, moving at speeds no surface wind
approaches. It is not a current in a wider flow; it is a **concentration** — a core of very fast air
embedded in slower air on either side.

**Why does the atmosphere concentrate its fastest winds into ribbons?** Because of Lesson 3 and
Lesson 7 acting together. Where a **horizontal temperature gradient** is steep — at the boundary
between polar and tropical air — the pressure difference between the two sides **grows with
height**, since a cold column shrinks faster than a warm one. By the tropopause that accumulated
difference is enormous, and the geostrophic wind it drives is correspondingly fast. **A jet sits
where a thermal gradient is steepest, at the height where the accumulated pressure difference is
greatest.**

Which gives the single most useful sentence in this lesson: **a jet is a thermal gradient made
visible as a wind.** Find the gradient and you have found the jet.

### The three extratropical jets, distinguished

| Jet | Where | Why it is there | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Polar front jet (PFJ)** | Above the **polar front** of Lesson 8, around 40-60°, at roughly 9-11 km | The steepest **surface** thermal gradient on the planet, between polar and tropical air | Strongly **meandering**; its waves are the Rossby waves of Lesson 7; it **steers** the temperate cyclones of Lesson 14 |
| **Subtropical westerly jet (STWJ)** | At the **tropopause break**, around 25-35°, at roughly 12-14 km — **higher and more poleward-stable than the PFJ** | The poleward limb of the Hadley cell conserving angular momentum as it moves poleward, plus the thermal contrast at the tropopause break | **Steadier** and less wavy than the PFJ; strongest in **winter**. **This is the jet that matters for India** (Lesson 19) |
| **Arctic / Antarctic jets** | High latitudes | ✅ Named in Majid's three-type list | Seasonal and polar-night features |

⚠️ **And the fourth, which the definition excludes: the tropical easterly jet (TEJ)**, around 15°N,
flowing **east to west**. ✅ Majid's *Geography of India* printed 4.8 explains it; ✅ his *Indian and
World Geography* printed 3.35 defines it out of existence. **Its mechanism is Lesson 19's**; this
lesson's business is the **definitional gap**.

```text
   THE CAPABILITY TESTED — hemispheric symmetry of the jet family:
   [1] JETS ARE NOT A NORTHERN-HEMISPHERE PHENOMENON. ✅ Majid's own list
       includes "the ARCTIC/ANTARCTIC Jet streams" — PLURAL AND BOTH POLES.
   [2] THE MECHANISM IS SYMMETRIC. A jet requires a steep thermal gradient
       and a rotating planet. BOTH HEMISPHERES HAVE BOTH.
   [3] WHAT DIFFERS IS CHARACTER, NOT EXISTENCE:
       • THE SOUTHERN HEMISPHERE'S circumpolar flow is STRONGER AND LESS
         DISTURBED, because there is almost no land between 40°S and 60°S
         to force waves into it (Lesson 8's Roaring Forties, same cause).
       • THE NORTHERN HEMISPHERE'S flow is MORE MERIDIONAL, because large
         mountain ranges and land-sea contrasts force standing waves into it.
   [4] ⇒ A STATEMENT THAT JETS OCCUR IN ONLY ONE HEMISPHERE IS FALSE; A
         STATEMENT THAT THEY BEHAVE IDENTICALLY IN BOTH IS ALSO FALSE.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Objection.** "A jet stream is 12 kilometres above the ground. How can it matter to weather at the
surface?"

**Reply.** Three ways, each developed elsewhere in this file. **It steers.** Surface systems are
carried along the upper flow — ✅ Khullar states it directly for India's western depressions. **It
creates and destroys.** Divergence aloft, where the jet's flow spreads, **draws air upward from
beneath**, deepening a surface low; convergence aloft does the reverse. This is the "dynamically
induced" mechanism of Lesson 8's sub-polar low, now given its agent. **It controls duration.** ✓ C1's
preserved sentence: the damaging extremes are extremes of **duration**, and duration is set by the
wave pattern of the rebuilt diagram above.

**Unresolved residual.** **N-19 is not resolved and is not resolvable by authority.** ✅ Majid's two
books disagree, and this edition's position is that **his world-geography definition is too narrow**
rather than that either book is wrong — because the TEJ passage in the India volume is a correct
description of a real phenomenon and the IWG definition is a correct description of the extratropical
jets. **Both are shown with their page locations and the gap is taught as the finding.** ⚠️
Additionally, **U-04**: scan PDF p.108 carries **no folio in the extracted text**; the printed folio
3.35 is supplied from running-head readings at adjacent pages and is flagged in this lesson's pre-teach checklist and at U-04 in the uncertainty ledger.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Defining a jet as a wind that blows from west to east.** ✅ Majid does, at printed 3.35, and the
  definition **excludes the tropical easterly jet** that the same author explains in his India volume
  (N-19).
- ❌ **Listing three jet types for the world.** ✅ Majid's list — subtropical, polar front, Arctic /
  Antarctic — **contains no easterly jet**.
- ❌ **Saying a jet is fast "because the air is thin up there".** It is fast because the **thermal
  gradient beneath it is steep** and the resulting pressure difference **accumulates with height**.
- ❌ **Forgetting the seasonal speed signal.** ✅ "from a mean of **110 km per hour in summer** to about
  **190 kmph in winter**" — because the **gradient** is steepest in winter.
- ❌ **Confusing the polar front jet with the subtropical westerly jet.** Different latitudes,
  different heights, different steadiness and different jobs.
- ❌ **Confusing a jet with a Rossby wave.** The jet is the **fast core**; the wave is the **meander
  pattern in which it runs** (Lesson 7's correction).
- ❌ **Saying jets exist only in the Northern Hemisphere.** ✅ Majid's own list names the **Arctic /
  Antarctic** jets; the Southern Hemisphere's circumpolar flow is **stronger and less disturbed**.
- ❌ **Claiming to have answered the whole 2020 stem here.** ⇄ **P2 is split**: jet half here, eye half
  in Lesson 15.
- ❌ **Asserting that the flow is becoming more meridional.** Prohibited (U-02); routed in Lesson 19 as
  an open question.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Upper-air circulation is a **salient feature of world's physical geography**, and the
jets are the structures through which every surface system in Lessons 14 to 17 and 20 is steered,
deepened or stalled.

**Owned demand served — one half.**

**⇄ P2a — 2020 Prelims Q99**, jet half. The capability tested is **hemispheric symmetry**. ⚠️ **The key
is not available locally and none is inferred. No option letter and no elimination chain appears.
The eye half is in Lesson 15 and neither lesson claims the whole stem.**

**Answer use.** Open any upper-air answer by **deriving the jet from the gradient** rather than
asserting its existence: *"a jet stream is a narrow, fast core of upper-tropospheric wind that forms
where a horizontal temperature gradient is steep, because the pressure difference across the gradient
accumulates with height."* Then quote ✅ Majid's dimensions. The derivation plus one quantified
sentence is worth more than a paragraph of description.

### Revision points

1. **A jet is a thermal gradient made visible as a wind.** It forms where a horizontal temperature
   gradient is steep, at the height where the accumulated pressure difference is greatest.
2. ✅ **Majid printed 3.35: "a strong wind blowing from west to east in the upper part of the
   troposphere, usually near the level of tropopause (about 12,000 m above the sea level) in sinuous
   band."**
3. ✅ **Dimensions: 160-480 km wide, 900-2,150 m thick.** ✅ **Core speed "can exceed 300 kmph";
   exceptional "375 kmph" recorded.**
4. ✅ **Mean speed "from 110 km per hour in summer to about 190 kmph in winter"** — because the
   **thermal gradient** is steepest in winter.
5. ✅ **Majid's three types: sub-tropical · polar front of the middle latitudes · Arctic/Antarctic.**
   ❗ **None easterly.**
6. ✗ **N-19 / H11: ✅ Majid's IWG definition "blowing from west to east" EXCLUDES the tropical
   easterly jet that ✅ Majid's *Geography of India* printed 4.8 explains.** Same author, two books.
   ✗ **C2 asserts the TEJ as book-grounded without noticing the exclusion.**
7. **The right general definition:** a **narrow, fast, concentrated core of upper-tropospheric wind
   associated with a steep thermal gradient**. Nothing in it requires a westerly sense.
8. **Polar front jet:** above the polar front, ~40-60°, ~9-11 km, strongly **meandering**, steers
   temperate cyclones. **Subtropical westerly jet:** at the **tropopause break**, ~25-35°, ~12-14 km,
   **higher and steadier**, strongest in winter — **India's winter jet**.
9. ✅ **Majid's importance clause: "the position, thickness, dimensions and movements of the jet
   streams" influence global temperature, pressure, circulation, the monsoon and precipitation.**
10. ✗ **N-37: C1 line 537's corrupted diagram is rebuilt** as a two-column **zonal versus
    meridional** comparison: low amplitude = small north-south exchange, fast steering, moderate
    extremes; high amplitude = large exchange, slow steering, **persistent conditions and severe
    extremes**, with ✅ **blocking anticyclones** forming on tall ridges and moving "**very slowly**".
11. ✓ **C1's best sentence, preserved:** "most damaging weather extremes are extremes of **duration**,
    not of instantaneous intensity — and duration is governed by the **upper-air wave pattern**."
12. ⇄ **P2a: jets exist in BOTH hemispheres** — ✅ Majid names the **Arctic/Antarctic** jets. The
    Southern flow is **stronger and less disturbed** (little land, Lesson 8); the Northern is **more
    meridional** (mountains and land-sea contrasts force standing waves).
13. 🇮🇳 **India is governed by TWO jets in OPPOSITE directions**: the **STWJ west-to-east in winter**,
    which ✅ Khullar says steers the western depressions, and the **TEJ east-to-west in summer**,
    ✅ "stationed at **15°N latitude**" after S. Rama Rattan. **Their mechanism is Lesson 19's.**
14. ⚠️ **The jet rule, applied:** ✅ Khullar's and ✅ Majid's jet passages physically sit inside their
    **monsoon chapters**. This edition quotes them and **states at the point of quotation that it is
    quoting from a monsoon section into a weather lesson**.
15. ⚠️ **U-02: no claim is made that the flow is becoming more meridional or that blocking is becoming
    more frequent.**

### Concept check

**Question:** "A jet stream is a strong wind blowing from west to east near the tropopause. There are
three main types." Assess this definition against India's own upper-air geography.

**Model answer:** The definition is ✅ Majid's own, at printed 3.35: "a strong wind blowing **from
west to east** in the upper part of the troposphere, usually near the level of tropopause (**about
12,000 m** above the sea level)", followed by the list "(i) the **sub-tropical** jet stream, (ii) the
**polar front** jet stream of the middle latitudes, and (iii) the **Arctic/Antarctic** Jet streams".
**It is accurate for the extratropical jets and it excludes one of India's two.** ✅ The same author,
in *Geography of India* at printed 4.8, writes that "on the southern side of this upper air
anticyclone, the direction of air flow is **from east to west** … these easterly winds blowing in the
mid-troposphere are known as **tropical easterly jets**". **One object is defined out of existence in
one book and explained in the other.** India is the country where the gap shows, because it is
governed by a **westerly** jet in winter — which ✅ Khullar says "is responsible for **steering of the
western depressions** from the Mediterranean Sea" — and by an **easterly** jet in summer, ✅
"stationed at **15°N latitude**". A general definition must therefore drop the directional clause: a
jet is a **narrow, fast, concentrated core of upper-tropospheric wind associated with a steep thermal
gradient**, which is why ✅ its speed rises "from a mean of **110 km per hour in summer to about 190
kmph in winter**" as the gradient steepens. The three-type list is similarly a list of the
**extratropical** family.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a definition printed in a standard textbook can be applied outside
the region it was written for. The west-to-east clause is not an error about the subtropical or polar
jets; it is a **parochial generalisation**, and in a syllabus where India's summer jet runs the other
way, the generalisation fails exactly where it is needed most.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 18

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine jet streams as a family of upper-air phenomena,
and show how a standard textbook definition of them fails for the tropics.

**Model:** A jet stream is a **narrow, fast, concentrated core of upper-tropospheric wind** that forms
where a **horizontal temperature gradient** is steep: the pressure difference across the gradient
accumulates with height, so the geostrophic wind it drives reaches its maximum near the tropopause.
✅ Majid (printed 3.35) quantifies it — **160-480 km wide**, **900-2,150 m thick**, near the
tropopause at "**about 12,000 m**", with a core that "can **exceed 300 kmph**" and an exceptional
"**375 kmph**" on record; its mean runs "from **110 km per hour in summer** to about **190 kmph in
winter**", because the gradient is steepest in winter.

The **extratropical family** has three members in ✅ his list: the **subtropical**, the **polar
front** of the middle latitudes, and the **Arctic/Antarctic** jets.

The definition, however, opens "a strong wind blowing **from west to east**" — and that clause
**excludes an entire member**. ✅ Majid's own *Geography of India* (printed 4.8) describes air flowing
"**from east to west**" on the southern side of the Tibetan upper anticyclone, "known as **tropical
easterly jets**". The same author, two books, one object defined out of existence in the first. India
exposes it: ✅ Khullar's winter westerly jet "is responsible for **steering of the western
depressions**", while the summer easterly jet is ✅ "stationed at **15°N latitude**". **Drop the
directional clause and the definition holds.** *(230 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Jet defined by thermal gradient **4** · family enumerated including the
easterly **5** · quantification with attribution **3** · definitional exclusion exposed **3**

Progress: 19 / 20 · Stage: Advanced · Primary visual grammar: **Tibetan-barrier bifurcation map, paired by season**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 18 left a ribbon of fast air running west to east near the tropopause. Now put a wall in its
way.

**The Tibetan Plateau is not a mountain range. It is a plateau the size of a subcontinent standing
at roughly five kilometres**, which means it occupies the lower half of the troposphere across
thousands of kilometres of longitude. A jet at 10 or 12 kilometres is not blocked by it — but the
**air beneath and around** the jet is, and the plateau's presence distorts the whole flow field. In
winter the westerly jet **splits**, running one branch north of the plateau and one branch south of
it along the Himalayan foot.

And in summer the plateau does something stranger. Because it is **high**, its surface is heated by
the Sun at an altitude where the surrounding air at the same level is free atmosphere. **It becomes a
heat source in the middle of the troposphere** — a hotplate held up into the sky. Warm air aloft
means high pressure aloft, which means an **anticyclone at height**, and on the southern side of
that anticyclone the air flows **east to west**. That is India's second jet.

**One plateau, two seasons, two jets, in opposite directions.** The plateau is the single most
consequential piece of topography in the atmospheric geography of Asia, and ✗ C2 devotes one table
cell to the result.

**Caption.** One plateau drawn twice. In winter it **splits** a westerly jet and feeds subsidence over
north-west India; in summer it **becomes a heat source** and spins an anticyclone whose southern flank
is an easterly jet.

### ✅ D-08 — Khullar's bifurcated winter jet, in full

✅ **Khullar, printed folios 126-127 (scan PDF p.80; folios not read, U-06)** is the only quantified
upper-air treatment of India in the entire evidence base. ✗ **C2 carries one table cell against it.**

| ✅ What Khullar states | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| The westerly jet "is located along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas in winter** but it suddenly shifts to the north with the onset of the monsoon" | Fixes the **winter position** and the **seasonal switch** |
| "**northward movement of the subtropical jet is the first indication of the onset of the monsoon over India**" | ⚠️ **An indication, not a cause.** See N-22 |
| "**The southern branch is stronger, with an average speed of about 240 km p.h. at 200 mb compared with 70 to 90 km p.h. of the northern branch**" | **The bifurcation, the two branch speeds and the level, in one sentence.** ✗ C2 has none of it |
| "Air subsiding beneath this upper westerly current gives **dry outblowing northerly winds from the subtropical anticyclone over north-western India and Pakistan**" | The **winter dryness of north-west India** is a consequence of the jet |
| "**The upper jet is responsible for steering of the western depressions from the Mediterranean Sea**" | **The single most important sentence for Lesson 20.** No jet, no western disturbance reaching India |
| "Some of the depressions continue eastwards, **redeveloping in the zone of jet stream confluence about 30°N, 105°E** beyond the area of subsidence in the immediate lee of Tibet" | The two branches **re-join east of Tibet**, and a surviving system can **re-intensify** there |
| "**By the end of May the southern jet begins to break and later it is diverted to the north of Tibet Plateau** … the burst of the monsoon does not take place until the upper-air circulation has switched to its summer pattern" | The **timing** of the switch |
| Attributions: **Pedelaborde** (Fig. 5.4), **Koteshwaram** (Figs. 5.5 and 5.6, "Axis of the Easterly and Westerly Jet stream at **12 km**"), **T.N. Krishnamurti** (200 mb divergence, **June-August 1967**), **S. Rama Rattan** | Named, dated scholarship — the kind of evidence that distinguishes a Mains answer |

✗ **N-21 applied.** ✗ C2 line 20 gives the STWJ's winter position as "south of Himalayas around **~25
deg N in notes**" — **a hedge, not a source**. ✅ Khullar's **Fig. 5.4 carries reference parallels at
23½°N and 40°N** (printed 125-126, **folios read directly**), and his text places the winter jet
"along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**". **The hedge is replaced by the figure's own
reference lines.**

### ✅ D-09 — the Tibetan heat engine, and ✗ N-20

✅ **Majid, *Geography of India*, printed folio 4.8 (scan PDF p.69)** carries the only mechanism for
the tropical easterly jet anywhere in the repository, and it is a chain rather than an assertion:

```text
   THE ANTI-CYCLOGENESIS CHAIN — ✅ Majid *Geography of India* printed 4.8

   [1] A PLATEAU AT ~5 km IS HEATED BY THE SUMMER SUN.
       ⇒ ✅ "The summer-time heating of the Tibetan Plateau makes it a
          HIGH-LEVEL HEAT SOURCE."
       ⚠️ THE WORD "HIGH-LEVEL" IS DOING ALL THE WORK. A hot plain heats the
          air at the BOTTOM of the troposphere. A hot PLATEAU heats the air
          in the MIDDLE of it — at a level where elsewhere there is only
          free atmosphere.
                         ↓
   [2] WARM AIR ALOFT IS LESS DENSE, SO THE COLUMN ABOVE IT EXPANDS AND
       PRESSURE AT HEIGHT RISES.
       ⇒ ✅ "This 'HEAT ENGINE' produces a THERMAL ANTICYCLONE over this
          region. A WARM CORE ANTICYCLONE (HIGH PRESSURE) is formed over
          this plateau during the summer monsoon period."
       ⇒ ✅ "The formation of this anticyclone takes place in the middle
          part of the troposphere AT 500 mb LEVEL. It is the result of a
          process called ANTI-CYCLOGENESIS."
       ⚠️ NOTE THE CONTRAST WITH LESSON 8: the subtropical high is
          DYNAMICALLY induced. THIS one is THERMALLY induced, at height.
          A WARM-CORE HIGH IS AN UNUSUAL OBJECT AND IT IS WORTH SAYING SO.
                         ↓
   [3] AIR FLOWS OUTWARD FROM A HIGH (Lesson 7's anticyclonic circulation),
       SO ON THE SOUTHERN FLANK IT FLOWS EAST TO WEST.
       ⇒ ✅ "on the southern side of this upper air anticyclone, the
          direction of air flow is FROM EAST TO WEST. In fact, these
          easterly winds blowing in the mid-troposphere are known as
          TROPICAL EASTERLY JETS."
                         ↓
   [4] ✗ N-20 — THE CORRECTION, APPLIED:
       ⚠️ "MID-TROPOSPHERE" DESCRIBES THE ANTICYCLONE, WHICH ✅ MAJID HAS
          JUST PLACED AT 500 mb. IT DOES NOT DESCRIBE THE JET. THE TROPICAL
          EASTERLY JET'S CORE IS IN THE UPPER TROPOSPHERE, AROUND
          150-200 hPa. KEEP THE ANTI-CYCLOGENESIS MECHANISM — IT IS THE
          ONLY ONE IN THE REPOSITORY — AND CORRECT THE LEVEL.
       ⚠️ ✅ KOTESHWARAM'S OWN FIGURE CAPTION SUPPORTS THE CORRECTION: "Axis
          of the Easterly and Westerly Jet stream AT 12 km", which is upper
          troposphere, not 500 mb.
```

✅ **And the barrier claim that belongs with it.** ✅ Majid, same folio, after **Maung Tun Yin**: the
Tibetan Plateau "acts as a **physical barrier**"; "**at the beginning of June, the subtropical jet
stream disappears completely over northern India. At this time, the jet stream shifts to the north of
the Himalayas and Tibet, and takes up a position at about 40°N**"; and "in the middle of October, the
plateau proves to be the most important factor in causing the advance of the jet south of the
Himalayas **or bifurcate it into two parts**."

### ✗ N-22 — C2 over-states the causal role, and three sources say three different things

✗ **C2, line 30:** "Northward withdrawal of STWJ is a **pre-condition** for SW monsoon onset."

| Source | ✅ What it actually says | What kind of claim that is |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Khullar, printed 126 | "northward movement of the subtropical jet is the **first indication** of the onset of the monsoon over India" | An **indicator** — a sign, not a cause |
| ✅ Majid *India*, printed 4.8, after **Maung Tun Yin** | The jet "**disappears completely over northern India**" at the beginning of June | A **description of what happens**, not a causal claim |
| ✅ Majid *India*, same folio | The abrupt onset "is prompted by the **hydro-dynamic effect** of the Himalayas and **not by the thermally induced low pressure centre** over northwest India" | An explicit **causal attribution to something else** |

**✗ N-22 applied. "Indication", "disappearance" and "hydro-dynamic forcing" are three different
claims, and none of them is "pre-condition".** The edition states all three with their attributions
and **does not adopt C2's stronger formulation**.

↪ **And the boundary this touches is the sharpest in the file.** ⚠️ **The complete monsoon mechanism
— Halley's thermal theory, the ITCZ and the NITCZ/SITCZ, the air-mass theory, the monsoon front, the
burst, breaks, withdrawal, onset dates and the rainfall distribution — belongs to
`Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism` and is not taught anywhere in this file.** ⚠️ **The
Tibetan plateau treated as a MONSOON CAUSE is Topic 16's.** This lesson owns the plateau as a
**barrier to a jet** and as a **high-level heat source that spins an anticyclone**. It does not
explain the monsoon.

⚠️ **The jet rule, applied explicitly, as the governing audit requires.** ✅ **Khullar's jet passages
sit inside his monsoon chapter (Chapter 5, "CLIMATE"), and ✅ Majid's folio 4.8 sits inside his
Chapter 4 monsoon material. This edition is quoting from monsoon sections into a weather lesson, and
says so here at the point of quotation.**

### ✅ D-24 and ✗ N-40 — the three anticyclone types, including blocking

✅ **Majid IWG, printed 3.35 (scan PDF p.108)** supplies the book grounding that ✗ C1 §10 describes
without ever citing:

| Type | ✅ As Majid states it |
|---|---|
| **(a) Cold anticyclones** | "originate in the **Arctic and Antarctic** regions and advance in the **easterly and south-easterly** direction … of **very low thickness** … **die out when entering in the tropical regions**" |
| **(b) Warm anticyclones** | "originate in the belt of **subtropical high pressure where the winds descend in the Hadley's cell** … associated with **light wind, low humidity and cloudless skies**" |
| **(c) Blocking anticyclones** | "develop due to **obstruction in the air circulation in the upper atmosphere** … They develop over **North-West Europe and the Pacific Ocean between 140-170 W longitudes** … they move **very slowly**" |

✅ **General properties:** "**Anticyclones do not have fronts**" and "the wind blows **outward from the
centre** … **clockwise in the Northern Hemisphere and anticlockwise in the Southern**".

✗ **N-40 applied.** ⚠️ Majid's blocking geography — "**North-West Europe and the Pacific Ocean between
140-170 W longitudes**" — is **narrow**, and must be shown as **the book's stated examples, not as
the global distribution of blocking**. Blocking is a property of **high-amplitude upper flow**
wherever it occurs; those two regions are where Majid names it.

⚠️ **How blocking connects to the rebuilt diagram of Lesson 18.** A tall ridge in a high-amplitude
flow can close off and become a **quasi-stationary anticyclone**, which then **obstructs** the flow
behind it. Systems that would have been steered past are instead **diverted or stalled**. ✓ **C1's
preserved sentence applies directly: the damaging extremes are extremes of duration, and duration is
governed by the upper-air wave pattern.**

### Rossby waves over Asia — using Lesson 7's correction, not re-arguing it

⚠️ Lesson 7 established the distinction and this lesson **uses** it. A **Rossby wave** is a
planetary wave arising from the **latitudinal variation of the Coriolis parameter**; a **geostrophic
wind** is a point balance. ✗ Majid's heading "Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)" conflates them and ✗
C2 line 104 propagates it; ✓ C2 line 106 states the correct definition.

**What matters here is that large topography forces standing waves.** The Tibetan Plateau and the
Rockies are the two great obstacles in the Northern Hemisphere's westerlies, and both **anchor**
wave crests and troughs to particular longitudes. This is why the Northern Hemisphere's flow is more
meridional than the Southern's (Lesson 18, P2a): **the Southern Hemisphere has almost no comparable
topography between 40°S and 60°S.**

↪ **Route-out, and it is a refusal as much as a route.** ⚠️ **Arctic amplification and the
jet-waviness hypothesis** — the proposition that a warming Arctic reduces the pole-to-equator thermal
gradient and makes the upper flow more meridional — belongs to
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18_IPCC-Assessment-Reports`. **It is stated here as an open question
and never as causation.** ⚠️ **No sentence in this file claims that the flow is becoming more
meridional, that blocking is becoming more frequent, or that jet waviness has changed (U-02).**

↪ **Second route-out.** ⚠️ **Himalayan snow cover, glacier mass balance and GLOF** belong to
`Geography/advanced/06_Himalayan-Glaciers-GLOF`. This lesson owns the **snowfall-producing
circulation**; Topic 06 owns the **snowpack and the ice**.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — two highs that are not the same object.**

| | **Subtropical high** (Lesson 8) | **Tibetan warm-core anticyclone** (this lesson) |
|---|---|---|
| **Genesis** | ✅ **Dynamically** induced — convergence aloft forces subsidence | ✅ **Thermally** induced — a **high-level heat source** |
| **Core** | Not a warm-core feature in the relevant sense | ✅ **Warm core**, explicitly |
| **Level** | A surface-to-mid-tropospheric belt | ✅ Forms "in the middle part of the troposphere at **500 mb** level" |
| **Season** | Year-round, migrating | ✅ "during the **summer monsoon period**" |
| **Product** | Subsidence, inversion, the world's hot deserts | ✅ Outflow on the southern flank — **the tropical easterly jet** |

**Objection.** "Khullar's figures are decades old. A branch speed of 240 km/h at 200 mb cannot be
quoted as current."

**Reply.** It is not quoted as current and it is not quoted bare. It is quoted as **Khullar's figure,
with its page, inside a book-era attribution**, exactly as ✅ **Krishnamurti's analysis is dated to
June-August 1967** on the same pages. ⚠️ **The edition states no current jet speed from any source**,
because none was retrieved, and **asserts no trend**. What the book-era figures establish is the
**structure** — that the jet bifurcates, that the southern branch is the stronger, that it sits at
**200 mb**, and that it steers the western depressions. **Structure is what the answer needs; the
exact speed is a dated illustration of it.**

**Unresolved residual.** Three, all disclosed. **U-06**: Khullar's printed 126-127 are cited with
their **folios not read**, their printed identity inferred from the adjacent reads at PDF 79 →
124/125; **no Khullar folio is computed anywhere**. **U-07**: ✅ Majid *Geography of India*'s contents
list "**Jet Stream and Indian Monsoons 4.9**" and "**El-Nino and the Indian Monsoon 4.10**", and the
abridged scan **jumps from 4.8 straight to 4.18**, so both **appear to be absent**; **absence was not
proved exhaustively, nothing is drawn from either, and no claim is made that the printed book lacks
them**. And **N-20's level correction** is made against a named book on standard grounds, with
✅ Koteshwaram's own "at 12 km" caption noted as supporting it.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating the Himalaya and the Tibetan Plateau as the same object.** The **Himalaya** is a wall;
  the **plateau** is a raised surface the size of a subcontinent, and it is the **plateau** that is
  the high-level heat source.
- ❌ **Giving the jet one winter position.** ✅ It **bifurcates**: a southern branch at **240 km/h at
  200 mb** and a northern at **70-90 km/h**.
- ❌ **Omitting the level.** ✅ The branch speeds are stated **at 200 mb**; a speed without a level is
  not a jet statement.
- ❌ **Using C2's "~25 deg N in notes".** ✗ A hedge, not a source. ✅ Khullar's **Fig. 5.4 reference
  parallels are 23½°N and 40°N** (N-21).
- ❌ **Calling the jet's withdrawal a "pre-condition" of onset.** ✗ C2 does. ✅ Khullar says **"first
  indication"**; ✅ Maung Tun Yin says it **"disappears completely"**; and ✅ Majid attributes onset to
  the **hydro-dynamic effect of the Himalayas** (N-22).
- ❌ **Placing the tropical easterly jet in the mid-troposphere.** ✗ ✅ Majid's "mid-troposphere"
  describes the **anticyclone at 500 mb**; the **TEJ core is upper-tropospheric, around 150-200 hPa**
  (N-20).
- ❌ **Calling the Tibetan anticyclone a subtropical high.** One is **thermally** induced and
  warm-core; the other is **dynamically** induced.
- ❌ **Giving Majid's blocking locations as the global distribution.** ✅ "North-West Europe and the
  Pacific Ocean between 140-170 W longitudes" are **his stated examples** (N-40).
- ❌ **Explaining the monsoon.** The plateau as a **monsoon cause** is `Geography/advanced/16`'s.
- ❌ **Claiming a trend in jet waviness or blocking.** Prohibited (U-02); routed as an open question.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** India's upper-air circulation is a **salient feature of world's physical geography** in
its regional application, and it is the mechanism that delivers the geophysical phenomena of Lesson
20 to the subcontinent.

**Owned demands served — one third of a split demand.**

**⇄ O1 — 2021 GS-I Q14**, the **Himalayan-barrier-and-jet consequence**. **Lesson 13 is the primary
owner and carries the model.** **This lesson supplies the most ambitious limb of the inventory: that
a range of sufficient height and extent does not merely lift air but reorganises an entire sector's
upper-air circulation.** ❗ **This lesson does not claim the whole stem.**

**Answer use.** When an answer touches Indian winter weather, **begin above the ground**:
*"The subtropical westerly jet, bifurcated by the Tibetan massif, runs along the Himalayan foot in
winter at about 240 km/h at 200 mb, and ✅ Khullar records that 'the upper jet is responsible for
steering of the western depressions from the Mediterranean Sea.'"* **That sentence makes every
surface observation in Lesson 20 a consequence rather than a list.**

### Revision points

1. **The Tibetan Plateau is not a range but a raised surface at ~5 km**, occupying the lower half of
   the troposphere across thousands of kilometres.
2. ✅ **In winter the subtropical westerly jet BIFURCATES: the southern branch is stronger, "about
   240 km p.h. AT 200 mb", against "70 to 90 km p.h." for the northern** (Khullar printed 126-127,
   folios not read, U-06).
3. ✅ **The southern branch runs "along the southern slopes of the Himalayas in winter."** ✗ C2's
   "~25 deg N **in notes**" is a hedge; ✅ **Fig. 5.4's reference parallels are 23½°N and 40°N**
   (N-21).
4. ✅ **"The upper jet is responsible for steering of the western depressions from the Mediterranean
   Sea."** No jet, no western disturbance reaching India.
5. ✅ **"Air subsiding beneath this upper westerly current gives dry outblowing northerly winds from
   the subtropical anticyclone over north-western India and Pakistan"** — the winter dryness of the
   north-west, explained from above.
6. ✅ **The branches re-join: depressions may "redevelop in the zone of jet stream confluence about
   30°N, 105°E" beyond the subsidence in the lee of Tibet.**
7. ✅ **"By the end of May the southern jet begins to break and later it is diverted to the north of
   Tibet Plateau."** ✅ **Maung Tun Yin: at the beginning of June the jet "disappears completely over
   northern India" and "takes up a position at about 40°N."**
8. ✅ **The heat engine: "summer-time heating of the Tibetan Plateau makes it a HIGH-LEVEL HEAT
   SOURCE … a WARM CORE ANTICYCLONE (HIGH PRESSURE) … in the middle part of the troposphere at
   500 mb LEVEL … the result of a process called ANTI-CYCLOGENESIS."**
9. ✅ **The TEJ: "on the southern side of this upper air anticyclone, the direction of air flow is
   FROM EAST TO WEST … known as TROPICAL EASTERLY JETS."** ✅ **S. Rama Rattan: "the eastern jet
   becomes powerful and is stationed at 15°N latitude."**
10. ✗ **N-20: "mid-troposphere" describes the 500 mb ANTICYCLONE, not the jet. The TEJ core is
    UPPER-TROPOSPHERIC, around 150-200 hPa.** ✅ Koteshwaram's caption "at **12 km**" supports the
    correction.
11. ✗ **N-22: three sources, three claims — ✅ "first indication" (Khullar), ✅ "disappears completely"
    (Maung Tun Yin), ✅ onset "prompted by the HYDRO-DYNAMIC effect of the Himalayas and NOT by the
    thermally induced low pressure centre" (Majid).** ✗ **None of them is C2's "pre-condition".**
12. ✅ **Attributions to carry: PEDELABORDE (Fig. 5.4) · KOTESHWARAM (Figs. 5.5-5.6, "at 12 km") ·
    T.N. KRISHNAMURTI (200 mb divergence, JUNE-AUGUST 1967) · S. RAMA RATTAN · MAUNG TUN YIN.**
13. ✅ **Three anticyclone types (Majid printed 3.35): COLD** (Arctic/Antarctic origin, easterly and
    south-easterly advance, very low thickness, dying in the tropics) · **WARM** (subtropical high,
    descent in Hadley's cell, light wind, low humidity, cloudless) · **BLOCKING** ("obstruction in
    the air circulation in the upper atmosphere", moving "very slowly"). ✅ **"Anticyclones do not
    have fronts"**; outflow is **clockwise north, anticlockwise south**.
14. ✗ **N-40: Majid's "North-West Europe and the Pacific Ocean between 140-170 W longitudes" are his
    STATED EXAMPLES, not the global distribution of blocking.**
15. ⚠️ **U-07: Majid *Geography of India*'s printed 4.9 and 4.10 appear absent from the abridged
    scan, which jumps 4.8 → 4.18. Absence was not proved; nothing is drawn from either.** ⚠️ **U-02:
    no trend in jet waviness or blocking is asserted, and Arctic amplification is routed to
    `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18` as an OPEN QUESTION, never as causation.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a single plateau produces two jets running in opposite directions over the
same country in different seasons, and state the correction that the source material requires.

**Model answer:** The Tibetan Plateau acts in **two different capacities**. **In winter it is a
barrier.** Standing at roughly 5 km across thousands of kilometres, it splits the subtropical
westerly flow: ✅ Khullar records that "the **southern branch is stronger**, with an average speed of
about **240 km p.h. at 200 mb** compared with **70 to 90 km p.h.** of the northern branch", with the
southern branch running "along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**". That branch is decisive for
India because ✅ "the **upper jet is responsible for steering of the western depressions** from the
Mediterranean Sea", and the subsidence beneath it gives ✅ "**dry outblowing northerly winds** from
the subtropical anticyclone over north-western India and Pakistan". **In summer it is a heat
source.** ✅ Majid's *Geography of India* (printed 4.8) states that "the summer-time heating of the
Tibetan Plateau makes it a **high-level heat source**", producing a ✅ "**warm core anticyclone (high
pressure)** … in the middle part of the troposphere at **500 mb** level … the result of a process
called **anti-cyclogenesis**". Outflow on the anticyclone's southern flank therefore runs ✅ "**from
east to west**" — the **tropical easterly jet**, ✅ "stationed at **15°N latitude**" after S. Rama
Rattan. **The correction required is of level.** ✅ Majid writes that these easterlies blow "in the
**mid-troposphere**", but mid-troposphere is where he has just placed the **anticyclone**, at 500 mb;
the **jet's core is upper-tropospheric, around 150-200 hPa** — and ✅ Koteshwaram's own figure caption
reads "Axis of the Easterly and Westerly Jet stream **at 12 km**".

**Misconception to avoid:** That the plateau's two roles are the same mechanism at different times.
The winter role is **mechanical** — an obstacle splitting a flow. The summer role is **thermal** — a
heat source at height spinning a warm-core high, which is an unusual object precisely because most
high-pressure cells are dynamically induced and cold or neutral at core.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 19

**Original question (20 marks, 250 words).** Analyse the role of the Himalaya-Tibet system in
organising the upper-air circulation over South Asia, and explain its consequences for India's
weather.

**Model:** The system acts **mechanically in winter** and **thermally in summer**.

**Winter.** The plateau, at roughly 5 km, occupies the lower half of the troposphere and splits the
subtropical westerly jet. ✅ Khullar: "the **southern branch is stronger**, with an average speed of
about **240 km p.h. at 200 mb** compared with **70 to 90 km p.h.** of the northern branch", the
southern running "along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**". Three consequences follow.
✅ "The **upper jet is responsible for steering of the western depressions** from the Mediterranean
Sea" — India's entire winter rain in the north-west arrives on this conveyor. ✅ Subsidence beneath
it gives "**dry outblowing northerly winds** … over north-western India and Pakistan", which is why
the region is dry and cold between systems. And ✅ depressions may "redevelop in the **zone of jet
stream confluence about 30°N, 105°E**" where the branches re-join.

**Summer.** ✅ "Summer-time heating of the Tibetan Plateau makes it a **high-level heat source**",
producing by ✅ **anti-cyclogenesis** a ✅ "**warm core anticyclone … at 500 mb level**", whose
southern flank flows ✅ "**from east to west**" as the **tropical easterly jet**, ✅ "stationed at
**15°N**". ✅ Maung Tun Yin records the westerly jet taking "a position at about **40°N**".

⚠️ The TEJ's core is **upper-tropospheric**, not mid-tropospheric. ⚠️ No trend is claimed. *(220 words)*

**Rubric (20 marks):** Tibetan barrier mechanism **5** · bifurcation with branch speeds **5** · TEJ
anti-cyclogenesis chain **5** · Rossby waves and blocking **4** · monsoon-boundary routing **1**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 1 opened with a Stevenson screen. Lesson 20 closes with a 6-kilometre forecast grid, and the
loop between them is deliberate: **every number in the preceding nineteen lessons came from an
instrument, a criterion and a date.**

The object this lesson is built around is a case in point. Ask most candidates what a western
disturbance is and they will say: a low-pressure system from the Mediterranean that travels east and
brings winter rain to north-west India. That is where it was **born** and what it **does**. It is not
what it **is**.

**What it is, is a two-level object.** 📄 IMD describes it as a **cyclonic circulation in the lower
troposphere** with a **trough aloft in the middle and upper tropospheric westerlies** — and fixes it
by **longitude and latitude**. That is how India actually tracks one on a chart. And the distinction
is not pedantry: **origin cannot distinguish a western disturbance from a monsoon depression, and
structure can.** One is a trough embedded in the westerlies with a circulation beneath it; the other
is not.

✗ **Neither owner carries that definition.** Recovering it, together with the complete heat and cold
criteria, Kendrew's series and the Bharat Forecast System, is what this lesson exists to do.

**Caption.** A rail that draws the system as the state defines it, and a strip that puts the heat and
cold criteria side by side so their **symmetry** — the same departure bands, mirrored actual bands,
separate hill and coastal rules — is visible at a glance.

### 📄 D-05 — a dated two-system sequence, used for what it proves and nothing more

📄 **IMD Special Message No. 01, 25 January 2026, 1545 hours IST**, also records:

- The forecast: "light/moderate rainfall at many places over Western Himalayan region during
  **26th-28th** with **isolated heavy rainfall/snowfall and hail storm on 27th January, 2026**", and
  over the adjoining plains "**gusty winds speed reaching 40-60 kmph gusting to 70 kmph and hail**".
- Over Jammu-Kashmir-Ladakh and Himachal, "squally winds speed reaching **50-60 kmph gusting to 70
  kmph**".
- The sequence: "A **fresh and intense** Western Disturbance likely to affect northwest India from
  the night of **26th January**", and "**Another fresh Western disturbance** is likely to affect
  northwest India from the night of **30th January, 2026**."

⚠️ **What this proves and what it does not.** It proves that **two systems within one week are an
operationally recorded occurrence**, that **hail and squalls are among the products of a western
disturbance**, and that **IMD issues a structural description with coordinates**. ⚠️ **It proves
nothing whatever about frequency, intensity or trend. It is a single dated forecast message.** ✗
**C2's "hyperactive Western Disturbances" framing at its line 160 is therefore re-cast in this
edition as a hypothesis**, and no sentence anywhere in this file states that western disturbances are
increasing, decreasing, strengthening or weakening (U-02).

### ✅ D-07 — Khullar's winter consequences, with their attributions

✅ **Khullar, printed folio 134 (scan PDF p.84; folio not read, U-06):**

- ✅ "The western disturbances also cause a little rainfall in north-west India. The amount of
  rainfall gradually **decreases from the north and north-west to east**. The average rainfall during
  three months from **December to February is about 60 cm in the Himalayan region, 12 cm in Punjab,
  5.3 cm at Delhi**."
- ✅ "According to **Met unit at Indira Gandhi International Airport, New Delhi the average duration
  of occurrence of fog at the airport is 85 hours**", with "table 5.2" cited for the year-to-year
  variation. ⚠️ **U-15: Table 5.2 was not read. The 85-hour average is used and no range is stated.**
- ✅ "The minimum temperature by **5° to 10 °C below normal** are experienced."

⚠️ **Note how that last figure meshes with the criteria strip.** A **5-10 °C below-normal** minimum
spans IMD's **cold wave (4.5-6.4 °C negative departure)** and **severe cold wave (> 6.4 °C)** bands
simultaneously. **A book-era descriptive sentence and a current official criterion are describing the
same phenomenon in two vocabularies**, and showing them together is the kind of synthesis a Mains
answer is rewarded for.

✗ **The string "Bharat Forecast System" appears nowhere in C1 or C2** (N-39), although it is an

### 🇮🇳 India hook — the whole lesson, assembled as one winter

```text
             upper westerlies, ✅ "roughly along Long. 74°E to the north of
```

↪ **Four route-outs, all mandatory.** ⚠️ **ENSO, El Niño, La Niña, the Walker circulation, the Indian
Ocean Dipole and the IMD Long Period Average classification** — ✗ carried by **C2 at its lines 140
and 143** — belong to `Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity`,
`Geography/advanced/12_Indian-Ocean-and-IOD` and `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism`.

calendars, wheat yield response and procurement** → the `Economy` agriculture owner; this file states
that a western disturbance waters the rabi crop and **does not price it**. ⚠️ **Fog disruption to
rail and air operations** → `Geography/basic/33_Transport-Trade-and-Indian-Space-Programme`; the
85-hour fog figure is a **weather fact**, not an airport-management argument. ⚠️ **Cold-wave

Waves-and-Slow-Onset-Risk`; the **criterion** is ours and the **consequence** is theirs.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison worth carrying — the two systems a chart must distinguish.**

| | **Western disturbance** | **Monsoon depression** |
|---|---|---|
| **Family** | Mid-latitude **frontal** wave cyclone (Lesson 14) | A tropical system of the monsoon circulation |
| **Structure** | 📄 **Two-level**: lower-tropospheric circulation beneath a **trough in the middle and upper westerlies** | A surface low within a broadly uniform tropical air mass |
| **Embedded in** | The **westerlies** | The monsoon flow |
| **Season** | 📄 Cold season; the cold-wave season runs **November to March** | The monsoon season |
| **Track** | **West to east**, steered by the STWJ | Generally **east to west**, inland from the Bay |
| **Precipitation** | Moderate, widespread, with **snow** at altitude and recorded **hail** | Heavy, often with very high totals |
| **Owner** | **This file** | ↪ `Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism` |

⚠️ **Origin cannot make this distinction and structure can.** That is the whole argument for D-02.

**Objection.** "The official definition is an operational convenience. A textbook definition by
origin is adequate for an examination."

**Reply.** It is not, for three reasons. **It fails a chart test**: a candidate given two synoptic
charts and asked which shows a western disturbance cannot answer from origin, because origin is not
on the chart. **It fails the owned demand's own logic**: the 2026 Prelims demand in this lesson asks
for a **resolution objective** and a **developing institution** — the examiner is already testing the
measuring state rather than the textbook. **And it leaves C2's commonest trap unguarded**: students
treat the western disturbance as a monsoon feature, and only structure — a trough in the
**westerlies**, over a lower-level circulation — forecloses that.

**Unresolved residual.** Five, all disclosed and none resolved by assertion. **U-01**: the cloudburst
criterion still has no first-party anchor; the IMD glossary page returned **HTTP 404** at audit and
again at generation. **U-02**: no first-party statement of any trend in western-disturbance frequency
or intensity was retrieved, so none is made. **U-08**: the criteria sheets' version currency is
unconfirmed against a dated circular. **U-10**: no commissioned-radar count was retrieved. **U-15**:
Khullar's Table 5.2 was not read, so the 85-hour fog average is used and **no range is stated**.
⚠️ **I-05 also stands and is worth stating plainly: no ledger row exists for a western disturbance by
name, in any year, for any subject.** The India half of this topic is examined only through its
**consequences** — winter rain, fog, cold waves, hail, rabi damage — and through the 2021
mountain-barrier demand. **This edition therefore justifies its western-disturbance lessons on
mechanism and consequence, not on a direct PYQ**, and says so.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Defining a western disturbance by its origin.** ✗ C2 does. 📄 **IMD defines it structurally**:
  a lower-tropospheric cyclonic circulation beneath a **trough in the middle and upper tropospheric
  westerlies**, at **74°E north of 28°N**.
- ❌ **Calling it a low rather than a trough.** The **upper-level feature is a trough**, and the
  two-level description is what distinguishes it from a monsoon depression.
- ❌ **Giving the impact footprint as seven states.** 📄 The official list has **ten** jurisdictions;
  ✗ C2 omits **Ladakh, Chandigarh and Rajasthan**.
- ❌ **Quoting "3 to 5 disturbances per month" as settled.** ✗ **Khullar's own next sentence gives
  Kendrew's 2-4-5-5-4**, whose November value falls **below** that floor (N-23). Both shown, neither
  asserted.
- ❌ **Presenting Kendrew's series as a current IMD statistic.** ⚠️ It is a **mid-twentieth-century**
  climatology and must be dated on every use (N-24).
- ❌ **Stating any trend in western disturbances.** Prohibited (U-02). ✗ C2's "hyperactive" framing is
  re-cast as a **hypothesis**.
- ❌ **Giving the heat-wave criterion as "40 °C".** ✗ C1 does. 📄 The criterion also fixes **hills at
  30 °C**, **coastal at 37 °C with a 4.5 °C departure**, departure bands of **4.5-6.4** and
  **> 6.4 °C**, actual bands of **≥ 45** and **≥ 47 °C**, the **2-station / 2-consecutive-day /
  second-day** declaration rule, and the **warm night** (N-27).
- ❌ **Omitting the cold wave entirely.** ✗ Neither owner carries a single cold-wave criterion, though
  it is the defining hazard of the season this topic's India half owns (N-28).
- ❌ **Writing "Mission Mausam" with no ministry, outlay or agencies.** ✗ C2 does. 📄 **MoES, ₹2,000
  crore, and IMD, IITM Pune, NCMRWF, INCOIS and NIOT** (N-38). ⚠️ **No radar count** (U-10).
- ❌ **Using 67 per cent for the Bharat Forecast System's core-zone gain.** 📄 **PIB states 64 per
  cent** (U-11).
- ❌ **Restating the LPA bands or explaining El Niño.** ✗ C2 lines 140 and 143 carry them; they are
  **routed out** (I-04).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson completes the **salient features** clause for India's own weather systems
and supplies the **measurement register** — criteria, institutions and resolution — through which the
geophysical-phenomena clause's extremes are officially recognised.

**Owned demand served — one, and it was unanswerable from the repository before this edition.**

**P14 — 2026 Prelims GS-I Q84**, the **Bharat Forecast System's resolution objective and developing
institution**. ✗ **The string appears nowhere in either owner.** 📄 **PIB Release ID 2131392 of 26 May
2025 supplies both halves: IITM Pune, and 12 km → 6 km.** ⚠️ **The 2026 Set-A key held locally is
recorded as PROVISIONAL and is not disclosed. No option letter and no elimination chain appears.**

**Answer use.** In any answer touching Indian winter weather or Indian forecasting, **lead with the
structure and date the institution**: *"IMD describes a western disturbance as a lower-tropospheric
cyclonic circulation beneath a trough in the middle and upper tropospheric westerlies, located at
about 74°E north of 28°N (Special Message No. 01, 25 January 2026), steered by the southern branch of
the bifurcated subtropical westerly jet."* **That one sentence carries a structure, a coordinate, a
date and a mechanism**, and it is the best-value sentence in the whole India half.

### Revision points

1. 📄 **The operational definition, verbatim:** "The Western Disturbance as a **cyclonic circulation**
   over Himachal and adjoining Punjab in **lower tropospheric levels** with a **trough aloft in
   middle & upper tropospheric westerlies** roughly along **Long. 74°E to the north of Lat. 28°N**"
   (IMD Special Message No. 01, **25 January 2026, 1545 IST**).
2. **It is a TWO-LEVEL object located by coordinates.** ✗ C2 defines it **by origin alone**, and
   **origin is not structure**.
3. **It is a mid-latitude FRONTAL wave cyclone** (Lesson 14), **not** a monsoon feature and **not** a
   tropical system.
4. ✅ **Steered by the southern branch of the bifurcated STWJ** (Lesson 19): "the **upper jet is
   responsible for steering of the western depressions** from the Mediterranean Sea."
5. 📄 **The official footprint is TEN jurisdictions:** J&K, **Ladakh**, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand,
   Punjab, Haryana, **Chandigarh**, Delhi, Uttar Pradesh, **Rajasthan**. ✗ **C2 omits the three in
   bold.**
6. 📄 **A dated two-system sequence:** "a **fresh and intense** Western Disturbance … from the night
   of **26th January**" and "**another fresh Western disturbance** … from the night of **30th
   January, 2026**". ⚠️ **Proof of occurrence, not of frequency.**
7. 📄 **Its products include hail and squalls:** "isolated **heavy rainfall/snowfall and hail storm**
   on **27th January, 2026**"; winds "**40-60 kmph gusting to 70 kmph**" on the plains and "**50-60
   kmph gusting to 70 kmph**" over the hills.
8. ✅ **The precipitation gradient, December-February: "about 60 cm in the Himalayan region, 12 cm in
   Punjab, 5.3 cm at Delhi"**, decreasing "**from the north and north-west to east**".
9. ✗ **N-23: ✅ Khullar's two frequency statements disagree** — "on an average **3 to 5 disturbances
   per month**" against **Kendrew's 2 (Nov), 4 (Dec), 5 (Jan), 5 (Feb), 4 (Mar)**, whose November
   value falls below the floor. **Both shown; neither asserted.** ✗ C2 carries **no** figure.
10. ⚠️ **N-24: Kendrew's series is MID-20th-CENTURY and is dated on every use. It is never presented
    as a current IMD statistic.**
11. ✅ **Fog: "the average duration of occurrence of fog at the airport is 85 HOURS"** at IGI, New
    Delhi. ⚠️ **U-15: Table 5.2's year-to-year range was not read; no range is stated.** ✅ Minima
    "**5° to 10 °C below normal**".
12. 📄 **HEAT WAVE:** plains **≥ 40 °C**, hills **≥ 30 °C**; departure **4.5-6.4 °C**, severe
    **> 6.4 °C**; actual **≥ 45 °C**, severe **≥ 47 °C**; coastal **≥ 37 °C with a ≥ 4.5 °C
    departure**; declared when met at **2 stations** in a sub-division on **2 consecutive days**, on
    the **second day**; **warm night** only when the maximum stays **≥ 40 °C**, with minimum
    departures of **4.5-6.4 °C** and **> 6.4 °C**. ✗ **C1 gives the plains threshold only** (N-27).
13. 📄 **COLD WAVE:** plains **≤ 10 °C**, hills **≤ 0 °C**; negative departure **4.5-6.4 °C**, severe
    **> 6.4 °C**; actual minimum **≤ 4 °C** and **≤ 2 °C** for plains; coastal **≤ 15 °C with a
    −4.5 °C departure**; season **November to March**, casualties **December to March**. ✗ **Neither
    owner carries any of it** (N-28).
14. 📄 **BHARAT FORECAST SYSTEM: developed by the INDIAN INSTITUTE OF TROPICAL METEOROLOGY (IITM),
    PUNE; resolution FROM 12 km TO 6 km**; method the "**Triangular Cubic Octahedral Grid model**";
    claimed gains "**30% … in extreme rainfall forecasts and a 64% enhancement in core zones**";
    collaboration with **IMD and ISRO** (PIB Release ID **2131392**, **26 May 2025**). ✗ **Absent from
    both owners** (N-39). ⚠️ **U-11: use 64, not 67.**
15. 📄 **MISSION MAUSAM: Ministry of Earth Sciences; ₹2,000 crore; IMD, IITM Pune, NCMRWF, INCOIS and
    NIOT.** ✗ C2 names the mission and nothing else (N-38). ⚠️ **U-10: no radar count is stated.**
    ⚠️ **U-02 and I-05: no trend is asserted, and no ledger row exists for a western disturbance by
    name in any year for any subject — the India half is examined only through its consequences.**

### Concept check

**Question:** "A western disturbance is a low-pressure system that forms over the Mediterranean and
travels east to bring winter rain to north-west India." State what is right, what is missing, and
why the missing part is the examinable part.

**Model answer:** **What is right** is the origin and the broad effect: these are extra-tropical
systems that move east from the Mediterranean region via Iran, Afghanistan and Pakistan, and they
deliver north-west India's winter precipitation, with ✅ Khullar recording a December-February
gradient of "about **60 cm in the Himalayan region, 12 cm in Punjab, 5.3 cm at Delhi**". **What is
missing is the structure.** 📄 IMD describes the system as "a **cyclonic circulation** over Himachal
and adjoining Punjab in **lower tropospheric levels** with a **trough aloft in middle & upper
tropospheric westerlies** roughly along **Long. 74°E to the north of Lat. 28°N**" (Special Message
No. 01, **25 January 2026**). It is a **two-level object, fixed by coordinates** — and it is a
mid-latitude **frontal** wave cyclone, steered into India by the southern branch of the subtropical
westerly jet, which ✅ Khullar says "is responsible for **steering of the western depressions**".
**Why the missing part is the examinable part:** origin is not visible on a synoptic chart, so it
cannot distinguish a western disturbance from a monsoon depression, whereas structure can — one is a
trough embedded in the **westerlies** above a lower-level circulation; the other is not. Origin also
cannot explain why the system brings **snow at altitude, moderate widespread rain, and officially
forecast hail and squalls of 40-60 kmph gusting to 70 kmph**, nor why it leaves behind conditions
meeting IMD's **cold-wave** criteria — plains minimum **≤ 10 °C**, negative departure **4.5-6.4 °C**,
severe above **6.4 °C**.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a dated bulletin showing two systems in one week says anything about
frequency. It records an occurrence. **No first-party source stating a trend in western-disturbance
frequency or intensity was retrieved, and no such claim appears anywhere in this edition.**

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 20

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** Examine the western disturbance as a structural object
and as a measured one, and assess what India's forecasting institutions can and cannot yet tell us
about it.

**Model:** **Structurally**, a western disturbance is a mid-latitude frontal wave cyclone, not a
monsoon feature. 📄 IMD defines it as "a **cyclonic circulation** over Himachal and adjoining Punjab
in **lower tropospheric levels** with a **trough aloft in middle & upper tropospheric westerlies**
roughly along **Long. 74°E to the north of Lat. 28°N**" (**25 January 2026**). It is steered by the
southern branch of the Tibet-bifurcated subtropical westerly jet, which ✅ Khullar says "is
responsible for **steering of the western depressions** from the Mediterranean Sea".

**As a measured object** it is known through thresholds and products. 📄 The same message forecasts
"isolated **heavy rainfall/snowfall and hail storm**" and winds "**40-60 kmph gusting to 70 kmph**",
and is addressed to **ten** jurisdictions including Ladakh, Chandigarh and Rajasthan. ✅ Khullar gives
the precipitation decay — **60 cm Himalaya, 12 cm Punjab, 5.3 cm Delhi** — and **85 hours** of
average fog at IGI Airport. After clearance, 📄 IMD's **cold wave** applies: plains **≤ 10 °C**,
negative departure **4.5-6.4 °C**, severe above **6.4 °C**.

**What is not yet known.** ✅ Khullar's own two frequency statements disagree — "**3 to 5 per month**"
against **Kendrew's 2-4-5-5-4** — and Kendrew is **mid-twentieth-century**. ⚠️ **No first-party
statement of any trend was retrieved.** 📄 Resolution is improving — **12 km to 6 km**, at **IITM
Pune** — but resolution is not climatology. *(223 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** IMD structural definition **5** · Kendrew's series handled with its era **3** ·
heat and cold criteria complete **4** · forecasting institutions dated **2** · no-trend discipline
**1**

| Rabi crop calendars, wheat yield response, procurement, agricultural loss accounting | `Economy` agriculture owner |
| Fog disruption to rail and air operations; airport and railway management | `Geography/basic/33_Transport-Trade-and-Indian-Space-Programme` |
| Cold-wave mortality, shelters and the disaster response chain | `Disaster-Management/basic/09_Drought-Heat-Waves-and-Slow-Onset-Risk` |

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

All ten routing ledgers were swept in full across 2018-2026 for the two owner filenames, and then
re-swept across the same range for every weather, jet, disturbance, cyclone, extreme, instrument and
criterion keyword, so that **adjacency could be measured rather than assumed**.

**Nineteen ledger rows route to `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`. No ledger row routes to
`Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances`** — C2 owns nothing in its own right and
exists entirely as C1's India application.

**The owned profile, stated plainly.** **Twenty-one owned demands: seven Mains and fourteen
Prelims.** This is the largest owned load in this lane by a factor of three — Topic 12 had seven
owned demands in total and two owned Mains; Topic 11 had one owned Mains demand — and it is the first
in the lane whose owned load spans **both halves of the paper at full weight**. The seven Mains
demands sit at **three different mark weights** with **five structurally different directives**:
*briefly mention and explain*, *how*, *explain* (three times, applied to three different kinds of
object), *what is … explain its impact*, and *compare*. **No single answer spine can serve them**, and
that single fact — not the size of the subject matter — is what fixed this edition's lesson count.

**A recorded routing inconsistency, disclosed and not repaired (I-01).** **No `_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-*-2026.md`
file exists in the repository.** The two 2026 GS-I Mains demands are carried **only inside C1's manual
block at lines 657-684**, with the stated provenance "OCR-verified English text from the locally
preserved official 2026 GS-I scan". **Both are treated as owned on the strength of that provenance.
No ledger has been created or edited.**

**A second inconsistency, also recorded (I-05).** **No ledger row exists for a western disturbance by
name, in any year, for any subject.** The India half of this topic is examined only through its
**consequences** — winter rain, fog, cold waves, hail and rabi damage — and through the 2021 GS-I Q14
mountain-barrier demand. **This edition therefore justifies Lessons 19 and 20 on mechanism and
consequence, not on a direct PYQ, and says so.**

## ANSWER-NEUTRALITY RULE — BINDING THROUGHOUT THIS SECTION AND THIS FILE

1. This edition contains **no option letters, no answer keys and no elimination chains** anywhere,
   for any objective demand, owned or adjacent.
2. **P1 to P5 have no locally available key and none is inferred, reconstructed or implied.**
3. **P6 to P13 are recorded as having official Set-A keys held locally. Those keys are not disclosed
   here.** **P14's key is recorded as locally available and PROVISIONAL, and is not disclosed.**
   **Key availability is not key disclosure.**
4. **O1 to O7 are subjective Mains demands authored by UPSC and therefore do receive full 150- or
   250-word models. The fourteen objective demands never do.** The distinction is absolute: this
   edition may answer what UPSC asked in **essay** form and may never answer what UPSC asked in
   **objective** form.
5. The edition runs in **concept-check mode**. **No compiled four-option corpus exists anywhere in
   it.**

## BAND A — OWNED MAINS DEMANDS, ROUTED BY EXACT FILENAME TO THE TOPIC 13 OWNERS

Seven demands, three mark weights, five directives. **Each model is in the next H1 section and the
seven are pairwise distinct in construction.**

### A·1 — O1 · 2021 Mains GS-I, Question 14

**Demand.** The alignment of major mountain ranges of the world, and their impact on local weather.

**Directive decoded.** ***Briefly mention and explain*** · **15 marks · 250 words.** A **compound**
directive: a genuinely brief **inventory**, then an **explained consequence**. An inventory consuming
half the answer has failed the *briefly* and starved the *explain*.

**Construction required.** Short inventory, then **one organising variable applied back across it** —
whether a range lies **across** or **along** the prevailing flow.

⇄ **STRUCTURALLY SPLIT DEMAND.** **Lesson 13 is the primary owner and carries the model.** **Lesson
10** carries the Foehn-and-rain-shadow consequence. **Lesson 19** carries the
Himalayan-barrier-and-jet consequence — the claim that a range of sufficient height and extent
**reorganises an entire sector's upper-air circulation**. ❗ **No one of the three lessons claims to
have answered the whole stem, and each carries an explicit cross-reference to the other two.**

⚠️ **C1 §11 supplies no answer spine for this demand.** Building it was an obligation of this
edition, not an option.

### A·2 — O2 · 2022 Mains GS-I, Question 17

**Demand.** The troposphere as the layer determining weather processes.

**Directive decoded.** ***How*** · **15 marks · 250 words.** A *how* question wants **one mechanism
traced through one object**, not an inventory and not a comparison. If the answer could be reordered
without damage, it is not a *how* answer.

**Construction required.** A **five-link chain**: heated from below → lapse rate → instability →
overturning → moisture lifted and condensed → confined by the lid, with the **stratospheric contrast
as its proof**.

**Lesson 4** builds the chain. ⚠️ **C1 §11 supplies no spine for this demand either.** This is the
**narrowest** of the seven constructions by design: one layer, one property, one chain.

### A·3 — O3 · 2024 Mains GS-I, Question 4

**Demand.** Sea surface temperature rise and the formation of tropical cyclones.

**Directive decoded.** ***Explain***, applied to a **causal relation** · **10 marks · 150 words.**

**Construction required.** A **chain shown, then bounded**. Trace the feedback forward; close with the
qualification that makes it defensible — **potential intensity rises with ocean heat content, but
genesis needs five further gates, three of which are set by circulation and not by temperature**.
**The qualification is the mark-bearing half, not a hedge.**

**Lesson 15** builds it. ⚠️ **U-02 binds absolutely: no statement about storm numbers or trends
follows, and none is made.**

### A·4 — O4 · 2024 Mains GS-I, Question 6

**Demand.** The phenomenon of cloudbursts.

**Directive decoded.** ***Explain***, applied to a **phenomenon** · **10 marks · 150 words.**

**Construction required.** **A definition, a criterion and a mechanism — in that order.** This is a
*naming* answer, not a causal chain and not a comparison. ⚠️ **The criterion must carry its
provenance caveat**, because ✗ C1 tags it ✅ though **no book states it** and the **first-party IMD
glossary page returned HTTP 404** at audit and again at generation (N-25, U-01).

**Lesson 17** builds it, and states **the cloudburst rule in words** (§10.3(1)) because the ledgers
genuinely disagree (I-02).

### A·5 — O5 · 2024 Mains GS-I, Question 16

**Demand.** The phenomenon of 'Twisters' and their **concentration** around the Gulf of Mexico.

**Directive decoded.** ***Explain***, applied to a **spatial concentration** · **15 marks · 250
words.** The word *concentration* is the demand: the answer must explain **why there and not
elsewhere**.

**Construction required.** **Ingredients → a configuration argument → a comparative test.** The third
step is what converts a description into an argument.

**Lesson 17** builds it. ⚠️ **And this demand sits directly over the topic's worst book error:** ✗
**Leong classifies the tornado as a kind of tropical cyclone** at scan PDF p.119, and ✓ **C1 §9.4
contradicts its own named book without saying so**. **This edition states the conflict, names the
page, and teaches the supercell-mesocyclone chain** (N-06, N-07, N-10).

### A·6 — O6 · 2026 Mains GS-I, Question 4

**Demand, verbatim from C1's manual block.** *"What is the Fujiwhara effect? Explain its impact on
the movement and intensity of tropical cyclones."*

**Directive decoded.** ***What is … explain its impact*** · **10 marks · 150 words.**

**Construction required.** A **definition** followed by **two separable impacts**, **held apart**. The
stem separates movement from intensity and the evidence **differs in reliability between them**: the
movement effect is robust, the intensity effect is conditional. Merging them fails the second half.

**Lesson 16** builds it, promoting ✓ **C2 §4A** — the one place in either owner where the advanced
file **outranks an official glossary**. ⚠️ **Provenance clause required in the answer: the 300-750
nautical mile band and the 1921 date come from the NWS glossary, retrieved this pass as a SECONDARY
retrieval.**

### A·7 — O7 · 2026 Mains GS-I, Question 14

**Demand, verbatim from C1's manual block.** *"Compare the Loo, Chinook and Foehn winds with respect
to their regions of prevalence, their nature and climatic impacts."*

**Directive decoded.** ***Compare*** · **15 marks · 250 words.** The stem supplies the axes.

**Construction required.** **A classification held across all three columns** — not three
descriptions in sequence. The analytical move that lifts the answer is to state explicitly that
**two of the three are one mechanism and the third is another**.

**Lesson 10** builds it, and carries **the topic's central mechanism correction**: ✗ C1 calls the
saturated-then-dry adiabatic asymmetry "the single mechanism examiners test", while ✅ **Majid printed
3.24 says "the primary source of heat in the Chinook is the heat of compression"** and ✅ **Leong scan
PDF p.119 says "the air is compressed and warmed"**. **Two books, two independent passages, one
mechanism** (N-15).
