# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

One consolidated pass across the twenty lessons. **This edition runs in concept-check mode: there are
no compiled four-option questions, no option letters and no keys anywhere in it.** Each entry states
a **capability**, the **discrimination** it turns on, and the **lesson** that builds it.

## C·1 — Measurement and the register (Lessons 1, 6, 12, 20)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | State a climate definition with its period | ⇄ ✅ Leong **35 years** (scan PDF p.98) against ✅ Majid **30 years** (printed 3.51); the WMO normal is **30**; ✗ C1 gives **none**. **A climate statement without a base period is not a climate statement** |
| 2 | Read the Beaufort scale in two nomenclatures | ✗ **Five names moved from force 7 up.** "**Storm**" is force **11** in ✅ Leong and force **10** in 📄 the NWS. Force numbers and speeds are identical |
| 3 | Distinguish instrument from reading | A station maximum is a **Stevenson screen** value — louvred, white, ~1.25 m over grass, with a **double roof and an air space**. A rooftop thermometer is not a station reading |
| 4 | Use the isoline vocabulary | **isobar · isotherm · isohyet · isohel (sunshine) · isoneph (cloudiness) · isallobar (pressure CHANGE) · isanomaly (departure from the mean)**. ✗ Majid's Table 3.2 defines **isanomaly** as "equal barometric pressure" — the isobar's definition (N-03) |
| 5 | Convert a pressure value into a wind statement | ✅ "**Closely spaced isobars denote steep pressure gradient**." **Wind follows the gradient, never the value** |
| 6 | Use the okta correctly | A **unit of cloud amount**, one eighth of the sky. **Not a cloud type** |
| 7 | Attach provenance to a threshold | 📄 Heat wave at **40 °C plains / 30 °C hills**, at **2 stations** on **2 consecutive days**, declared on the **second**. ⚠️ **U-08: version currency unconfirmed** |

## C·2 — Radiation, temperature and the vertical (Lessons 2, 3, 4, 5)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 8 | State the radiation asymmetry | **Short in, long out; transparent in, opaque out; heated from below.** The Sun is hot so its beam is shortwave; the surface is cool so its emission is **thermal infrared** |
| 9 | Place albedo correctly | A property of the **surface**, not the place. **Water's albedo rises steeply at low solar elevation** — the inversion is the trap |
| 10 | Locate the dust maximum | **Arid and semi-arid surfaces**, not the humid tropics, which rainfall scavenges |
| 11 | Convert surplus and deficit into an obligation | The imbalance is **an instruction to transport energy poleward**, discharged by the three-cell circulation, the temperate cyclone and the jets |
| 12 | Read an isotherm bend | **January, Northern Hemisphere: poleward over sea, equatorward over land.** Magnitude is a **continentality index**. Altitude is a separate control |
| 13 | Handle superseded records | ✗ Al-Aziziyah **1922** decertified **13 September 2012**; standing record **56.7 °C, Death Valley, 10 July 1913** (⚠️ U-08, secondary). ✗ Alwar 1956 and Ganganagar 1935 superseded by **Phalodi, 51.0 °C, 19 May 2016** |
| 14 | Keep three lapse rates apart | **Environmental** (~6.5 °C/km; ✅ Majid **6.4**) describes **the surrounding air**; **dry adiabatic** (✅ **10 °C/km**) and **saturated** describe **a moving parcel** |
| 15 | Explain why weather is tropospheric | **Only a layer heated from below can overturn.** The stratosphere is heated **within and above itself** by ozone, so it is stable |
| 16 | State the tropopause structure | ~**16 km** equator, ~**11 km** mid-latitude, ~**8 km** pole, with a **break** where a jet sits — and **the highest tropopause is the coldest** |
| 17 | Define an inversion properly | A **negative lapse rate**. ✅ Majid lists **five** ground conditions including a **snow-covered surface** (✗ C1 gives four, N-17), and **four** types including the **frontal** (✗ C1 omits it, N-18) |
| 18 | State what an inversion does | ✅ It "**stops upward (ascent) and downward (descent) movements of air**" — **both** directions |

## C·3 — Forces, belts and winds (Lessons 6, 7, 8, 9, 10)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 19 | Enumerate the four forces | ✅ **Gravity** (vertical) · **pressure-gradient** (the only initiator) · **Coriolis** (deflective) · **friction** (to ~**500 m**) |
| 20 | State Coriolis properties | **Apparent · right-angled to motion · hemisphere-dependent · latitude-dependent · speed-dependent.** ✅ "**absent along the equator but increases progressively towards the poles**". **It does no work** |
| 21 | Separate a balance from a wave | **A geostrophic wind is a point balance; a Rossby wave exists because Coriolis VARIES with latitude.** ✗ Majid's heading "Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)" conflates them; ✗ C2 line 104 propagates it; ✓ C2 line 106 corrects it (N-01) |
| 22 | Explain surface convergence | **Friction** slows the wind, weakening Coriolis, so the wind crosses the isobars **into** the low. Frictionless air would circle a low forever |
| 23 | Count the belts | ✅ "**In all, there are seven pressure belts on the globe.**" ✗ C1 tabulates **four types** (N-31) |
| 24 | Classify a belt's genesis | **Thermal**: Equatorial Low, Polar Highs. **Dynamic**: Subtropical Highs, Sub-Polar Lows. **A subtropical high is not a cold place** |
| 25 | Name the trade-wind deserts | ✅ **Sahara, Kalahari, Atacama, Great Australian.** ✗ C1 substitutes the **Thar**, which is a **monsoon-margin and rain-shadow** desert (N-16) |
| 26 | Name the winds for both hemispheres | ✅ **South-Westerlies** north, **North-Westerlies** south; ✅ **Roaring Forties, Furious Fifties, Shrieking Sixties** from **40°S to 60°S** |
| 27 | Identify a daily reversal | ✅ Scale limit "**15 miles from the coast**" (≈ 24 km). The **valley wind blows UP** by day; the **mountain wind** drains down at night |
| 28 | Ground the Loo honestly | ✅ **One sentence in Majid printed 3.6**, with no time, season, speed or mechanism. ✗ C1's "3-6 pm, May-June" is **unsourced**; ✓ C2 warns against universalising it; **U-14: no first-party IMD definition** |
| 29 | Name the Foehn's primary term | ✅ "**The primary source of heat in the Chinook is the heat of compression**"; rainout is ✅ a **strengthening** term. ✗ C1 inverts them (N-15) |
| 30 | Place the family | ✅ **Foehn · Chinook · Zonda (120 km/h) · Santa Ana · Berg** — one mechanism. ✗ Majid puts the Berg in "Germany"; it is **southern African** (N-13) |

## C·4 — Moisture, cloud and precipitation (Lessons 11, 12, 13)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 31 | Separate the humidity measures | **Absolute** is per unit **volume** (✅ grams per cubic metre); **specific** is per unit **mass** and is conserved during ascent; **relative** is a **ratio with a moving denominator** |
| 32 | Explain the dawn maximum | ✅ Relative humidity's cycle has "**phase opposite to that of temperature**", peaking **near dawn** — **with no water added** |
| 33 | Explain the dewless cloudy night | The cloud **re-radiates longwave downward**, so the surface never reaches the **dew point**. **Calm** is equally necessary |
| 34 | State the water shares | ✅ Oceans **97 %**; the rest mostly ice; rivers, lakes and soil moisture **under 1 %**; the **atmosphere 0.35 %** — a small, fast store delivering a large flux |
| 35 | List the ten genera | ✅ **Cirrus · Cirrostratus · Cirrocumulus · Altostratus · Altocumulus · Stratocumulus · Nimbostratus · Cumulus · Cumulonimbus · Stratus**, high cloud at **20-40,000 ft** (≈ 6,000-12,000 m) |
| 36 | Read a genus as a mechanism | **Nimbostratus = raining layer = frontal.** **Cumulonimbus = raining heap = convective.** **Genus is a statement about rate** |
| 37 | Give the radiative sign | **Cloud-top temperature decides it.** High and cold → **warming**; low and warm → **cooling** |
| 38 | Derive orographic rain | **Dry** ascent to the condensation level → **saturated** ascent above → **dry** descent on the lee. **The maximum lies BELOW the crest** |
| 39 | Apply the alignment test | **Across the flow = rain maker and Foehn maker. Along the flow = barrier and climatic divide** |
| 40 | Keep two thresholds apart | ✅ Majid: a **wet day** is "at least **1 mm**". IMD's **rainy day** is **≥ 2.5 mm** — ⚠️ **no first-party anchor retrieved** (N-30, U-01) |

## C·5 — Severe weather (Lessons 14, 15, 16, 17)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 41 | Explain frontal lifting | A **density contrast**, not buoyancy and not relief. ✅ Cold front = **triangular spikes**; warm front = **rounded knobs** |
| 42 | State what ends a temperate cyclone | ✅ **Occlusion** — "the **lifting of the warm air wedge off the ground**" destroys the surface contrast that fuelled it. ✅ **3-10 days** |
| 43 | Place the frontal inversion | ✅ "**The warm air overlies the cold air**" — that **is** an inversion, and it is the rung ✗ C1 omits (N-18) |
| 44 | Name the tropical cyclone's fuel | ✅ **Latent heat**, not a density contrast. It **dies over land** because the fuel line is cut |
| 45 | Explain the eye | **Subsidence inside the eyewall, compressively warmed** — hence ✅ "**calm, rainless centres**" and a **warm core**, which is why ✅ pressure is "about **965 mb**" |
| 46 | List the six genesis gates | Warm ocean **through depth** · deep moisture · **Coriolis (not within ~5°)** · **low shear** · a seed disturbance · deep conditional instability. ✅ "**between 6° and 20°**" |
| 47 | Refuse the frequency inference | **Potential intensity** depends on ocean heat content; **genesis** depends on circulation. ⚠️ **U-02: no trend is asserted anywhere** |
| 48 | Define binary interaction | 📄 **300-750 nautical miles** (≈ 556-1,389 km), **Sakuhei Fujiwhara, 1921** — ⚠️ **secondary retrieval**. ✗ C1 drops the lower bound and the unit (N-33) |
| 49 | Correct the merger claim | ✓ "Merger increases **circulation size** more reliably than **maximum wind speed**." The centroid is **circulation-weighted**, not geometric |
| 50 | Give five supercell ingredients | Warm moist low-level air · **dry air above** · steep lapse rate · **strong shear** · a trigger — plus a **cap**. **Shear destroys a tropical cyclone and creates a supercell** |
| 51 | Classify the tornado | **A mesoscale vortex of a severe thunderstorm.** ✗ Leong calls it "a different kind of tropical cyclone" (N-06); ✗ his 500 m.p.h. is not reproduced (N-07); ✗ the willy-willy is a **dust devil** (N-10) |
| 52 | Correct the tornado's causality | ✗ Majid's glossary: "developed in response to extremely low pressure". **The deficit is a CONSEQUENCE of rotation under cyclostrophic balance** (N-04) |
| 53 | Argue a concentration | **Ingredients → configuration → comparative test.** North America has **no east-west barrier**; **South Asia's Himalaya is exactly that barrier** |
| 54 | State the cloudburst rule | **Mechanism is Topic 13's** (2024 GS-I Q6); **flood consequence is Disaster Management's** (2022 GS-III Q8). **The physics is identical; the directive word separates them** |

## C·6 — Upper air and India (Lessons 18, 19, 20)

| # | Capability | The discrimination |
|---:|---|---|
| 55 | Define a jet properly | **A narrow, fast, concentrated core of upper-tropospheric wind associated with a steep thermal gradient.** ✗ Majid's "blowing **from west to east**" excludes the TEJ his own India volume explains (N-19) |
| 56 | Quantify a jet | ✅ **160-480 km** wide, **900-2,150 m** thick, near the tropopause at "**about 12,000 m**", core above **300 kmph**, mean **110 kmph summer / 190 kmph winter**, exceptional **375 kmph** |
| 57 | Explain the seasonal speed signal | **A jet's speed is a reading of the thermal gradient beneath it**, which is steepest in winter |
| 58 | Compare zonal and meridional flow | Low amplitude → fast steering, moderate extremes. High amplitude → **persistence**, **blocking**, **severe extremes**. ✓ "**Most damaging weather extremes are extremes of duration**" |
| 59 | State the bifurcation | ✅ Southern branch "**about 240 km p.h. at 200 mb**" against ✅ "**70 to 90 km p.h.**" northern. ✗ C2's "~25 deg N **in notes**" is a hedge (N-21) |
| 60 | Trace the anti-cyclogenesis chain | ✅ **High-level heat source → warm-core anticyclone at 500 mb → outflow east-to-west on the southern flank = the TEJ**, ✅ "stationed at **15°N**". ✗ The **jet's core is upper-tropospheric**, not mid (N-20) |
| 61 | Narrow the causal claim | ✅ "**first indication**" · ✅ "**disappears completely**" · ✅ onset "prompted by the **hydro-dynamic effect**". ✗ **None of them is C2's "pre-condition"** (N-22) |
| 62 | Define a western disturbance structurally | 📄 A **lower-tropospheric cyclonic circulation** beneath a **trough in the middle and upper tropospheric westerlies**, at **74°E north of 28°N**. ✗ **C2 defines it by origin alone** |
| 63 | Give the official footprint | 📄 **Ten** jurisdictions. ✗ C2 omits **Ladakh, Chandigarh and Rajasthan** |
| 64 | Handle Kendrew honestly | ✅ "**3 to 5 per month**" against ✅ **2-4-5-5-4**, which disagree (N-23); and the series is **mid-20th-century** (N-24). ✗ C2 carries **no** figure |
| 65 | State both extreme criteria | 📄 **Heat**: 40/30 °C entry, 4.5-6.4 and > 6.4 departure, ≥ 45 and ≥ 47 actual, 37 °C coastal, **2 stations / 2 days / second day**, **warm night**. 📄 **Cold**: 10/0 °C entry, same departure bands, ≤ 4 and ≤ 2 actual, ≤ 15 °C coastal, **November-March** |
| 66 | Date the institutions | 📄 **Bharat Forecast System: IITM Pune, 12 km → 6 km**, PIB **2131392**, **26 May 2025**. 📄 **Mission Mausam: MoES, ₹2,000 crore, IMD / IITM Pune / NCMRWF / INCOIS / NIOT.** ⚠️ **No radar count** (U-10); **use 64 %, not 67** (U-11) |

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Two parts. **Part I** carries the **seven owned Mains models** at their stated caps. **Part II**
carries the twenty **original** questions built in the lessons, listed with their unique rubrics so
that the whole practice set can be seen at once.

**The construction table, which is the point of Part I.** This is the first topic in this lane
carrying **seven owned Mains models**, and they must be **visibly different in construction**. **No
two share a spine.**

| # | Demand | Directive | Marks / cap | **Spine shape** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **O1** | 2021 GS-I Q14 · mountain alignment and local weather | *Briefly mention and explain* | 15 / ≤250 | **Short inventory, then one organising variable applied back across it** |
| **O2** | 2022 GS-I Q17 · the troposphere as the weather layer | *How* | 15 / ≤250 | **A single mechanism traced through one layer, proved by a contrast** |
| **O3** | 2024 GS-I Q4 · SST rise and cyclone formation | *Explain* (causal relation) | 10 / ≤150 | **A chain shown, then bounded by a qualification** |
| **O4** | 2024 GS-I Q6 · the phenomenon of cloudbursts | *Explain* (a phenomenon) | 10 / ≤150 | **Definition → criterion → mechanism** |
| **O5** | 2024 GS-I Q16 · Twisters and their concentration | *Explain* (a spatial concentration) | 15 / ≤250 | **Ingredients → configuration argument → comparative test** |
| **O6** | 2026 GS-I Q4 · the Fujiwhara effect and its impact | *What is … explain its impact* | 10 / ≤150 | **Definition → two separable impacts of differing confidence** |
| **O7** | 2026 GS-I Q14 · Loo, Chinook and Foehn compared | *Compare* | 15 / ≤250 | **A classification sustained across three columns** |

---

## PART I — THE SEVEN OWNED MAINS MODELS

### O1 · 2021 GS-I Q14 — 15 marks, 250 words

**Question.** Briefly mention the alignment of major mountain ranges of the world and explain their
impact on local weather.

**Spine: short inventory, then one organising variable applied back across it.**

The world's major ranges fall into two alignment classes, and the class decides the weather. The
**Rockies** and the **Andes** run **north-south**; the **Himalaya** and the **Alps** run broadly
**west-east**; the **Western Ghats** and Australia's **Great Dividing Range** run north-south along
their coasts; the **Scandinavian Highlands** run north-east to south-west.

The organising variable is whether a range lies **across** the prevailing flow or **along** it.

A range **across** the flow forces ascent. The parcel cools at the **dry adiabatic rate** to its
condensation level and at the slower **saturated rate** above it, precipitating on the windward slope
— with the maximum usually **below the crest**, since the upper slope receives already-depleted air.
Descending the lee, it warms at the **dry rate**, so the leeward foot is **hotter and drier** than the
windward foot. ✅ Majid records the consequence as "a narrow zone about **300 km east of the crest of
the Rockies**" where the **Chinook** blows, and ✅ Leong lists **Atacama** and the **Great
Australian** deserts among trade-wind deserts in such lee positions. The **Western Ghats** reproduce
it exactly: a drenched western scarp and a dry Deccan at the same latitude.

A range **along** the flow lifts little and instead acts as a **barrier and climatic divide** — the
Himalaya's role in winter, when it separates two regimes and, as it does so, splits the subtropical
jet itself. *(233 words)*

### O2 · 2022 GS-I Q17 — 15 marks, 250 words

**Question.** How does the troposphere determine weather processes?

**Spine: a single mechanism traced through one layer, proved by a contrast.** ⚠️ **C1 supplies no
spine for this demand; it is built here.**

The troposphere determines weather because of **where its heat source is**.

The atmosphere is largely transparent to incoming **shortwave** radiation and substantially opaque to
outgoing **longwave**, so the solar beam is absorbed overwhelmingly **at the surface**, and the
surface — being cool — radiates in the **thermal infrared**, which the lowest air absorbs. **The
troposphere is therefore heated from below.**

A layer heated at its base must have temperature **falling with height**, and it does: at about
**6.5 °C per kilometre**, with ✅ Majid giving "**6.4 °C per 1000 m**" at printed 3.8 and again in his
glossary. Warm, less dense air lying beneath cold, denser air is an **unstable** arrangement, so the
layer **overturns**: parcels rise, denser air sinks to replace them, and **convection becomes
possible**.

Overturning is decisive because this layer also holds **most of the atmosphere's mass and nearly all
its water vapour**. Rising air cools, reaches **saturation**, condenses into cloud, precipitates, and
releases **latent heat** that drives further ascent.

The **contrast proves it**. The stratosphere is heated **within and above itself**, by ozone absorbing
ultraviolet, so its temperature **rises** with height; warm air lies on cold; a displaced parcel
returns; it **cannot overturn**, and has no weather. Its dryness is a consequence of that stability.

The **tropopause** therefore acts as a lid, which is why convective towers spread into **anvils**
rather than continuing upward. *(248 words)*

### O3 · 2024 GS-I Q4 — 10 marks, 150 words

**Question.** Explain the relationship between a rise in sea surface temperature and the formation of
tropical cyclones.

**Spine: a chain shown, then bounded.**

A tropical cyclone is a **latent-heat engine**. ✅ Majid (printed 3.30) names "an abundant supply of
water vapour and thus the necessary **latent heat** to fuel these storms". A warmer sea evaporates
more vigorously; the vapour is carried inward and lifted in the **eyewall**; condensation releases
heat that **warms the core aloft**; a warmer column weighs less, so surface pressure falls — ✅ Leong
records "about **965 mb**" — and the stronger inflow evaporates still more. A higher ocean heat
content therefore raises a storm's **potential intensity**.

**The inference stops there.** Genesis requires five further conditions: deep moisture, sufficient
**Coriolis** — ✅ "between **6° and 20°**" — **low vertical shear**, a seed disturbance, and deep
conditional instability. Three are set by **circulation**, not temperature. **No claim about storm
numbers or trends follows.** *(135 words)*

### O4 · 2024 GS-I Q6 — 10 marks, 150 words

**Question.** Explain the phenomenon of 'Cloudbursts'.

**Spine: definition → criterion → mechanism.**

A **cloudburst** is an extremely intense, short-duration, highly localised rainfall event produced by
a deep convective column.

⚠️ **The criterion, with its provenance.** It is commonly defined as about **100 mm of rainfall in
one hour over roughly 20-30 km²**. ⚠️ **No local book states it, and the first-party IMD glossary page
was not retrievable**, so it is carried as an operational threshold and not as a verified figure.

**The mechanism has four steps.** A deep convective column develops a very strong updraught, which
**suspends a large water load** that cannot fall while ascent exceeds droplet fall speed. Over steep
relief, **orographic lifting superposes on convective lifting**, deepening the column and **anchoring
it to one place**. When the updraught collapses or the storm stalls, the entire load is **released at
once** over a very small area.

Its scale lies **below routine forecast resolution**. *(146 words)*

### O5 · 2024 GS-I Q16 — 15 marks, 250 words

**Question.** Explain the phenomenon of 'Twisters' and their concentration around the Gulf of Mexico.

**Spine: ingredients → configuration argument → comparative test.**

A twister, or **tornado**, is a **mesoscale rotating column descending from a severe thunderstorm's
rotating updraught, the mesocyclone** — ✅ named in Majid's glossary. It is **not** a tropical cyclone:
✅ it is "seldom larger than a **kilometre** in diameter", lasts minutes, forms over **land**, and is
in **cyclostrophic** balance, which is why ✅ its rotation is counterclockwise in the north "**except
in rare instance**".

**Five ingredients must coincide**: warm moist low-level air; **dry air above it**; a steep lapse
rate; **strong vertical wind shear**, which creates horizontal spin that a strong updraught **tilts
into the vertical**; and a lifting trigger — with a **cap** that stores instability for explosive
release.

**Concentration is a configuration argument.** The ingredients arrive from different directions, so a
belt exists only where a continent delivers them repeatedly to one corridor. North America does. The
**Gulf of Mexico** supplies warm moist air along an **unobstructed northward land corridor**. The
**Rockies**, lying **across** the westerlies, spill **dry elevated air** eastward and generate a **lee
trough**. Their boundary is the **dryline**, itself a trigger. A **mid-latitude jet** overhead
supplies the shear. And — decisively — **there is no east-west mountain barrier**, so cold dry polar
air streams south unobstructed.

**The comparative test confirms it.** South Asia has warmth, moisture and depth, but the **Himalaya
is exactly that east-west barrier**, so the cold dry supply is blocked and the ingredients assemble
only briefly. ⚠️ No Indian count is stated. *(247 words)*

### O6 · 2026 GS-I Q4 — 10 marks, 150 words

**Question.** What is the Fujiwhara effect? Explain its impact on the movement and intensity of
tropical cyclones.

**Spine: definition → two separable impacts of differing confidence.**

**Definition.** When two tropical cyclones come within a certain distance, each lies inside the
other's circulation and is carried by it, so both orbit a **common centroid** — counterclockwise in
the Northern Hemisphere. 📄 The NWS gives the band as **300-750 nautical miles** (≈ 556-1,389 km),
"depending on the sizes of the cyclones", naming **Sakuhei Fujiwhara**, **1921** — ⚠️ a secondary
retrieval.

**Impact on movement — robust.** ✓ The centroid is "**weighted by vortex circulation and structure**"
and need **not** lie midway, so the smaller storm travels the longer arc. Four outcomes alter the
track — mutual orbit, absorption, elastic deflection and straining out — and only escape does not.

**Impact on intensity — conditional.** ✓ "Merger increases **circulation size** more reliably than
**maximum wind speed**." Interaction may equally **weaken** a storm, since shear tilts the vortex and
displaces its warm core. *(138 words)*

### O7 · 2026 GS-I Q14 — 15 marks, 250 words

**Question.** Compare the Loo, Chinook and Foehn winds with respect to their regions of prevalence,
their nature and climatic impacts.

**Spine: a classification sustained across three columns.**

**The classification first, because it decides everything.** The **Chinook and the Foehn are one
mechanism under two names**; the **Loo is a different mechanism altogether**. ✅ Leong's own 1967
Cambridge paper pairs them as "**Fohn (Chinook) winds**".

**Regions.** The **Loo** blows over the **Satluj-Ganga plain** of north-west India and Pakistan. The
**Chinook** occupies ✅ "a narrow zone about **300 kilometers east of the crest of the Rockies**, from
New Mexico north into Canada". The **Foehn** occupies the ✅ **Alpine** leeward valleys, with ✅ the
**Zonda** on the Andean lee and ✅ **Santa Ana** in southern California as the same family.

**Nature.** The Chinook and Foehn are **relief-forced compressive descents**: ✅ "the **primary source
of heat** … is the **heat of compression**", the air heating at ✅ the **dry adiabatic rate of
10 °C/km**, ✅ **strengthened** by windward rainout. ✅ Leong: "the air is **compressed and warmed**".
The **Loo has no mountain, no ascent and no descent**; it is hot because the plain it crosses is hot.

**Climatic impacts.** ✅ The Chinook raised Spearfish from **−20 °C to 7 °C in two minutes**, removes
✅ "**half metre of snow in a single day**", and ✅ "frequent Chinooks means **winter is mild**". ✅ The
Foehn raises Alpine temperature "**15° to 30 °F within an hour**" and ✅ "causes **avalanches**". The
**Loo desiccates** a plain already at ✅ **18 °C** diurnal range. *(236 words)*

# REMEDIATION

**Forty corrections, restated as a student-facing error list with page locations.** Each is applied in
the lesson named in its row. The arrangement is by **kind of error**, because the kind is what a
student needs to recognise.

## R·A — Mechanism and physical-sign errors (six, and every one sits under an owned demand)

| ID | The error, and where it is printed | What is correct | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---:|
| **N-01** | ✗ **Majid IWG printed 3.12 (scan PDF p.85)** heads a section "**Geostrophic Winds (Rossby Waves)**" and defines **only geostrophic balance** beneath it: "winds moving between pressure areas along the paths that are **parallel to the isobars** … the **pressure gradient force equals the Coriolis force**". ✗ **C2 line 104 repeats the equation by citation** | **They are different objects.** A **geostrophic wind is a point balance**; a **Rossby wave is a planetary wave that exists because the Coriolis parameter VARIES with latitude** — the beta effect, which is precisely the term geostrophic balance omits. ✓ **C2 line 106 already states the correct definition**, so the owner carries both the error and its corrective | **7** |
| **N-04** | ✗ **Majid IWG printed 3.52 (scan PDF p.125)**, glossary: a tornado is "an intense, destructive cyclonic rotation, **developed in response to extremely low pressure**" | **The causality is reversed.** The pressure deficit is a **consequence** of the rotation under **cyclostrophic** balance, not its cause. ✅ The same entry correctly adds "**associated with mesocyclone formation**", and **that half is adopted** | **17** |
| **N-05** | ✗ **Majid IWG printed 3.33 (scan PDF p.106)**: "the tornado depends on moisture as an energy supply and **occurs mostly in moist tropical air**" | **Tornadoes are overwhelmingly a mid-latitude continental phenomenon.** ✅ Majid's own scan PDF p.107, though OCR-degraded, legibly puts the ideal setting **ahead of a cold front** where warm moist air meets "**cooler, drier air from polar regions**" | **17** |
| **N-06** | ✗ **Leong scan PDF p.119**: "**Tropical cyclones, typhoons, hurricanes and tornadoes — All these are different kinds of tropical cyclones**", and "tornadoes are small but very violent **tropical [or] sub-tropical cyclones**" | **A tornado is a mesoscale vortex of a severe thunderstorm.** Different scale, lifetime, parent, fuel, surface, force balance and core. ✓ **C1 §9.4 is right and contradicts its own named book silently; this edition states the conflict** | **17** |
| **N-15** | ✗ **C1 lines 116-122** call the saturated-then-dry adiabatic asymmetry "**the single mechanism examiners test**" and attribute it to its named books | **Both named books say the opposite and agree with each other.** ✅ **Majid printed 3.24: "the PRIMARY SOURCE OF HEAT in the Chinook is the HEAT OF COMPRESSION"**, with rainout as a ✅ **strengthening** term; ✅ **Leong scan PDF p.119: "the air is COMPRESSED AND WARMED."** Teach **compression as primary, rainout as amplifier** | **10** |
| **N-20** | ✗ **Majid *Geography of India* printed 4.8 (scan PDF p.69)**: the easterlies blow "in the **mid-troposphere**" — having just placed the Tibetan warm-core anticyclone at **500 mb** | **Mid-troposphere describes the anticyclone, not the jet.** The **TEJ's core is upper-tropospheric, around 150-200 hPa**. ✅ Koteshwaram's own caption reads "Axis of the Easterly and Westerly Jet stream **at 12 km**". **Keep the anti-cyclogenesis mechanism; correct the level** | **19** |

## R·B — Obsolete values, records and nomenclature (five)

| ID | The error | What is correct | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---:|
| **N-08** | ✗ **Leong scan PDF p.105 and its duplicate p.107** print Beaufort force names that are the **superseded nomenclature**: 2 Slight Breeze, 7 Moderate Gale, 8 Fresh Gale, 9 Strong Gale, 10 Whole Gale, 11 Storm | 📄 **NWS/WMO: 2 Light Breeze, 7 Near Gale, 8 Gale, 9 Severe Gale, 10 Storm, 11 Violent Storm.** **The force numbers and speed bands are identical; only the labels moved, by one position from force 7 up.** "Storm" is force **11** in Leong and **10** in the NWS | **1** |
| **N-09** | ✗ **Leong scan PDF p.105 and p.119**: the hurricane threshold is "**more than 75 m.p.h.**" | 📄 **The standard is 64 knots**, which the NWS renders as **72-83 mph at force 12** (≈ 116-134 km/h). State Leong's as the **book's** and the knot threshold as **the standard** | **1**, 15 |
| **N-10** | ✗ **Leong scan PDF p.119** calls the **willy-willy** a tropical cyclone of north-western Australia | ⚠️ **In modern Australian usage a willy-willy is a DUST DEVIL**; Australian tropical cyclones are called tropical cyclones. The two differ in **scale, energy source and duration** | **17** |
| **N-11** | ✗ **Majid IWG printed 3.8 (scan PDF p.81)**: a **56 °C** afternoon maximum in **Libya**, **September 1922**, with **Al-Aziziyah** named on the same page | ⚠️ **The WMO decertified the Al-Aziziyah 1922 record on 13 September 2012.** The standing record is **56.7 °C at Greenland Ranch (Furnace Creek), Death Valley, 10 July 1913**. ⚠️ **U-08: the WMO page was not retrieved first-party; this is a corroborated secondary correction** | **3** |
| **N-12** | ✗ **Khullar printed 135 (scan PDF p.84; folio not read)**: "the highest temperatures so far recorded are **50.5 °C at Alwar on 10th May, 1956 and 50.6 °C at Ganganagar on 14th June, 1935**" | ⚠️ **India's recognised national maximum is 51.0 °C at Phalodi, Rajasthan, on 19 May 2016.** **Keep Khullar's monthly heat march; replace the record and show the old pair as superseded** | **3** |

## R·C — Definitional, taxonomic and quantitative conflicts (fourteen)

| ID | The conflict | Disposition | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---:|
| **N-02** | ✅ **Majid states 6.4 °C per 1000 m twice** (printed 3.8 and 3.52); ✗ **C2 line 37 says "about 6.5 deg C/km IN THE NOTES"** — a hedge; the **ICAO/WMO standard is 6.5 °C/km** | **Show both, attribute both, teach 6.5.** The normal lapse rate is an **average**, not a law — which is why an inversion can exist | **4** |
| **N-03** | ✗ **Majid's Table 3.2, printed 3.51**, defines "**Isanomaly**" as "**Equal barometric pressure**" — the isobar's definition, given correctly in the very next row | An **isanomaly** joins points of equal **departure from the mean**, normally of temperature. **The defect is shown and not reproduced** | **1** |
| **N-13** | ✗ **Majid printed 3.6 (scan PDF p.79)** lists "**Berg (Germany)**" | The **Berg wind is southern African** — a foehn-type descent off the interior escarpment. **Marked at its true location on M6 with the book's claim flagged** | **9**, 10 |
| **N-14** | ✗ **Majid contradicts himself within two pages**: printed 3.24 says the Chinook means "**Snow eating wind**"; his **Table 3.1 at printed 3.25** says "**ice-eater**". ✅ Leong says "**the snow-eater**" | **Both cells are shown and neither is asserted** as the book's settled claim | **10** |
| **N-16** | ✗ **C1 line 225** gives the trade-wind deserts as "Sahara, **Thar**, Kalahari" | ✅ **Leong scan PDF p.117: "Sahara, Kalahari, Atacama and the Great Australian Deserts."** The **Thar is a monsoon-margin and rain-shadow desert**; the substitution is unsourced **and physically weaker** | **8** |
| **N-17** | ✗ **C1 line 69** gives **four** ground-inversion conditions | ✅ **Majid printed 3.9 gives five**, the fifth being a **snow-covered surface** — the condition the western Himalaya satisfies after a disturbance clears | **5** |
| **N-18** | ✗ **C1 lines 73-77** give radiation, valley and subsidence inversions | ✅ **Majid printed 3.9 gives low-level, upper-air and FRONTAL.** Two consequences in opposite directions: Majid's **upper-air type explicitly covers subsidence**, so **C1's ⚠️ can be raised to ✅**; but **C1 has no frontal rung**, and **the frontal inversion is the western disturbance's own** | **5** |
| **N-19** | ✗ **Majid IWG printed 3.35** defines a jet as "blowing **from west to east**" and lists **three types, none easterly**; ✅ **Majid *Geography of India* printed 4.8** then explains the **tropical easterly jet** | **The same author, two books, one object excluded by the first and explained by the second.** ✗ C2 asserts the TEJ as book-grounded without noticing. **Teach the definitional gap as the finding** | **18** |
| **N-21** | ✗ **C2 line 20**: the STWJ "south of Himalayas around **~25 deg N in notes**" — a hedge, not a source | ✅ **Khullar's Fig. 5.4 carries reference parallels at 23½°N and 40°N** (printed 125-126, **folios read**), and his text places the winter jet "along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**" | **19** |
| **N-22** | ✗ **C2 line 30**: the jet's northward withdrawal is a "**pre-condition**" for monsoon onset | **Three sources, three weaker claims.** ✅ Khullar: "the **first indication**". ✅ Maung Tun Yin: it "**disappears completely**". ✅ Majid: onset is "prompted by the **hydro-dynamic effect** of the Himalayas and **not** by the thermally induced low pressure centre". **None is "pre-condition"** | **19** |
| **N-23** | ✗ **Khullar printed 134's two frequency statements disagree**: "on an average **3 to 5 disturbances per month**" against **Kendrew's 2 (Nov), 4 (Dec), 5 (Jan), 5 (Feb), 4 (Mar)** — whose November value falls **below** the stated floor and whose series averages four | **Show both; assert neither.** ✗ **C2 carries no WD frequency figure at all** | **20** |
| **N-27** | ✗ **C1 lines 246-248** give the heat-wave criterion as the **plains 40 °C threshold only** | 📄 The criterion additionally fixes **hills at 30 °C**, **coastal at 37 °C with a 4.5 °C departure**, departure bands of **4.5-6.4** and **> 6.4 °C**, actual bands of **≥ 45** and **≥ 47 °C**, the **2-station / 2-consecutive-day / second-day** declaration rule, and the **warm night** | **20** |
| **N-28** | ✗ **Neither owner states any cold-wave criterion**, though the cold wave is the defining hazard of the season this topic's India half owns | 📄 **Plains ≤ 10 °C, hills ≤ 0 °C; negative departure 4.5-6.4 and > 6.4 °C; actual minimum ≤ 4 °C and ≤ 2 °C for plains; coastal ≤ 15 °C with a −4.5 °C departure; season November to March**, casualties December to March | **20** |
| **N-29** | ⇄ **The averaging period is given as 35 years by ✅ Leong (scan PDF p.98) and 30 by ✅ Majid (printed 3.51), and ✗ C1 gives none** | **The WMO climatological normal is 30. Teach 30; show 35 as the book's** | **1** |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

**Why this section exists in the form it does.** `GEOGRAPHY-MAP-LOCATION-BANK.md` was measured at
audit and re-hashed unchanged at generation. Of **169** weather assets this topic needs, **148 are
absent**, **21 match lexically** and **five of those 21 are lexical false positives** — `ridge` inside
"Adam's Bridge", `loo` inside "Floods", `bora` inside "Tambora", `norther` inside "northern", `okta`
inside "Loktak".

> **What the bank actually supplies, in full — sixteen rows, every one a container, a range or a
> sea:** Mediterranean · Caspian Sea · Black Sea · Iran · Afghanistan · Hindu Kush · Karakoram · Pir
> Panjal · Kashmir Valley · Srinagar · Shimla · Dras · Leh · Western Ghats (with "Orographic rain" in
> its significance cell) · Gulf of Mexico (inside the Mississippi row) · Caribbean Sea (with
> "Hurricanes" in its significance cell).

**Not one weather process, system, index, instrument, isoline, institution, local wind or
severe-weather term is in the bank at all.** This is the weakest inheritance any topic in this lane
has received. **The bank is not owned by this lane and has not been edited.** The gap is closed
**internally**, through the seven map assets below and the asset register that follows them.

---

own location with its season and thermal character.

```text
   M6 — THE NAMED WINDS OF THE WORLD, BY MECHANISM FAMILY

   ■ FAMILY 1 — RELIEF-FORCED COMPRESSIVE DESCENTS (Lesson 10)
     ✦ CHINOOK — ✅ "a narrow zone about 300 KILOMETERS EAST OF THE CREST OF
       THE ROCKIES from New Mexico north into Canada". ✦ SPEARFISH, SOUTH
       DAKOTA pinned: ✅ −20 °C to 7 °C in TWO MINUTES, 1943. ✅ gusts to
       160 km/h · ✅ removes "half metre of snow in a single day" · ✅ the
       CHINOOK WALL CLOUD marked over the crest.
     ✦ FOEHN — the ALPINE leeward valleys. ✅ "15° to 30 °F within an hour"
       (≈ 8-17 °C) · ✅ "melts snow and causes AVALANCHES" ↪ the SNOWPACK is
       Geography/advanced/06's.
     ✦ ZONDA — the ANDEAN lee, Argentina. ✅ "can attain 120 KILOMETER PER
       HOUR … comparable to Chinook and Fohn".
     ✦ SANTA ANA — SOUTHERN CALIFORNIA, placed by ✅ Table 3.1 UNDER THE FOHN
       ROW, which is why the row is a FAMILY and not a wind.
     ✦ BERG WIND — marked on the SOUTHERN AFRICAN escarpment, with ✗ Majid's
       "Germany" attribution FLAGGED ON THE MAP AS THE SOURCE'S CLAIM (N-13).

   ■ FAMILY 2 — COLD OUTBREAKS
     ✦ BORA — the ADRIATIC. ✅ Trieste 36 days a year; mean 50 km/h winter and
       40 km/h summer; up to 100 km/h in winter.
     ✦ MISTRAL — the RHÔNE VALLEY, ✅ "master wind", "may reach a speed of
       100 KM PER HOUR", with the ✦ BISE noted beside it.
     ✦ NORTHER — TEXAS, from the GULF OF MEXICO to the western CARIBBEAN SEA.
     ✦ PAMPERO — the PAMPAS, ✅ "Southern Hemisphere equivalent of the
       Norther".

   ■ FAMILY 3 — HOT SOURCE-REGION WINDS
     ✦ KHAMSIN — Egypt and the eastern Sahara. ✅ "Regularly blows at a
       50-DAY PERIOD (Khamsin = 50). Temperature varies between 40-50 °C",
       with ✦ GIBLI (Libya), ✦ SIROCCO and ✦ LEVECHE as adiabatic variants.
     ✦ HARMATTAN — WEST AFRICA. ✅ "also known as DOCTOR as it reduces the
       relative humidity in the coast countries of GULF OF GUINEA".
     ✦ HABOOB — southern SAHARA and SUDAN. ✅ Majid: "Hot, DAMP wind often
       containing sand and dust", "3 hrs", "24 per year", with the advance of
       the ITCZ. ✗ N-34: the standard description — a DRY thunderstorm-outflow
       DENSITY CURRENT — is printed beside the cell; NEITHER IS ASSERTED.
     ✦ ETESIAN — the EASTERN MEDITERRANEAN, cool dry north-easterly of summer
       and early autumn.
     ✦ LEVANTER — the STRAIT OF GIBRALTAR, damp and foggy for perhaps two days.
     ✦ THE LOO — the SATLUJ-GANGA PLAIN, DRAWN WITH NO SLOPE AT ALL, the one
       wind on this map with NO LEEWARD-DESCENT STAGE. ⚠️ U-14: no first-party
       definition retrieved; ✗ C1's clock-time re-tiered (N-26).

   ■ FAMILY 4 — DIURNAL THERMAL REVERSALS (Lesson 9), drawn as insets
     ✦ SEA BREEZE / LAND BREEZE — ✅ "does not normally exceed 15 MILES FROM
       THE COAST" (≈ 24 km).
     ✦ VALLEY WIND (ANABATIC, upslope, daytime) / MOUNTAIN WIND (KATABATIC,
       downslope, "the NIGHT BREEZE"). ✅ "KATABATIC: Air drainage from
       elevated regions, flowing as GRAVITY WINDS."
     ✦ ORCHARDS marked ON THE SLOPE, not the floor: ✅ "the valley slopes are
       the preferred places of ORCHARDS."

   ■ FAMILY 5 — INDIAN PRE-MONSOON NAMED WINDS AND SHOWERS, marked and routed
```

## THE SINGLE CAUSAL SPINE — FROM INSOLATION TO THE WESTERN DISTURBANCE

```text
   THE WHOLE TOPIC AS ONE CHAIN. EVERY ARROW IS A LESSON.

   [L2]  THE SUN DELIVERS SHORTWAVE RADIATION UNEQUALLY ACROSS LATITUDE
          │ the atmosphere is transparent to it and opaque to the longwave
          │ the surface returns → THE AIR IS HEATED FROM BELOW
          ▼
   [L3]  THE SURFACE TEMPERATURE FIELD IS THEREFORE UNEQUAL
          │ isotherms bend at coasts because land and sea differ in specific
          │ heat, mixing, penetration depth and currents
          ▼
   [L4]  TEMPERATURE FALLS WITH HEIGHT IN THE HEATED LAYER → THE TROPOSPHERE
          │ unstable, overturning, wet; capped by the TROPOPAUSE
          ▼
   [L5]  WHERE THE PROFILE INVERTS, EXCHANGE STOPS → INVERSION, A LID
          │ fog, frost, haze, cold confined beneath it
          ▼
   [L6]  UNEQUAL TEMPERATURE PRODUCES UNEQUAL PRESSURE
          │ and the GRADIENT, not the value, is the force
          ▼
   [L7]  FOUR FORCES ACT: PGF STARTS IT, CORIOLIS BENDS IT, FRICTION DRAGS IT
          │ → geostrophic flow above ~500 m; convergence into lows below it
          │ → and because CORIOLIS VARIES WITH LATITUDE, PLANETARY WAVES EXIST
          ▼
   [L8]  THE RESULT IS SEVEN BELTS AND THREE CELLS, DISCHARGING THE POLEWARD
          │ TRANSPORT OBLIGATION [L2] IMPOSED
          ├──► [L9]  within them, DAILY THERMAL REVERSALS: land/sea, slope
          └──► [L10] and RELIEF-FORCED COMPRESSIVE DESCENTS: the Foehn family
          ▼
   [L11] MOVING AIR CARRIES MOISTURE; COOLING IT TO ITS DEW POINT CONDENSES IT
          ▼
   [L12] CONDENSATION MAKES CLOUD, WHOSE FORM RECORDS THE LIFTING AND WHOSE
          │ HEIGHT DECIDES ITS RADIATIVE SIGN
          ▼
   [L13] SUSTAINED LIFTING MAKES RAIN: CONVECTIONAL, OROGRAPHIC, FRONTAL
          │ and only the OROGRAPHIC is fixed to a place
          ▼
   [L14] WHERE TWO AIR MASSES MEET, THE LIGHTER RIDES OVER THE DENSER →
          │ FRONTS, CYCLOGENESIS, THE TEMPERATE CYCLONE
          ├──► [L15] in the tropics, a different engine: LATENT HEAT, warm
          │           core, eye and eyewall
          ├──► [L16] two such vortices steer each other: FUJIWHARA
          └──► [L17] two orders of magnitude down: SUPERCELL, TORNADO,
                      CLOUDBURST, HAIL
          ▼
   [L18] ALL OF IT IS STEERED FROM ABOVE BY JETS, WHICH SIT WHERE THE THERMAL
          │ GRADIENT IS STEEPEST, AND BY THE WAVES THEY RUN IN
          ▼
   [L19] OVER ASIA, A PLATEAU 5 km HIGH SPLITS THE WINTER JET AND BECOMES A
          │ HEAT SOURCE IN SUMMER, PRODUCING TWO JETS IN OPPOSITE DIRECTIONS
          ▼
   [L20] ALONG THE WINTER JET TRAVELS THE WESTERN DISTURBANCE — A TWO-LEVEL
          OBJECT, A CIRCULATION BENEATH A TROUGH IN THE WESTERLIES — WHOSE
          ARRIVAL, PRECIPITATION, FOG AND SUBSEQUENT COLD ARE ALL MEASURED
          AGAINST CRITERIA SET BY AN INSTITUTION, ON A GRID NOW REFINED FROM
          12 km TO 6 km.
          ▼
   [L1]  AND EVERY NUMBER IN THE CHAIN CAME FROM AN INSTRUMENT, A CONVENTION
          AND A DATE. THE LOOP CLOSES WHERE IT OPENED.
```
