# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

**The sixth H1 and the last teaching section of this edition. Nothing is taught after this point —
`COVERAGE MATRIX` and `SOURCE LEDGER` are apparatus.** Headings below are **topic-specific**: they
name what this topic actually contains rather than following a template, and they do not repeat the
teaching introductions.

---

## RN·1 — The measurement register: what a weather number is made of

- **Climate = statistics of weather over a stated period.** ⇄ ✅ Leong **35 years** (scan PDF p.98) ·
  ✅ Majid **30 years** (printed 3.51) · WMO normal **30** · ✗ **C1 gives none** (N-29). **Teach 30.**
- **Stevenson screen:** louvred, white, ~1.25 m over grass, ✅ **double roof with an air space**
  (Leong scan PDF p.102). Without it you measure the box.
- **Rain gauge:** sited clear of obstacles by ✅ **at least twice their height** (scan PDF p.100,
  printed 95). **The siting rule is the examinable part.**
- **Other instruments:** mercury and aneroid **barometers**, **barograph**, **altimeter**,
  **anemometer** cups, **wind vane**, **sunshine recorder**, max/min thermometers,
  **wet-and-dry-bulb**. ✅ Majid's **Table 3.3** lists 24; ✗ it prints "**Antinometer**" for
  actinometer and **its last three rows shift in extraction** — read from a render only (N-35).
- **Beaufort, 0-12**, devised by 📄 **Admiral Sir Francis Beaufort (1774-1857) in 1805**. ✗ **N-08:
  five names moved from force 7 up** — 7 Moderate→**Near** Gale · 8 Fresh→**Gale** · 9
  Strong→**Severe** Gale · 10 Whole Gale→**Storm** · 11 Storm→**Violent Storm**; and 2 Slight→**Light
  Breeze**. **Force numbers and speeds unchanged.** "**Storm**" is force **11** in Leong, **10** in
  the NWS.
- ✗ **N-09:** Leong's hurricane threshold "**more than 75 m.p.h.**" against 📄 the standard **64
  knots** (NWS force 12 at 72-83 mph ≈ 116-134 km/h).
- **Isolines, the fifteen of ✅ Table 3.2:** isallobar (pressure **change**) · **isamplitude** (range)
  · **isanomaly** (departure from the mean) · isobar · **isocryme** (winter temperature) · isohel ·
  isohyet · **isokeraun** (thunderstorm frequency) · **isomer** · isoneph · **isonif** (snowfall) ·
  **isophene** · **isoryme** (frost) · **isoterp** · isotherm. ✗ **N-03: Majid defines isanomaly as
  "equal barometric pressure"** — the isobar's definition, in the next row.
- **Okta** = **one eighth of the sky**, a unit of cloud **amount** (✅ scan PDF p.105). **Not a type.**
- **Naming rule:** ✅ "**Winds are named for the direction from which they come**" (printed 3.6).
- **Units, binding (N-36):** SI-first with the source unit in parentheses; Leong is wholly imperial;
  **the IMD sheets lose degree signs in extraction** and were re-read from the rendered PDFs.

## RN·2 — The energy chain, and the two things it forbids

- **Short in, long out; transparent in, opaque out; heated from below.** The Sun is hot → shortwave;
  the surface is cool → **thermal infrared**.
- **Cascade:** reflection by cloud · **scattering** (returns part to space **and** delivers the
  **diffuse** component) · minor absorption aloft · **majority absorbed at the surface** · surface
  emits longwave · absorbed by water vapour, CO₂ and cloud · **re-radiated downward** · the rest
  escapes, partly through the **atmospheric window**.
- **Albedo** is a property of the **surface**. Ordering: fresh snow > desert sand > dry soil > grass >
  forest > water — **but water's albedo rises steeply at low solar elevation**. ⚠️ **No albedo value
  is stated anywhere**; ✗ C1's albedo block has none and no source supplies one.
- **Dust loading** is highest over **arid and semi-arid** surfaces, lowest over **open ocean far from
  land** and **ice-covered high latitudes**, low over the **washed humid tropics**.
- **Latitude reduces insolation twice over:** larger footprint **and** longer slant path; at high
  latitude a high-albedo surface adds a third.
- ✅ **Majid's six controls of temperature distribution (printed 3.5-3.6):** latitude · altitude ·
  distance from the sea · prevailing winds and air masses · ocean currents · nature of the surface.
- **Surplus at low latitudes, deficit at high → an OBLIGATION to transport energy poleward**,
  discharged by the three cells, the temperate cyclone and the jets.
- **Two prohibitions, absolute.** ↪ The **greenhouse-gas inventory, radiative forcing, attribution
  and any trend** belong to `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/17` and `/18`. ↪ **Aerosols as an
  air-quality problem** belong to the Environment air-pollution owner. ⚠️ **No trend of any kind is
  asserted in this file (U-02).**

## RN·3 — India's temperature field, and two superseded records

- **Three superimposed controls on any temperature map:** **latitude** (baseline) · **altitude**
  (correction) · **continentality** (distortion).
- **The bending rule.** **January, Northern Hemisphere: poleward over sea, equatorward over land.**
  July reverses it. **The magnitude is a continentality index** — large over north-west India and at
  the Bay head, small along the narrow peninsula.
- **Four reasons water differs** (✅ printed 3.8): specific heat · **mixing** · **penetration depth** ·
  **currents**; plus **evaporation** diverting energy into latent heat.
- ✅ **Greatest annual ranges: Siberia ~64 °C**; west of **Hudson Bay ~60 °C**. ✅ **Hemispheric means
  15 °C north, 12.7 °C south.** ✅ "**Near the equator, the daily range exceeds the annual range. Near
  the two poles, the daily range is reduced to almost zero.**"
- ✅ **Khullar's January field (printed 133, folio read):** the **10 °, 15 °, 20 °, 25 °C** isotherms.
- ✅ **Khullar's winter numbers (printed 132, folio read):** season **November to March**; **January
  coldest**; Ganga plain **12.5-17.5 °C**; northern **two thirds below 21 °C** with afternoons at
  **27 °C**; mean minima **~5 °C north-west, ~10 °C Gangetic**; **Dras −45 °C, the coldest place in
  India**.
- ✅ **The heat march (printed 135, folio not read, U-06): March ~40 °C southern Deccan → April ~45 °C
  northern MP → May ~48 °C Rajasthan → June, Punjab-Haryana.** Night minima "rarely below **20 °C**";
  diurnal range "as high as **18 °C in Rajasthan**".
- ✗ **N-11: Majid's Libya 56 °C of September 1922 (Al-Aziziyah) was decertified 13 September 2012**;
  standing record **56.7 °C, Greenland Ranch / Furnace Creek, Death Valley, 10 July 1913** (⚠️ U-08,
  secondary). ✗ **N-12: Alwar 50.5 °C (1956) and Ganganagar 50.6 °C (1935) superseded by Phalodi,
  51.0 °C, 19 May 2016.**

## RN·4 — The vertical column, and the three lapse rates

- **Layers are named by the SLOPE of the temperature curve, not by composition.** Troposphere falls ·
  stratosphere **rises** (ozone absorbing ultraviolet) · mesosphere falls; the pauses are the
  reversals. ✅ **Homosphere / heterosphere boundary at about 480 km.**
- **Weather is tropospheric because only a layer heated from below can overturn.**
- ⇄ **N-02: ✅ Majid 6.4 °C per 1000 m, twice (printed 3.8 and 3.52); ✗ C2 "6.5 … in the notes";
  ICAO/WMO standard 6.5 °C/km.** Teach 6.5. **It is an average, not a law** — which is why an
  inversion can exist.
- **Three rates, never merged:** **environmental** (~6.5 °C/km, the surrounding air) · **dry
  adiabatic** ✅ **10 °C/km** (a parcel) · **saturated adiabatic** (less than dry, latent heat
  released).
- **Tropopause: ~16 km equator · ~11 km mid-latitude · ~8 km pole, with a BREAK** where a jet sits.
  **The highest tropopause is the COLDEST.**
- **The lid gives storms their tops** — anvils and outflow canopies.
- **The 2022 "how" chain:** heated from below → lapse rate → instability → overturning → moisture
  lifted and condensed → confined by the lid, **proved by the stratospheric contrast**.

## RN·5 — Inversion: one definition, four types, five conditions

- **Definition:** ✅ "an **increase of air temperature with increase in height** … also called as the
  **negative lapse rate**" (printed 3.8).
- **Four types.** **Ground-surface / low-level** (radiative) · **valley** (cold-air drainage) ·
  **upper-air / subsidence** — ✅ "**compression of the descending air** as it happens in the case of
  **subtropical high pressure belts (anticyclones)**" · **frontal** — ✅ "the **warm air overlies the
  cold air**". ✗ **N-18: C1 gives three and omits the FRONTAL**, which is the western disturbance's
  own; and because Majid's upper-air type names subsidence, **C1's ⚠️ on subsidence is raised to ✅**.
- **Five ground conditions** (✅ printed 3.9): long winter nights · cloudless clear sky · dry air with
  low relative humidity · calm or slow air movement · ❗ **snow-covered surface**. ✗ **N-17: C1
  carries four.** Snow is decisive because it has **high albedo by day and emits efficiently at
  night** — the post-western-disturbance Himalaya satisfies all five.
- ✅ **Ground type occurs "in the tropical and subtropical areas during long winter nights only"**,
  and "**the duration and height of the surface inversion increase poleward**".
- **Three mechanisms, kept apart:** **radiative** (surface cools) · **advective** (warm air over a
  cold surface — ✅ Newfoundland, Gulf Stream over Labrador) · **subsidence** (compression).
- ✅ **Consequences (printed 3.10-3.11):** visibility "**less than 1 km**"; "**In 1952, in London about
  four thousand people died because of the urban smog**"; frost damage to "**wheat, barley, gram,
  lentil, mustard, peas, vegetables, chilly, potatoes, and fruit orchards**"; sugarcane **red-rot** in
  "**Western Uttar Pradesh, Uttarakhand, Punjab and Haryana**"; the Alpine thermal belt — "**the lower
  parts of valleys of the Alps Mountains are almost without settlements, while the upper parts are
  inhabited**".
- ✅ **The closing physics: inversion "stops upward (ascent) AND downward (descent) movements of
  air."** **Both directions.**
- ↪ **Smog, PM2.5 and GRAP → Environment. Frost crop loss → Economy and `Disaster-Management/basic/09`.**

## RN·6 — Pressure quantified, and the gradient rule

- ✅ **Standard sea-level pressure 1013.25 millibars** (= 1013.25 hPa) ≈ **1 kg/cm²** ≈ **14.7 psi**
  (printed 3.11).
- ✅ **Decreases by about 50 per cent for every 5 km of ascent** — proportional, not linear.
- ✅ **Boiling point 100 °C at sea level → 90 °C at 3,000 m → 84 °C at 5,000 m.** ✅ "At places like
  **Leh and Kargil**, boiling potatoes and pulses is a very slow process." ⚠️ **No value is
  interpolated for Leh itself.**
- ✅ **"Pressure gradient is also called as barometric slope. CLOSELY SPACED ISOBARS denote STEEP
  pressure gradient while WIDELY SPACED isobars are indicative of GENTLE or LOW pressure gradient."**
- **Wind follows the GRADIENT, never the value.** ✅ Leong's tropical cyclone is small — "**50 to 200
  miles**" — but has "a **much steeper pressure gradient**".
- **Charts show mean sea-level pressure**, reduced to a common datum. Without the reduction there are
  no isobars.
- ↪ **Altitude physiology is not Geography's**; named once and dropped.

## RN·7 — The four forces, and the correction at the centre of the topic

- ✅ **Four forces (printed 3.12):** **gravitational** (vertical, balanced by the vertical pressure
  gradient) · **pressure-gradient** (the only initiator) · **Coriolis** ("a **deflective** force …
  **right** in the Northern Hemisphere, **left** in the Southern"; ✗ printed "**Cariolis**") ·
  **friction** ("extends to a height of about **500 m**").
- **Six Coriolis properties:** apparent · right-angled to motion · hemisphere-dependent ·
  **latitude-dependent** · speed-dependent · acts on any moving mass. **It does no work.**
- ✅ **Ferrel's Law** (scan PDF p.116) and ✅ **the Law of Deflection** (scan PDF p.117): Coriolis "is
  **absent along the equator but increases progressively towards the poles**".
- **Geostrophic balance:** PGF = Coriolis; flow **parallel to the isobars**, low on the left in the
  north; valid in the **free atmosphere**.
- **Within the friction layer the balance breaks** — slower wind, weaker Coriolis, so the wind
  **crosses the isobars into the low**. **Surface convergence is a friction effect.**
- ✗ **N-01, the topic's central correction.** ✗ ✅ Majid printed 3.12 heads a section "**Geostrophic
  Winds (Rossby Waves)**" and defines only geostrophic balance; ✗ **C2 line 104 repeats it by
  citation**; ✓ **C2 line 106 gives the correct definition.** **A geostrophic wind is a point
  balance; a Rossby wave exists because the Coriolis parameter VARIES with latitude — the beta
  effect.** ⚠️ **Rossby-wave flow is approximately geostrophic at every point along it, which is why
  they are confused and why the wave is still a property of the whole pattern.**
- **Three balances:** **geostrophic** (PGF = Coriolis) · **gradient** (adds curvature) ·
  **cyclostrophic** (curvature dominates, Coriolis negligible — **the tornado**).
- ↪ **Coriolis on ocean currents and the Ekman spiral → `Geography/basic/12`.**

## RN·8 — Seven belts, three cells, two naming conventions

- ✅ **"In all, there are SEVEN pressure belts on the globe"** (printed 3.13). ✗ **N-31: C1 tabulates
  four TYPES. Seven is the COUNT.**
- **Genesis split.** **Thermal:** Equatorial Low, the two Polar Highs. **Dynamic:** the two
  Subtropical Highs, the two Sub-Polar Lows. ⚠️ **A subtropical high is not a cold place.**
- ✅ **Subtropical high:** convergence aloft "results in the **subsidence of air**", giving
  "**contraction of air volume, increase density**" → **subsidence inversion → the hot deserts**.
- ⇄ **N-32, three conventions:** ✅ Leong — Equatorial Low **0**, Sub-Tropical High **30°**,
  "**Temperate Low**" **60°**; ✅ Majid — **5°N-5°S**, **25-35°**, "**Sub-Polar Low**" **60-65°**; ✗ C1
  mixes them. **Shown as alternatives; neither declared wrong.**
- ✅ **Equatorial Low is "not stationary and shifts north and south with the change in seasons."**
- ✅ **Trade winds are "the MOST REGULAR of all the planetary winds."**
- ✅ **Westerlies: SOUTH-WESTERLIES north, NORTH-WESTERLIES south.** ✗ **C1 names only the Northern.**
  ✅ **40°S-60°S: Roaring Forties, Furious Fifties, Shrieking or Stormy Sixties** — strongest because
  almost no land supplies friction.
- ✗ **N-16: the trade-wind deserts are ✅ Sahara, Kalahari, ATACAMA and the GREAT AUSTRALIAN**
  (scan PDF p.117). ✗ **C1 substitutes the Thar**, a **monsoon-margin and rain-shadow** desert.
- ✅ **The 30°-40° migration band** gives **California, Iberia, central Chile, southern Africa and
  south-western Australia** winter-only westerlies. ↪ The climate is `Geography/basic/19`'s.
- ✅ **Hadley, 1735** for the original model; the three-cell arrangement develops it. **Its known
  limit — that the middle cell is not a simple direct loop — is the gateway to Lessons 18 and 19.**
- ↪ **The monsoon → `Geography/advanced/16`. The Thar as a desert → `Geography/basic/07` and
  `Geography/advanced/07`.**

## RN·9 — Winds that reverse daily, and the one that does not

- **A thermal local circulation is defined by its DAILY REVERSAL**, not its size.
- ✅ **Scale limit: the land and sea breeze "does not normally exceed 15 MILES FROM THE COAST"**
  (≈ 24 km) — the only scale figure in either book, and the discriminator against a planetary wind.
- ✅ **The VALLEY WIND blows UP by day**, "called the valley wind **for the place of origin** … a warm
  wind and a **daytime or late afternoon** phenomenon"; ✅ **the MOUNTAIN WIND, "also known as the
  night breeze"**, drains down "**due to greater density**".
- ✅ **"Katabatic: Air drainage from elevated regions, flowing as GRAVITY WINDS"** (glossary, printed
  3.52) — on which ✗ **C1's ⚠️ tier is raised to ✅**. **Anabatic = upslope.**
- ✅ **Land use follows:** afternoon hill cloud from the valley wind; ✅ "the valley **slopes** are the
  preferred places of **orchards**" because moving air prevents frost stagnation.
- **The Loo, honestly grounded.** ✅ **One sentence in Majid printed 3.6**, with **no time, season,
  speed or mechanism**. ✗ **N-26: C1's "3-6 pm, May-June" is tagged ✅ but unsourced**; ✓ **C2 line 36
  warns against universalising it**; ⚠️ **U-14: no first-party IMD definition retrieved.** **Mechanism
  as it may be stated:** a flat, dry, sparsely vegetated plain heats intensely; air drawn across it
  arrives dry and is heated further in transit.
- ✗ **N-34: Majid's Haboob is "Hot, damp"; the standard description is a DRY thunderstorm-outflow
  density current. Both shown, neither asserted.** ✅ "3 hrs", "24 per year", with the ITCZ's advance.
- ✗ **N-13: Majid places the Berg wind in "Germany"; it is SOUTHERN AFRICAN.**
- ↪ **Heat-wave health, Heat Action Plans and wet-bulb thresholds → `Disaster-Management/basic/09`.
  Urban heat island as planning → `Geography/basic/28`.**

## RN·10 — The Foehn family: compression first, rainout second

- ✗ **N-15, the topic's central mechanism correction.** ✅ **Majid printed 3.24: "The PRIMARY SOURCE
  OF HEAT in the Chinook is the HEAT OF COMPRESSION."** ✅ **Leong scan PDF p.119: "The air is
  COMPRESSED AND WARMED."** ✗ **C1 lines 116-122 call the adiabatic asymmetry "the single mechanism
  examiners test".** **Compression is the engine; rainout is the amplifier.**
- ✅ **Rainout is explicitly a strengthening term:** the Chinook is "**strengthened if precipitation
  takes place** in the air stream as it rises over the windward side".
- ✅ **The descent is FORCED:** the wind "develops with the uplift of relatively **mild stable air**",
  and "the low pressure trough **pulls the wind down** the eastern slope"; ✅ it heats at the **dry
  adiabatic rate of 10 °C/kilometer**.
- **Three reasons the correction is physical, not bibliographic.** Rainless Foehn events occur; the
  same physics operates **without any mountain** (subsidence inversion, Hadley descent, the Tibetan
  warm core); and the heat is generated **during the descent**, not carried over the crest.
- ✅ **Evidence set:** **Spearfish, South Dakota, 1943, −20 °C to 7 °C in two minutes** · the belt "a
  narrow zone about **300 kilometers east of the crest of the Rockies from New Mexico north into
  Canada**" · the **Chinook wall cloud** · gusts to **160 km/h** · removal of "**15 cm (6 inch) of
  snow a day … half metre of snow in a single day**" · ✅ Leong's Alpine "**15° to 30 °F within an
  hour**" (≈ 8-17 °C) and Rockies "**35 °F within 15 minutes**" (≈ 19 °C) · "**melts snow and causes
  avalanches**" · "**the snow-eater**" · "frequent Chinooks means **winter is mild**".
- ✅ **The family:** **Foehn** (Alps) · **Chinook** (Rockies) · **Zonda** (Andean lee, **120 km/h**) ·
  **Santa Ana** (southern California, under ✅ Table 3.1's **Fohn** row) · **Berg** (southern Africa).
  ✗ **C1 omits Zonda and Santa Ana.** ✅ **Fohn row: "may attain 10-20 °C in spring with relative
  humidity of 10 per cent."**
- ✗ **N-14: ✅ Majid's body text says the Chinook means "Snow eating wind" (printed 3.24); his own
  Table 3.1 says "ice-eater" (printed 3.25); ✅ Leong says "the snow-eater". Both cells shown; neither
  asserted.**
- ✅ **Leong's 1967 Cambridge paper pairs them as "Fohn (Chinook) winds"** — the examiner treated them
  as one object.
- ↪ **Avalanche initiation and snowpack stability → `Geography/basic/06` and `Geography/advanced/06`.**

## RN·11 — Moisture: a ratio with a moving denominator

- ✅ **"Cold air cannot contain as much water vapour as warm air"** (printed 3.37).
- **Dew point:** the temperature at which saturation occurs at **constant pressure and constant
  vapour content** (✅ glossary, printed 3.52).
- **Two moves on the saturation plane.** **Horizontal** = temperature change = **relative** humidity
  changes, vapour content unchanged. **Vertical** = vapour content changes = evaporation or
  condensation.
- **Three measures:** **absolute** (per unit **volume**; ✅ Leong, **grams per cubic metre**) ·
  **specific** (per unit **mass**, **conserved** during ascent) · **relative** (a percentage of a
  moving ceiling).
- ✅ **"Relative humidity commonly displays a daily cycle which has PHASE OPPOSITE to that of
  temperature so that the HIGHEST VALUES ARE OBSERVED NEAR DAWN and the lowest during the**
  [afternoon]."
- ✅ **Water shares:** oceans **97 %**; the rest mostly ice; rivers, lakes and soil moisture **under
  1 %**; **the atmosphere 0.35 %**. **A small, fast store delivers a large flux.**
- **Four condensation products, one event:** **dew** (surface) · **frost** (surface below 0 °C) ·
  **fog** (a layer) · **cloud** (a lifted parcel). **Fog and cloud differ only in how the cooling was
  achieved.**
- **P1's answer:** on a cloudy night the cloud **re-radiates longwave downward**, so the surface never
  reaches its dew point. **Calm** is equally necessary.
- **P9's answer:** vapour declines sharply with height because its **source is the surface** and
  because **ascent condenses the excess out** — faster than density declines.
- **Wet-bulb:** a wet, ventilated bulb depressed below the dry-bulb by evaporation; the two coincide
  at saturation. ↪ **The survivability threshold is `Disaster-Management/basic/09`'s.**
- ↪ **Evapotranspiration, soil moisture and the water balance → `Geography/basic/04`.**

## RN·12 — Cloud: form records lifting, height decides sign

- ✅ **The ten genera (scan PDF p.109), "by form, by height and by appearance":** Cirrus (Ci) ·
  Cirrostratus · Cirrocumulus · Altostratus · Altocumulus · Stratocumulus · Nimbostratus · Cumulus ·
  Cumulonimbus · Stratus (St). ✗ **D-13: C1's cloud table has four rows, no genus list, no height
  band, no unit and no isoline.**
- ✅ **High cloud "mainly cirrus (Ci) of feathery form at 20-40,000 FEET above ground"** ≈ **6,000-
  12,000 m** (N-36).
- **Four roots:** **cirro-** (high, icy) · **alto-** (middle) · **strato-** (layer → gentle, widespread
  ascent) · **cumulo-** (heap → convective ascent) · **nimbo-/-nimbus** (precipitating).
  **Nimbostratus = frontal rain. Cumulonimbus = convective rain.**
- **Cumulonimbus is the only genus spanning all three bands**, base near the ground, top at the
  tropopause, with an **anvil**.
- **The radiative rule (P3):** every cloud **reflects shortwave** (cooling) and **traps longwave**
  (warming); **cloud-top temperature decides the net sign**. **High, thin, icy → weak reflection +
  strong trapping → WARMING. Low, thick, wet → strong reflection + weak trapping → COOLING.**
- **Apparatus:** **oktas** · **isoneph** (cloudiness) · **isohel** (sunshine).
- ⚠️ **Scan caution: Leong p.106 is duplicated at p.108 and p.105 at p.107** — a **read finding from
  renders, not a hash finding** (U-12); **no claim is made that any page is missing** (U-13).
- ↪ **Cloud seeding and weather modification → Environment; stated once and dropped.**

## RN·13 — Rain: three mechanisms, and the one fixed to a place

- **Three lifting mechanisms:** **convectional** (buoyancy) · **orographic** (relief) · **frontal**
  (density contrast). ⚠️ A fourth, **convergence**, drives the equatorial low and the cyclone inflow.
- **Only orographic rainfall is anchored to a place**, which is why the wettest places have
  addresses.
- **The traverse, with all three rates:** **dry** ascent to the condensation level → **saturated**
  ascent above, precipitating → **dry** descent on the lee, warming and drying. **The maximum lies
  BELOW the crest.** ✅ **Leong's Fig. 113** (scan PDF p.115) draws it.
- **The Foehn and the rain shadow are ONE traverse read for temperature and for moisture.**
- **The alignment test (O1).** **Across the flow = rain maker and Foehn maker. Along the flow =
  barrier and climatic divide.** Worked: Rockies N-S across the westerlies → ✅ the Chinook belt;
  Andes N-S → ✅ **Atacama**; Himalaya W-E → barrier in winter, rain maker in summer; Alps → ✅ Foehn
  valleys; Western Ghats N-S → wet scarp, dry Deccan; Great Dividing Range → ✅ **Great Australian**
  deserts.
- ✗ **N-30: ✅ Majid's "wet-day: when at least 1 mm of rainfall is recorded" against IMD's rainy day at
  ≥ 2.5 mm** — ⚠️ **no first-party anchor this pass (U-01).** **State both with their agencies.**
- 🇮🇳 **Western Ghats** — same latitude, short distance, order-of-magnitude difference.
  **Mawsynram and Cherrapunji** — explained by an **abrupt scarp, funnelling and a short sea fetch**:
  **moisture flux × forced ascent rate, not altitude.** ⚠️ **No rainfall figure is stated for either.**
- ✅ **Khullar's December-February gradient: 60 cm Himalaya · 12 cm Punjab · 5.3 cm Delhi.**
- ↪ **SW monsoon rainfall → `Geography/advanced/16`. NE monsoon and Tamil Nadu's October-December
  rain → `Geography/basic/16`** (✅ Khullar's "**about 75 cm between October and December**" is stated
  once as a boundary marker and routed). ↪ **Rain as a weathering agent → `Geography/basic/04`.**

## RN·14 — Air masses, fronts and the machine the western disturbance belongs to

- **A front lifts air by DENSITY CONTRAST**, not buoyancy and not relief.
- **An air mass needs a uniform source region and a long residence time**, so properties extend
  **through its depth**; moving on begins to **modify** it. **Codes:** c/m × A/P/T/E. **cP** Siberian;
  **mT** Bay of Bengal; **cT** Saharan and Arabian.
- ✅ **Chart symbols (printed 3.27): cold front = "a series of TRIANGULAR SPIKES, pointing in the
  direction of frontal movement"; warm front = "a series of ROUNDED KNOBS".**
- ✅ **Occluded front = "the overrunning of a surface warm front by a cold front and the subsequent
  lifting of the warm air wedge off the ground; initial precipitation is MODERATE TO HEAVY."**
- ✅ **Cyclogenesis: "On the average, a temperate cyclone takes 3-10 DAYS to progress through the
  stages."**
- **Five stages:** stationary polar front → wave with a low at the kink → open wave with a warm sector
  → occlusion begins → occluded and decaying. **Occlusion destroys the surface contrast that fuelled
  it.**
- **Cold front:** steep, abrupt ascent, cumulonimbus, intense brief narrow rain, sharp temperature
  drop. **Warm front:** shallow, gradual ascent, a lowering cirrus-to-nimbostratus sequence, moderate
  prolonged widespread rain **ahead** of the surface front.
- ✅ **Every front is an inversion** — "the warm air overlies the cold air" — **the rung ✗ C1 omits**.
- 🇮🇳 **India's winter rain in the north-west is FRONTAL; its summer rain is not.** ✗ **C2 defines the
  western disturbance by ORIGIN ALONE. Origin is not structure.**
- ↪ **Mid-latitude climates produced by frontal activity → `Geography/basic/22`.**

## RN·15 — The tropical cyclone, and the inference that must be refused

- **It runs on LATENT HEAT:** ✅ "an abundant supply of water vapour and thus the necessary **latent
  heat** to fuel these storms". **The feedback:** warm sea → evaporation → inward and upward transport
  → condensation → **warm core aloft** → lower surface pressure → stronger inflow.
- **It dies over land within about a day** because the fuel line is cut.
- ✅ **Leong's figures (scan PDF p.119):** "between **6° and 20°** north and south"; "most frequent
  from **July to October**"; diameter "only **50 to 200 miles**" (≈ 80-320 km); "a **much steeper
  pressure gradient**"; winds "over **100 m.p.h.**" (≈ 160 km/h); eye pressure "about **965 mb**";
  force 12 at "**75 m.p.h.**" (✗ N-09).
- ✅ **Named historical cases: Swatow, 1922, "drowned 50,000 people"; Barbados, 1780, "about 6,000
  inhabitants were reported dead."** ⚠️ Historical records, not statements about present risk.
- ✅ **"The central area is designated as the 'Eye'"; "calm, rainless centres"; the highest velocities
  "always recorded ADJACENT TO THE CENTRE."** **The eye is warm because air SUBSIDES inside the
  eyewall and is COMPRESSIVELY WARMED.** **The warm core is why the pressure is so low.**
- **Six genesis gates, all necessary, none sufficient:** warm ocean **through depth** · deep moisture
  · **sufficient Coriolis** · **low vertical shear** · a seed disturbance · deep conditional
  instability.
- 🇮🇳 **The Bay of Bengal's lethality is EXPOSURE:** funnel shape · broad shallow shelf · freshwater
  cap resisting mixing · low deltaic coast · dense settlement. ⚠️ **No frequency count, ratio or trend
  for either basin appears anywhere (U-02).**
- **What may be said:** a warmer ocean with greater heat content raises **potential intensity**. **What
  may not:** anything about storm numbers, because three gates are set by **circulation**.
- ↪ **SST, ocean heat content and the IOD → `Geography/basic/12` and `/advanced/12`. Preparedness,
  shelters and IMD colour codes → `Disaster-Management/basic/07` (I-03). Storm surge →
  `Geography/basic/10` and `/advanced/10`.**

## RN·16 — Binary interaction: a steering phenomenon first

- **A cyclone is a CIRCULATION, not an object.** Each storm lies inside the other's wind field.
- 📄 **Band: 300-750 nautical miles (≈ 556-1,389 km), "depending on the sizes of the cyclones";
  Sakuhei Fujiwhara, 1921.** ⚠️ **Secondary retrieval.** ✗ **N-33: C1 gives "several hundred to around
  fourteen hundred kilometres" — lower bound dropped, unit lost.**
- ✓ **The centroid is CIRCULATION-WEIGHTED**: "weighted by vortex circulation and structure; it need
  **not** lie geometrically halfway between the eyes". **It sits nearer the larger storm.**
- ✓ **"Merger increases CIRCULATION SIZE more reliably than MAXIMUM WIND SPEED; never write 'two
  cyclones combine into a super-cyclone.'"**
- **Orbital sense follows the vortices' own rotation:** counterclockwise north, clockwise south.
- **Five outcomes:** mutual orbit · absorption/merger · elastic deflection · straining out · escape.
  **In four of five the TRACK CHANGES.**
- ✓ **It is three-dimensional:** vertical **shear tilts and ventilates** a vortex; **asymmetric
  convection** displaces the effective circulation centre; **background steering** superposes.
- ✓ **Four cases: Hilary-Irwin (eastern Pacific, 2017) · Seroja-Odette (Australian region, 2021) ·
  Parma-Melor (western North Pacific, 2009).**
- ⚠️ **Neither local book contains the word "Fujiwhara".** The mechanism has **no book layer**.
- 🇮🇳 **The North Indian Ocean** is closed in the north and divided by a peninsula, so binary
  interaction is **structurally less available** — a **geometric argument, not a count** — and **more
  consequential**, because a deflection open ocean would absorb is here the difference between
  landfall and none.
- ↪ **Shipping and maritime safety → `Geography/basic/33`.**

## RN·17 — Severe convection, and a configuration argument

- **Five supercell ingredients:** warm moist low-level air · **dry air above** · a steep lapse rate ·
  **strong vertical shear** · a lifting trigger. Plus a **cap** storing instability for explosive
  release.
- **Shear supplies the rotation** by tilting horizontal spin into the vertical. ⚠️ **Shear destroys a
  tropical cyclone and creates a supercell** — same variable, opposite signs.
- ✗ **N-06: ✗ Leong — "Tropical cyclones, typhoons, hurricanes and tornadoes — All these are different
  kinds of tropical cyclones."** **A tornado is a mesoscale vortex of a severe thunderstorm.** ✓ C1
  §9.4 is right and contradicts its own book **silently**.
- ✗ **N-07: the 500 m.p.h. figure is refused; no tornado wind speed is stated in this file.**
  ✗ **N-10: the willy-willy is a DUST DEVIL in modern Australian usage.**
- ✗ **N-05: ✗ Majid's "occurs mostly in moist tropical air" against ✅ his own PDF 107 — ideal
  conditions "ahead of a cold front" where warm moist air meets "cooler, drier air from polar
  regions".** ✗ **N-04: his glossary reverses the causality — "developed in response to extremely low
  pressure"; the deficit is a CONSEQUENCE of rotation under cyclostrophic balance.** ✅ The same entry
  correctly names **mesocyclone formation**.
- ✅ **Usable Majid tornado content:** "**seldom larger than a kilometre in diameter**"; rotation
  "**counterclockwise … except in rare instance**"; "**funnel clouds form out of other clouds … work
  downward toward the ground**"; usually **gray**; "**in winter … a brilliant white**".
- **The configuration argument (O5).** North America: a **warm Gulf** with an unobstructed northward
  corridor · the **Rockies** supplying dry elevated air and a **lee trough** · the **dryline** ·
  a **mid-latitude jet** overhead · and **no east-west barrier**. **South Asia's Himalaya is exactly
  that barrier**, so the ingredients assemble for **hours, not months over a fixed corridor**.
- **Cloudburst mechanism:** deep convective column → **suspended water load** → **orographic
  superposition anchoring it** → collapse or stall releasing the load → **below forecast resolution**.
  ⚠️ **N-25: the criterion — about 100 mm in one hour over ~20-30 km² — is carried at ⚠️, not ✅**;
  ✗ C1 tags it ✅ though no book states it, and ⚠️ **U-01: the first-party IMD page returned HTTP 404**.
- **The cloudburst rule, in words:** **mechanism is Topic 13's** (2024 GS-I Q6); **flood consequence
  is Disaster Management's** (2022 GS-III Q8). **The physics is identical; the directive word
  separates them** (I-02).
- **Hail size measures the updraught** that held the stone aloft.
- ⚠️ **U-09: no Indian tornado or Kalbaisakhi count, rating, date or casualty figure is stated.**
- ↪ **Flash-flood management → `Disaster-Management/basic/08`. Landslide and debris flow →
  `Geography/basic/04` and `/advanced/04`. Hail damage economics → `Economy`.**

## RN·18 — Jets: a thermal gradient made visible as a wind

- **A jet forms where a horizontal temperature gradient is steep**, because the pressure difference
  **accumulates with height**; the maximum is near the tropopause.
- ✅ **Majid printed 3.35:** "a strong wind blowing **from west to east** … near the level of
  tropopause (**about 12,000 m**) in sinuous band"; **160-480 km wide**, **900-2,150 m thick**; core
  "can **exceed 300 kmph**"; mean "**110 km per hour in summer** to about **190 kmph in winter**";
  exceptional **375 kmph**. ✅ Their "**position, thickness, dimensions and movements**" influence
  global temperature, pressure, circulation, monsoon and precipitation.
- ✅ **Three types listed: sub-tropical · polar front · Arctic/Antarctic. NONE EASTERLY.**
- ✗ **N-19 / H11: ✅ the IWG definition EXCLUDES the tropical easterly jet that ✅ Majid's own
  *Geography of India* printed 4.8 explains.** ✗ **C2 asserts the TEJ as book-grounded without
  noticing.** **The correct general definition drops the directional clause.**
- **PFJ:** ~40-60°, ~9-11 km, strongly **meandering**, steers temperate cyclones. **STWJ:** at the
  **tropopause break**, ~25-35°, ~12-14 km, **steadier**, strongest in **winter** — **India's winter
  jet**. **Polar night jet** named as the winter stratospheric vortex jet.
- ✗ **N-37: C1 line 537's corrupted diagram rebuilt.** **Low amplitude / zonal** = small north-south
  exchange, fast steering, moderate extremes. **High amplitude / meridional** = large exchange, slow
  steering, **persistence**, **blocking**, **severe extremes**.
- ✓ **C1's preserved sentence:** "most damaging weather extremes are extremes of **duration**, not of
  instantaneous intensity — and duration is governed by the **upper-air wave pattern**."
- ⇄ **P2a: jets exist in BOTH hemispheres.** The Southern flow is **stronger and less disturbed**
  (little land); the Northern is **more meridional** (mountains force standing waves).
- ⚠️ **The jet rule, applied:** ✅ Khullar's and ✅ Majid's jet passages sit **inside their monsoon
  chapters**, and this edition states so **at the point of quotation**.
- ↪ **Aviation meteorology and clear-air turbulence → `Geography/basic/33`; not Geography's to teach.**

## RN·19 — The Himalaya-Tibet system as an organising structure

- **Winter — mechanical.** ✅ The STWJ **bifurcates**: southern branch "about **240 km p.h. at 200
  mb**" against ✅ "**70 to 90 km p.h.**" northern; the southern runs "along the **southern slopes of
  the Himalayas**". ✗ **N-21: C2's "~25 deg N in notes" is a hedge; ✅ Fig. 5.4's reference parallels
  are 23½°N and 40°N.**
- ✅ **"The UPPER JET is responsible for STEERING of the western depressions from the Mediterranean
  Sea."** ✅ **Subsidence beneath it gives "dry outblowing NORTHERLY winds from the subtropical
  anticyclone over NORTH-WESTERN INDIA AND PAKISTAN."** ✅ **Depressions may redevelop "in the zone of
  jet stream confluence about 30°N, 105°E."**
- **Summer — thermal.** ✅ "Summer-time heating of the Tibetan Plateau makes it a **HIGH-LEVEL HEAT
  SOURCE** … a **WARM CORE ANTICYCLONE (HIGH PRESSURE)** … in the middle part of the troposphere at
  **500 mb LEVEL** … the result of a process called **ANTI-CYCLOGENESIS**." ✅ On its southern flank
  the flow is "**from east to west** … known as **TROPICAL EASTERLY JETS**", ✅ "stationed at **15°N
  latitude**".
- ✗ **N-20: "mid-troposphere" describes the 500 mb ANTICYCLONE, not the jet. The TEJ core is
  UPPER-tropospheric, ~150-200 hPa.** ✅ Koteshwaram's caption "**at 12 km**" supports it.
- ✅ **"By the end of May the SOUTHERN JET BEGINS TO BREAK."** ✅ **Maung Tun Yin: the jet "disappears
  completely over northern India" and "takes up a position at about 40°N."**
- ✗ **N-22: three claims, none of them "pre-condition"** — ✅ "**first indication**" · ✅ "**disappears
  completely**" · ✅ onset "prompted by the **hydro-dynamic effect** of the Himalayas and **not** by
  the thermally induced low pressure centre".
- ✅ **Attributions: PEDELABORDE · KOTESHWARAM ("at 12 km") · T.N. KRISHNAMURTI (200 mb divergence,
  JUNE-AUGUST 1967) · S. RAMA RATTAN · MAUNG TUN YIN.**
- ✅ **Three anticyclone types (printed 3.35): COLD** (Arctic/Antarctic, easterly and south-easterly,
  very low thickness, dying in the tropics) · **WARM** (subtropical, Hadley descent, light wind, low
  humidity, cloudless) · **BLOCKING** ("obstruction in the air circulation in the upper atmosphere",
  "move **very slowly**"). ✅ "**Anticyclones do not have fronts**"; outflow **clockwise north,
  anticlockwise south**. ✗ **N-40: Majid's "North-West Europe and the Pacific between 140-170 W" are
  his STATED EXAMPLES, not the global distribution.**
- **Two highs compared:** the **subtropical high** is **dynamically** induced; the **Tibetan
  anticyclone** is **thermally** induced and **warm-core** — an unusual object.
- ⇄ **O1's barrier half: a range of sufficient height and extent does not merely lift air; it
  REORGANISES AN ENTIRE SECTOR'S UPPER-AIR CIRCULATION.** **Lesson 13 is the primary owner.**
- ⚠️ **U-07: Majid's printed 4.9 and 4.10 appear absent from the abridged scan, which jumps 4.8 →
  4.18. Absence was not proved; nothing is drawn from either.**
- ↪ **The monsoon mechanism and the plateau as a monsoon cause → `Geography/advanced/16`. Himalayan
  snow, mass balance and GLOF → `Geography/advanced/06`. Arctic amplification and jet waviness →
  `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/18`, as an OPEN QUESTION, never as causation (U-02).**

## RN·20 — The western disturbance and the measuring state

- 📄 **The operational definition (IMD Special Message No. 01, 25 January 2026, 1545 IST):** "The
  Western Disturbance as a **cyclonic circulation** over Himachal and adjoining Punjab in **lower
  tropospheric levels** with a **trough aloft in middle & upper tropospheric westerlies** roughly
  along **Long. 74°E to the north of Lat. 28°N**." **A TWO-LEVEL OBJECT, FIXED BY COORDINATES.**
- **It is a mid-latitude FRONTAL wave cyclone**, steered by the **southern branch of the bifurcated
  STWJ**. ✗ **C2 defines it by origin alone; origin is not structure.**
- 📄 **The official footprint is TEN jurisdictions:** J&K · **Ladakh** · Himachal Pradesh ·
  Uttarakhand · Punjab · Haryana · **Chandigarh** · Delhi · Uttar Pradesh · **Rajasthan**. ✗ **C2
  omits the three in bold.**
- 📄 **Products, from the same message:** "isolated **heavy rainfall/snowfall and hail storm** on
  **27th January, 2026**"; plains winds "**40-60 kmph gusting to 70 kmph**"; hills "**50-60 kmph
  gusting to 70 kmph**". 📄 **Sequence: a "fresh and intense" WD from the night of 26th January and
  "another fresh Western disturbance" from the night of 30th January, 2026.** ⚠️ **Proof of
  occurrence, not of frequency.**
- ✅ **Precipitation gradient, December-February: "about 60 cm in the Himalayan region, 12 cm in
  Punjab, 5.3 cm at Delhi"**, decreasing "**from the north and north-west to east**".
- ✗ **N-23: ✅ "on an average 3 to 5 disturbances per month" against ✅ Kendrew's 2 (Nov), 4 (Dec), 5
  (Jan), 5 (Feb), 4 (Mar) — November falls BELOW the stated floor.** **Both shown; neither asserted.**
  ⚠️ **N-24: Kendrew is MID-20th-CENTURY and is dated on every use.** ✗ **C2 carries no figure.**
- ✅ **Fog: "the average duration of occurrence of fog at the airport is 85 HOURS"** (IGI, New Delhi).
  ⚠️ **U-15: Table 5.2's range was not read; no range stated.** ✅ **Minima "5° to 10 °C below normal"**
  — which spans IMD's cold-wave and severe-cold-wave departure bands simultaneously.
- 📄 **HEAT WAVE:** plains **≥ 40 °C**, hills **≥ 30 °C**; departure **4.5-6.4 °C**, severe **> 6.4 °C**;
  actual **≥ 45 °C**, severe **≥ 47 °C**; coastal **≥ 37 °C with a ≥ 4.5 °C departure**; declared at
  **2 stations** in a sub-division on **2 consecutive days**, on the **second day**; **warm night**
  only when the maximum stays **≥ 40 °C**, with minimum departures **4.5-6.4** and **> 6.4 °C**;
  season **March to July**, damage **May to July**. ✗ **C1 gives the plains threshold only (N-27).**
- 📄 **COLD WAVE:** plains **≤ 10 °C**, hills **≤ 0 °C**; negative departure **4.5-6.4 °C**, severe
  **> 6.4 °C**; actual minimum **≤ 4 °C**, severe **≤ 2 °C** (plains only); coastal **≤ 15 °C with a
  −4.5 °C departure**; season **November to March**, casualties **December to March**. ✗ **Neither
  owner carries any of it (N-28).**
- 📄 **BHARAT FORECAST SYSTEM (PIB Release ID 2131392, MoES, 26 MAY 2025):** developed by the **Indian
  Institute of Tropical Meteorology (IITM), Pune**; resolution **from 12 km to 6 km**; method the
  "**Triangular Cubic Octahedral Grid model**"; gains "**30 % … in extreme rainfall forecasts and a
  64 % enhancement in core zones**"; collaboration with **IMD and ISRO**; launched at **Vigyan
  Bhawan**. ✗ **N-39: the string appears nowhere in either owner.** ⚠️ **U-11: use 64, not 67.**
- 📄 **MISSION MAUSAM (PIB 2054427 and 2131392):** **Ministry of Earth Sciences**; **₹2,000 crore**;
  agencies **IMD, IITM Pune, NCMRWF, INCOIS and NIOT**. ✗ **N-38: C2 names the mission and nothing
  else.** ⚠️ **U-10: no radar count is stated.**
- **Halving a grid quarters a cell's area**, so a region is described by **four times as many
  points** — and Lesson 17's cloudburst occupies roughly **20-30 km²**, which a 12 km grid cannot
  resolve. **Resolution is a statement about which phenomena the state can see.**
- ⚠️ **U-02 and I-05, binding and final: no trend in western-disturbance frequency or intensity is
  asserted anywhere in this edition**; ✗ **C2's "hyperactive" framing is re-cast as a hypothesis**;
  and **no ledger row exists for a western disturbance by name, in any year, for any subject**, so
  these lessons are justified on **mechanism and consequence**, not on a direct PYQ.
- ↪ **ENSO, the Walker circulation, the IOD and the LPA classification → `Geography/basic/12`,
  `/advanced/12` and `/advanced/16` (I-04). Rabi crop calendars and procurement → `Economy`. Fog
  disruption to rail and air → `Geography/basic/33`. Cold-wave mortality and shelters →
  `Disaster-Management/basic/09`.**

## RN·21 — The seven owned Mains spines, as a single recall strip

| Demand | Directive | **Spine, in one line** |
|---|---|---|
| **O1** 2021 Q14 | *Briefly mention and explain* | **Short inventory, then one organising variable — across versus along the flow — applied back across it** |
| **O2** 2022 Q17 | *How* | **One chain through one layer: heated from below → lapse rate → instability → overturning → moisture → lid, proved by the stratosphere** |
| **O3** 2024 Q4 | *Explain* (causal relation) | **Trace the latent-heat feedback, then bound it with the five other genesis gates** |
| **O4** 2024 Q6 | *Explain* (a phenomenon) | **Definition → criterion with its provenance caveat → four-step mechanism** |
| **O5** 2024 Q16 | *Explain* (a concentration) | **Five ingredients → the configuration that delivers them → the South Asian comparative test** |
| **O6** 2026 Q4 | *What is … explain its impact* | **Definition → movement (robust) and intensity (conditional), never merged** |
| **O7** 2026 Q14 | *Compare* | **State the classification first — two are one mechanism, the third is another — then hold three columns** |

## RN·22 — The twelve sentences that carry the most marks

1. **Climate needs a period, and the period is an institutional decision** — 30 years, with ✅ Leong's
   35 shown.
2. **Short in, long out; the air is heated from below** — and therefore temperature falls with height.
3. **An isotherm bends poleward over sea in January, and the magnitude of the bend is a
   continentality index.**
4. **An inversion is a negative lapse rate, and it stops ascent AND descent.**
5. **Wind follows the pressure gradient, never the pressure value.**
6. **A geostrophic wind is a balance at a point; a Rossby wave exists because Coriolis varies with
   latitude.**
7. **Seven belts; the subtropical highs and sub-polar lows are DYNAMICALLY induced.**
8. **Descending air is warm because it is COMPRESSED; windward rainout is an amplifier.**
9. **Relative humidity peaks at dawn because the ceiling falls, not because water is added.**
10. **A high cloud warms because its top is cold; a low cloud cools because its top is warm.**
11. **A tropical cyclone runs on latent heat and dies over land; a temperate cyclone runs on a density
    contrast and dies by occlusion.**
12. **A western disturbance is a lower-tropospheric circulation beneath a trough in the westerlies,
    at 74°E north of 28°N — and no trend in it may be asserted.**

---

# COVERAGE MATRIX

Apparatus. This section teaches nothing. It records, in one place, how the twenty lessons map on to
the syllabus clauses, the twenty-one owned demands, the forty corrections, the sixteen uncertainties,
the twenty visual grammars, the twenty rubrics, the seven map assets and the thirty-eight
delegations.

## CM·1 — Syllabus clause × lesson

| GS-I clause | This topic's share | Lessons | Held by others |
|---|---|---|---|
| **"Salient features of world's physical geography"** | **The entire atmospheric half** — radiation, temperature in both dimensions, pressure, wind, moisture, cloud, precipitation, air masses, fronts and upper-air circulation | **1-14, 18, 19** | The lithospheric and hydrospheric halves: Topics 01-12 |
| **"Important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes, Tsunami, Volcanic activity, cyclone etc."** | **Cyclone genesis, structure and binary interaction; tornado; cloudburst; thunderstorm; hail; heat wave and cold wave as meteorological events** | **15, 16, 17, 20** | Earthquake and tsunami: Topic 03. Cyclone **preparedness, warning and response**: Disaster Management. Flood response: Disaster Management |
| **"Changes in critical geographical features … and the effects of such changes"** | **Nothing.** Topic 13 teaches weather, which is by definition not a change in a geographical feature | **none** | Climate change: Environment. Classification shift: Topic 14. Monsoon variability: Topic 16. Cryosphere: Topic 06 |

**Three frozen consequences, carried through the file.** **First**, this topic's footprint is **a
medium, not a place**, which is why its size is derived from obligations. **Second**, the word
**"cyclone etc."** is the hook that pulls the whole severe-weather family into GS-I, and the examiner
has used it **four times in three years** — 2024 Q4, 2024 Q6, 2024 Q16 and 2026 Q4. **Third, no
climate-change claim is Topic 13's to make**: this edition may describe a mechanism by which a warmer
ocean raises a cyclone's potential intensity, and **may not state a trend** in cyclone number,
western-disturbance frequency, heat-wave frequency or jet waviness.

## CM·2 — The master lesson matrix

| # | Lesson | Stage | Primary visual grammar | Owned demands | Corrections applied | Uncertainties flagged | Map assets | Rubric |
|---:|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

**Without these exclusions this topic would sprawl past twenty-eight lessons.** The excluded bodies,
by name: the **entire monsoon mechanism** (Halley's thermal theory, the ITCZ and NITCZ/SITCZ, the
air-mass theory, the monsoon front, burst, breaks, withdrawal, onset dates and rainfall
distribution); **climate classification** (Köppen, Thornthwaite, Trewartha, Stamp, Kendrew's and
R.L. Singh's divisions); **ENSO, El Niño, La Niña, the Walker circulation, the IOD, the DMI, the MJO,
the LPA classification and Ocean Mean Temperature**; **cyclone preparedness, shelters, evacuation and
the colour-coded ladder as an instrument**; **cloudburst flood response**; **Heat Action Plans, heat
mortality and wet-bulb survivability**; **tsunami**; **glacier mass balance, snow cover, avalanche
risk management and GLOF**; **polar weather and amplification**; **rabi crop calendars, yield
response and insurance**; **the greenhouse-gas inventory, radiative forcing, IPCC content and
attribution**; **air pollution, PM2.5, smog policy and GRAP**; **aviation meteorology, clear-air
turbulence and rail fog disruption**; **urban heat island as planning**; **weather modification**;
and **the Thar as a landform system**.

Geography*** has a usable text layer, and **only its content is quoted from extracted text**.

## SL·5 — Source priority, as applied

| Tier | Source | Role, as exercised in this edition |
|---:|---|---|
| **1** | **C1** | Controlling owner. Its control clauses bind, **except** where this edition proves a clause wrong — the Foehn single-mechanism claim (N-15), the trade-wind desert list (N-16), the inversion taxonomy (N-17, N-18), the cloudburst tier (N-25) and the corrupted diagram at line 537 (N-37) |
| **2** | **C2** | India application, subordinate to C1 on conflict. **Quantitatively empty and therefore never a source for a number.** Carries a propagated conceptual error at line 104 (N-01). **Its §4A Fujiwhara block is the one place where it outranks an official glossary, and is used as such** |
| **3** | **Verified official live sources** | **Outrank the books** on anything quantitative, institutional, nominal, criterial or post-2000 — the IMD criteria sheets, the 25 January 2026 Special Message, PIB 2131392 and 2054427, and the NWS Beaufort scale |
| **4** | **Page-rendered and text-layer local books** | Majid *IWG* Chapter 3; Leong Chapters 13-14; Khullar printed 124-127 and 132-135; Majid *Geography of India* printed 4.8. **Book-era quantities are attributed and dated** |
| **5** | **Coverage package** | **Ordering map only. Never evidence** |
| **—** | **Qdrant** | **Unavailable three times. Non-blocking. Excluded from the manifest** |

**Conflict rule, as applied.** Where a book and an official source disagreed, **the official source
won** and the book's version was shown as a dated, attributed contrast — N-08, N-09, N-11, N-12,
N-27, N-28, N-33. Where an owner and an official source disagreed, **the official source won** —
N-25, N-27, N-38, N-39. Where **two books disagreed and no official source settled it, both were
quoted with their exact page locations and the conflict was taught as the finding** — N-02, N-29,
N-31, N-32, and the jet-speed and jet-definition divergence at N-19. Where **a book was physically
self-contradictory, the contradiction was shown with both page locations and neither side asserted**
— N-14, N-19, N-23 and N-34. And where **an owner contradicted its own named book and the owner was
right, this edition said so and named the page** — N-06, the tornado — **because a student who reads
Leong after reading this edition must not conclude the edition was careless**.
